You cannot leave Boston is the second volume in a series of commentaries on the letters His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada wrote to Satsvarupa Das Goswami. It covers only one year, 1969, and chronicles the development of the Boston temple and the establishment of ISKCON Press under Srila Prabhupada’s guidance.

# “You Cannot Leave Boston”

## Preface

The commentaries in this book are an elaboration on Śrīla Prabhupāda’s letters to me. In *Caitanya-caritāmṛta,* Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja describes his process in writing about the life and activities of Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu: “Svarūpa Dāmodara wrote short codes, whereas Raghunātha dāsa Gosvāmī wrote elaborate descriptions. I shall now describe Śrī Caitanya Mahārāja’s activities more elaborately, as if fluffing out compressed cotton.”

I elaborate in two basic ways. I fill in the background of Prabhupāda’s life and activities that surrounded the particular letters. This is an elaboration on Śrīla Prabhupāda the person. I also elaborate on the teachings in his letters. I think if Prabhupāda knew his letters were being printed with comments, he would expect that I present the philosophical substance of his teachings. Therefore, this book is not only a memoir, but a discussion of Prabhupāda’s *vāṇī.*

## 1: January 1969

Los Angeles  
18th January, 1969  
My Dear Satsvarupa,  
Please accept my blessings. I am in due receipt of your letter dated January 11, 1969 and have carefully noted the contents. From this letter I can understand that texts number 6 and 7 are missing in the manuscript of the third canto which you have in Boston. The original manuscript is in New York, and when I go there, maybe in April, I will find this for you. In the meantime, you may skip four pages for these missing texts and continue numbering after skipping four numbers which will be filled in later. Convey my blessings to the three girls who are newly staying at the Boston temple, namely Joy Fulcher, Arlene Warmbrand and Rita. I hope you are all well.  
Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

I received this letter at our little Boston storefront on Glenville Avenue. By this time, we had rented an apartment across the street from the temple, and my wife and I lived there with the three girls Prabhupāda mentions in this letter.

Getting a letter from Prabhupāda was exciting for everyone in the temple. In this letter, as in many other letters Prabhupāda wrote to me when I was in Boston, he asks me to “Convey my blessings to the three girls.” I didn’t have to convey his blessings because as soon as the girls read my letter, they felt Prabhupāda had conveyed them himself. There was no question of them not reading the letter themselves. I couldn’t say, “Yes, I got a letter from Prabhupāda, but it’s the temple president’s business and none of yours.” It was all the other devotees could do to restrain themselves from opening the letter before I got home from work.

Arlene was a typist. Prabhupāda sent her to Boston from another center to help me type his dictation tapes. Although I was the only devotee typing Prabhupāda’s dictation at that time, Arlene (later Arundhatī dāsī) eventually took it over as my other duties increased. Joy Fulcher (later Jāhnavā dāsī) was from Seattle and Prabhupāda sent her to Boston to help Jadurāṇī paint.

Pradyumna lived in Boston too—in the temple basement, studying Sanskrit under Prabhupāda’s direction and marking Prabhupāda’s manuscripts with proper diacritics. He also audited a Sanskrit course at Harvard to complete his Sanskrit studies.

To taste these letter exchanges with Prabhupāda, it’s useful to know a little background. ISKCON was very much a family in those days. Our relationships in the Boston temple were sweet, familiar, and innocent. These girls were respectful, pleasant, and cooperative. They felt wanted in Boston because Prabhupāda had sent them and we had welcomed them.

Someone might think that three devotees joining a temple was not much of an event, but it was a real blessing. I had been in Boston for almost two years, and although we had done a lot of preaching and gone out regularly on *harināma,* no one had joined. To suddenly have three new devotees in the temple boosted our *harināma* party and made it more exciting for everyone. Just to have three more people at *Bhāgavatam* class was wonderful.

Another point about those years is that devotees could choose their own services and live in whatever temples they liked, although eventually Prabhupāda said we should ask his permission before leaving one temple for another. Prabhupāda knew what service each devotee was doing, and he often claimed someone for one project or another. For example, I received a letter from Prabhupāda informing me that Madana-mohana dāsa, who had been living in New York, was planning to move to Boston. Madana-mohana had been indexing Prabhupāda’s *Bhagavad-gītā* and Prabhupāda didn’t want him to stop his service just because he had moved.

This letter is an example of an exchange between Prabhupāda as author and me as his typist. Just looking at this letter reminds me of all the pleasure I felt doing that service for him. I was able to learn so much about Kṛṣṇa consciousness because of that service—Prabhupāda was always speaking into my ears.

I don’t type with any accuracy anymore; I seem to have lost the ability. When I was typing for Prabhupāda, however, I was competent and accurate and conscientious. I didn’t have an electric typewriter, but a manual one. I pounded away on that in the early mornings before anyone else was up. I had no other time to type in the day because I had to attend the morning program, chant my rounds, and work at the welfare office.

I began typing for Prabhupāda even before I was initiated. When Prabhupāda went to San Francisco, a man named Neal began typing for him. In the first letter I received from Prabhupāda specifically addressed to me (January 1967), Prabhupāda told me that Neal had left and asked whether I could take responsibility for typing his dictation. I continued to type for him until the end of 1969, when Arundhatī finally took it over completely.

I cannot overemphasize how dear this service was to me, or how important. At this point in ISKCON’s history, Prabhupāda was not a world traveler. Up until the early months of 1969, we see Prabhupāda traveling all over America, writing and preaching. Still, I couldn’t travel with him because of my job and the fact that I was the president of that small Boston center. Therefore, to receive tapes from Prabhupāda in the mail, always hearing his voice, him rustling pages and pressing the dictaphone button—and all the exchanges between author and typist—satisfied my longing to be with him.

By his mercy, I felt the philosophy passing through my being, not just through my mind, but entering into my flesh, blood, and bones. His words flowed through me constantly as I typed. I can’t express how grateful I am that he gave me such a connection to him, and so much of his association as he traveled.

His words would stay with me throughout my day. For example, I sometimes dozed on the bus on the way to work. As I nodded off, I would hear phrases from his purports running through my brain. I was also able to use what I heard from Prabhupāda when giving lectures and writing my BTG articles. Prabhupāda appreciated that.

In this particular letter, Prabhupāda acknowledged my report that two texts were missing in the dictation. Even though it was an important message, I sent it in a letter to Prabhupāda. We almost never made long distance calls at that time, and neither was Śrīla Prabhupāda accessible by phone.

Prabhupāda mentioned that he would take the missing texts from the manuscript he had in storage in New York. He was referring to the original manuscript he had brought with him from India, or to one he had typed himself at the beginning of his preaching in America. (Prabhupāda personally typed up to the end of the Second Canto and was starting on the Third by the time he left for San Francisco in January of 1967.)

\* \* \*

Los Angeles  
31st January, 1969  
My Dear Satsvarupa,  
Please accept my blessings. I have received your letter dated January 28, 1969 regarding Jadurany’s sick health. She requires complete rest. All of her work should be suspended, and she should be given liquid foods, just like barley water mixed with milk. Purchase pearl barley from the market, and the recipe is 1 cup of barley and four cups of water to be boiled for at least 1/2 hour. That liquid preparation may be mixed with milk and sugar, and she may take. Jadurany must not exert herself in any way. She should take complete rest and chant Hare Krishna. When she next wants to begin work, she must take my permission. For the time being, all work must be suspended.  
Regarding your letter dated January 25, 1969, I have already sent to you tapes #2 and #3. As an experiment, I sent tape #3 by ordinary mail at the concession rate for tapes. Please let me know when this tape #3 has reached you. If it does not arrive too late then we can continue to exchange in the mail at this postage rate of five cents. You do not require to send finished tapes to Dinesh. Please send them directly to me.  
I think that work on the pictures shall remain suspended for the time being. Altogether I have received four pictures for the *Krishna* book, and two pictures for the temple, one of My Guru Maharaj and one of Gour Kishore das Babaji.  
Regarding the two missing verses of the third canto, I shall get them done conveniently. I hope that by this time Jadurany’s health must have improved. Please convey my blessings to the others.  
Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

By the time I received this letter, Prabhupāda had already started working on *Kṛṣṇa* book. His original premise was that he would start writing as soon as he had a “quick artist.” Prabhupāda wrote to Jadurāṇī on December 5, 1968:

As soon as possible, I will require some artist who will paint pictures from the *Bhagavatam* as I give hints on what to paint. But the artist must be very quick. Two or three pictures must be done every week. These pictures will be used for my new book *Krsna*, which I will begin as soon as I get the assistance of a quick painter. I am sure that you could do this but you are already engaged with so much work.

Jadurāṇī, of course, wanted to do these new paintings herself, and although Prabhupāda agreed to her proposal, he also sent Joy Fulcher from Seattle to help her.

Prabhupāda conceptualized the *Kṛṣṇa* book as a large illustrated book with at least one painting per chapter. Jadurāṇī was ready to start painting for it immediately. Prabhupāda then sent a second letter outlining five scenes from the first chapter and recommended an illustrated *Bhagavad-gītā* to the artists as reference. (Many of the images the ISKCON artists painted were later copied from that book—Kṛṣṇa driving Arjuna’s chariot and other scenes from the beginning of the Tenth Canto.)

We had little money, so Jadurāṇī painted on plywood and small pieces of sheet rock. As soon as she and Jāhnavā sent their first paintings to Prabhupāda for approval, the project took off.

I was the typist for *Kṛṣṇa* book. It was so wonderful for ISKCON that Prabhupāda began to write it. We didn’t understand why he had decided to write *Kṛṣṇa* book, but later he told us that he was afraid he might not live to finish translating the Tenth Canto, so he wanted to give us at least a summary of it. His mood was similar to Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja’s mood, who summarized the *Antya-līlā* at the beginning of the *Madhya-līlā* because he was afraid he would die before he finished writing the book. Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī did live to complete the *Caitanya-caritāmṛta,* but Prabhupāda didn’t finish the Tenth Canto. Now we reap the benefit of his wisdom in writing *Kṛṣṇa* book. If Prabhupāda hadn’t given us Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes, we wouldn’t have been able to hear them from anyone else. Indian visitors to the temple often tried to tell us stories about Kṛṣṇa, but we almost always rejected whatever they said as un-bona fide. We tended to reject everything that we didn’t directly hear from Prabhupāda.

By the time I received this letter, Jadurāṇī had become ill. I don’t remember all the symptoms of her illness, but I do remember that she was extremely weak. Still, she refused to slow down. She drove herself past the limits of a normal person, almost as if she didn’t believe she had a material body. We were so neophyte in those days, and devotees would often fanatically apply philosophical points such as “I’m not this body.” We considered it laziness or *māyā* to indulge the body in sickness. It’s as if we thought that giving any attention at all to the body would reveal our lack of faith and advancement in Kṛṣṇa consciousness, and we often tried to work twenty-four hours a day. If anyone tried to correct that mentality, they were seen as *“māyā* personified.”

In Jadurāṇī’s case, she got worse. When Prabhupāda heard about her health problems and saw that she was still painting, his instruction was unequivocal: she had to stop painting. Because Prabhupāda told her to stop painting and to chant, she started chanting a hundred rounds a day, and of course, she followed Prabhupāda’s recommended diet and only that.

Prabhupāda continued to dictate *Kṛṣṇa* book even though his artist was sidelined, but we can imagine how disappointing it might have been to him that his main artist couldn’t work. Of course, she wasn’t the only artist painting, but we thought of her as if she were. Prabhupāda, however, was more concerned with her health than with his book.

It is touching the way Prabhupāda was so much our father, so down-to-earth, that he could give her this rather homey remedy for her illness. We could say that his remedy is unsophisticated—he didn’t even diagnose the patient—but we certainly didn’t feel like that. She didn’t take his letter with her to a health care consultant. She simply accepted that barley water would cure her illness.

I don’t think we even knew how to prepare it at first. Then it occurred to us that it was meant to be a drink. We took Prabhupāda’s instructions so completely literally that this is all we administered to her. She was not allowed to eat any other food because Prabhupāda recommended only barley water. Later we inquired from Prabhupāda again whether this diet wasn’t too restrictive, and he told her she could also eat *dāl, capātīs,* fruit, and *sabjī.* Jadurāṇī was sick for so long that later, he suggested she go to Hawaii where the climate would be better for her and where she could eat mangos.

Prabhupāda expected us to balance service and *sādhana* and to maintain good health. He considered a devotee’s health of primary importance; the demand on him or her for service was secondary. It’s not that the spiritual master insists that the work be done without concern for the devotee’s well-being. Prabhupāda did not immediately replace Jadurāṇī with another artist. He gave her time to recover and to take up her service again. Prabhupāda wasn’t whimsical or sentimental, but it was very much a part of his teaching to his disciples that they lead regulated lives and eat proper diets. He taught us not to neglect our health early in our Kṛṣṇa consciousness because it was so much his own mood. Not only did we learn to maintain our own health, but we learned not to neglect other devotees who may be suffering from illness.

Prabhupāda maintained a balanced outlook toward health. He neither encouraged fanaticism nor malingering. Health was not a central issue in our lives; it didn’t take precedence over surrender and hard work. In this case, although Prabhupāda offered different solutions to her problem, when there was no sign of improvement even after going to Hawaii, he sent her back to Boston and told her to go out on *saṅkīrtana.* That was the other side. With the same authority that Prabhupāda used to force her to stop all work, he now told her to live a normal life again. Prabhupāda did want work done, and he was encouraged by devotees who obviously tried to follow a simple health regimen while they continued their services. That was how he taught us to behave: depend on Kṛṣṇa and take care of our health. He wanted us to always maintain the attitude of wanting to come to a higher physical state so that we could resume our services.

It is also interesting to note that Prabhupāda didn’t send Jadurāṇī to a doctor. He simply gave her this remedy. Under his care, we didn’t then run to a naturopath, then a homeopath when the naturopath failed, then a chiropractor when the homeopath failed. We never discussed medicine or different treatments. Everything was simple and straightforward. We made some attempt to regain health, and if that didn’t work, we tried to live normally again. What else could we do if we were so chronic?

We should also note that Prabhupāda didn’t judge a devotee who was ill or devalue him because he wasn’t able to make the same front-line contribution he expected of others. Still, the movement was moving forward under Prabhupāda’s force, and if Jadurāṇī had not struggled back to resume painting again, Prabhupāda would have replaced her. Prabhupāda was able to care for one devotee who was recuperating in the back lines and still accomplish his aims through another, front-line soldier.

When other devotees have tried to apply this principle, it has sometimes led to the sick devotee feeling abused. Often temple leaders favor productive devotees, especially those who can sell books and collect money, and then when they become ill, they don’t care for them anymore. As a movement, we have to learn to continue moving in a forward direction while caring for our injured soldiers, as Prabhupāda did.

This letter also stresses Prabhupāda’s authority over each of his disciples. I was both the temple president and Jadurāṇī’s husband, but she would not obey my instruction to take care of herself. When Prabhupāda ordered her, however, it was as if Kṛṣṇa had spoken. There was no question of her not following his order. I remember that there were at least two such expressions in this letter that forced Jadurāṇī into submission, and she immediately changed her habits, stopped painting, began chanting a hundred rounds a day, and drank only barley water. That is the power of the guru’s order, that we do what we don’t want to do.

Prabhupāda mentions Dīneśa in this letter. Dīneśa was Kṛṣṇa dāsī’s new husband. Prabhupāda had personally married Kṛṣṇa dāsī and Subala and sent them to New Mexico to open a temple. While Subala was away from home for some time, Kṛṣṇa dāsī developed a relationship with a young man coming to the temple. She then decided to leave her husband and marry this man, who was later initiated as Dīneśa. Prabhupāda was shocked. He said that he didn’t leave Vṛndāvana to put up with this and that he could go back to Vṛndāvana.

We all understood immediately that Kṛṣṇa dāsī was in *māyā.* Subala went to live with Prabhupāda as his servant. Prabhupāda was so compassionate that he wrote to Kṛṣṇa dāsī, “Please come to me.” She wrote back that she wanted to be accepted with her new husband. Prabhupāda did accept her back and her new husband also became an initiated devotee. Dīneśa knew how to make records. He made the “Vande ’ham” record, one of the first records in ISKCON.

I don’t remember the details, but it seems that Dīneśa wrote to me independently and asked me to send him Prabhupāda’s dictation tapes when I had finished typing them. It’s likely that he told me Prabhupāda said he should get the tapes. I always checked with Prabhupāda first before complying with devotees’ requests for tapes and manuscripts. In this case, Prabhupāda told me not to send the tapes to Dīneśa.

## 2: February 1969

Los Angeles  
12th February, 1969  
My Dear Satsvarupa,  
Please accept my blessings. I am in due receipt of your letter dated February 7, 1969, and I have noted the contents. Regarding Jadurany’s diet, let her take fruits, vegetables, milk, chapaties, and also when she is hungry she may take a little rice and some thin mung dahl. Please keep me informed of her progress.  
Regarding your idea of having an installation ceremony for the Deities, it is very good suggestion, and you may also have the sacrificial fire. The Deities should be bathed with milk, and while bathing, They must have covering of thin cotton cloth, and the whole Body and the clothing should be wet by pouring the milk. You can see the picture of this in the new *Back To Godhead,* and perhaps you saw the ceremony when it was held in New York. The Deities should be decorated with sufficient flowers and nice gorgeous dresses and ornaments. Beforehand, you should polish the Bodies with a mixture of tamorine pulp and fullers earth. Apply the pulp on the Bodies, then rub it and then polish nicely with tissue paper. This will make a very good shine. Then bath the Deities with milk, and dress very nicely with ornaments, and place Them on the throne with flowers, candles, etc. The throne, if possible, should be coated with silver sheets, and the canopy should be red velvet with gold embroidered work. Before the Deities, on the staircase, there may be some silver polished cups, pitchers, etc. I think the Deities have already helmets and peacock feathers and hair to be dressed with. If not, make arrangements for this also. I do not know how big is the throne, but if it is very big, then within the throne there may be a raised seat to accommodate the Deities. On the whole, everything should be very gorgeous; then it will be successful.  
Actually, although Radha-Krishna is worshipped in Vrindaban which is like a simple village, but we worship Laxmi-Narayan, and the worship is accepted by the Radha-Krishna Deity. Actually, in our present status, we can not worship Radha-Krishna. But as all the Vishnu Murtis are situated in Krishna, therefore, our Radha-Krishna worship is transferred to Vishnu, Lord Narayan. Vishnu worship is the regulative devotional principles, and Radha-Krishna worship is spontaneous service of eternal feelings. Therefore, as Laxmi-Narayan is the Deity of great opulences, similarly our Radha-Krishna Murtis also should be worshipped with great pomp and dignity. Krishna will give you the proper intelligence to do this very nicely. So far as prayers, you may sing Hare Krishna, Govinda Jai Jai, and Govindam Adi Purusham.  
It is not mentioned how many hands the different Brahmas from the different universe have. Maybe four hands. Yes, they ride on swans.  
I am sending herewith the tape, so you do the work on your dictaphone and send me regularly the typed copies. Don’t stop them it makes me stop also. Unless I get back the typed copies it doesn’t encourage me. So you send me the typed copies and I shall send you the tapes regularly. What about the 3rd Canto? I know that I have made up to the 3rd Canto 7th Chapter and the present tape is continuation, but I am in darkness where are the papers. So give me an account where are the papers.  
I am concerned about Jadurany’s health condition—has she got any fever? If she has feverish condition, she should simply lie down and do no work.  
Hope you are well, and please keep me informed of your temple affairs, as well as about Jadurany’s health condition.  
Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

In this letter, Prabhupāda adjusted Jadurāṇī’s restricted diet. Thinking back to this incident, I realize that although the mood of obedience in a disciple is good, obedience can be better applied when the disciple inquires further. We can take an instruction and follow it absolutely; that is first-class. If the guru elaborates on the specific details of his instruction, however, then doubts can be quelled and often, important information will be conveyed. Sometimes we realize that while we are applying the letter of the law, we have missed the guru’s point in giving the instruction. Therefore, it is a disciple’s duty not to simply hear an instruction and carry it out, but to ask for further details.

Prabhupāda was always intimately involved in his disciples’ lives, which is evidenced by his asking me in this letter to keep him informed about Jadurāṇī’s health. By his fatherly presence, he encouraged both reverence toward him and friendly intimacy, almost domesticity. Prabhupāda was the father of our little devotional family; no one questioned his authority. That is standard for guru and disciple.

What was unique to our relationship with Prabhupāda, however, was that the concept of disciple was new to us. In our own family lives, many of us had called our fathers by their first names or rebelled against them. We had never learned respect, but we respected and loved Prabhupāda. Just by his nature, Prabhupāda did not allow familiarity—we rarely even touched his feet and almost never joked with him—but our affection was unanimous. There was no question of distance between us. We were almost all young, independent Americans. We weren’t looking for institutional life, but we were drawn to Prabhupāda out of love. If someone didn’t have affection for Prabhupāda, he or she couldn’t join the movement.

Prabhupāda followed our lives closely. It was still early in the movement—there weren’t many disciples—and we filled our letters to Prabhupāda with the details of our lives and preaching.

In 1969, I wrote Prabhupāda on the average of twice a week. I would tell him what was going on at the temple, ask philosophical questions, report on my typing services, and give him any other pertinent information. We were always eager to hear from him. We knew we didn’t know much about Kṛṣṇa consciousness, so it was natural for us to look to him to respond to our needs and to make decisions both in spiritual life and in our material dealings.

Immature devotees often go about their service until their faith is tested by a crisis. Often, they give up their service. Prabhupāda protected us from bewilderment by always clarifying our confusion and providing solutions to our problems. In that sense, we were like his children.

We became bewildered, for example, when the Maharishi craze came to Boston. We weren’t bewildered by their philosophy, but we couldn’t understand how such a non-devotional movement could so quickly become prominent when we were the genuine representatives of Lord Caitanya in the West.

Another time the police chased us off the street while we were selling magazines. We were happy to do *saṅkīrtana* and Prabhupāda was pleased with us. How, then, could the police defeat us? Weren’t we Kṛṣṇa’s *saṅkīrtana* warriors? We turned to Prabhupāda and he told us what to do.

An even more extreme example of our dependence and immaturity came early in my marriage. I wrote to Prabhupāda and asked him when we were supposed to have sex. Prabhupāda answered that I shouldn’t have asked him that question because he was a *sannyāsī.* “A *sannyasi* is not supposed to be asked about anything sexual. But still, because you are so much dependent on my instruction, so I must give you information as far as possible.” (Letter, September 20, 1968) It was true, we were completely dependent on him.

Prabhupāda had asked a lot from us. He had asked us to sacrifice our youth—to forget our career dreams and our attachment to material success—and to give everything to Kṛṣṇa. In our minds, of course, that meant giving everything to him. Then he had to help us. We didn’t know anything and we were so naive. He always made us feel that he was grateful for our service. Out of that gratitude and out of the sincere desire to spread Kṛṣṇa consciousness, Prabhupāda took the burden of training his disciples himself. He hoped we would learn and then train others. In this way, Prabhupāda was the temple president of all five or six ISKCON temples. He was the authority to whom we all could turn. It was he who kept all the temple presidents in check.

Prabhupāda’s accessibility also had something to do with the size of the movement. Much of our service was directly under his supervision. I was his typist, Jadurāṇī his painter, and Pradyumna his Sanskrit student. He looked at each of our offerings and corrected them or praised us for them. Our services were an intrinsic connection linking us to his direct association.

Unfortunately, we didn’t appreciate just how much Prabhupāda gave us of himself until he left us. The night Śrīla Prabhupāda disappeared, one devotee remarked that we wouldn’t know how much we appreciated Prabhupāda until he was taken away. We took him for granted right up until the end.

One example of our simple dependence on Prabhupāda is addressed in this letter when Prabhupāda talks about the Deity installation. The Deities being referred to here are the *vijaya-vigraha* still presiding in the Boston temple. Jaya Govinda dāsa, who was living in Vṛndāvana at the time, had sent us unsolicited a set of small brass Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa Deities, similar to the ones commonly sold in Loi Bazaar. They arrived in Boston just after Prabhupāda installed Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa at 26 Second Avenue.

When the Deities arrived, I started worshiping Them immediately. I was already getting up early to type Prabhupāda’s dictation, but I rose even earlier to accommodate the Deity worship. I don’t remember why I suddenly decided that these Deities should be installed, but I wrote to Prabhupāda and asked his permission.

Devotees sometimes wonder why I didn’t ask Prabhupāda to do the installation himself, but it just seemed too much to expect Prabhupāda to travel to Boston at that time just to install these small Deities. Of course, as I later came to understand the level of devotion required to install Deities, I realize my decision was naive. When Rādhā-Gopīvallabha arrived in Boston in 1971, we did ask Prabhupāda to install Them.

Still, there is something sweet about this memory. I often present Prabhupāda’s letter as an example of his disciples’ simplicity in those days. So many rituals have since been introduced into the installation procedures, but Prabhupāda’s formula was not ritualistic at all. In this letter he lists the essentials: red velvet (that didn’t come from India, but from the Deity installation in Los Angeles), silver pitchers and cups—“On the whole, everything should be very gorgeous and then it will be successful.” To Prabhupāda, Deity worship meant devotees chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa and spending as much as they could afford on gorgeous arrangements.

Prabhupāda also mentioned that we could sing the “Govindam” prayers. These prayers were introduced into mainstream ISKCON through the “Hare Kṛṣṇa” album in the summer of 1969. We didn’t know the entire *Brahma-saṁhitā,* and we certainly didn’t sing “Govindam” in the morning to greet the Deities then as we do now. Still, we were familiar with these few verses and we sometimes sang them in *kīrtana.*

In this letter, Prabhupāda explains that we are actually worshiping Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa as Lakṣmī-Nārāyaṇa and that our worship would be accepted by the Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa Deities. Because the Viṣṇu *mūrtis* are within Kṛṣṇa, our worship is transferred to Viṣṇu.

This is not the only place Prabhupāda makes this statement, and often devotees question the meaning of it. Does he mean that we cannot worship Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa, and that all our temples are really worshiping Lakṣmī-Nārāyaṇa? My understanding of this statement is that because Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa worship is performed in the mood of spontaneous devotion, and because we have not achieved that platform of Kṛṣṇa consciousness, our worship must be performed in the mood of awe and reverence and thus offered to Lakṣmī-Nārāyaṇa. We cannot worship Kṛṣṇa in a tree and offer only some dry *capātīs* as Sanātana Gosvāmī did. We are not qualified.

In order to actually worship Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa in the Vṛndāvana mood, we have to be aware of our *siddha-svarūpa.* Only then can we execute our worship from our eternal position. That is the meaning of *rāgānugā* worship.

However, the question remains: if we worship Lakṣmī-Nārāyaṇa, does that mean we will go to Vaikuṇṭha instead of Goloka Vṛndāvana? No. Although Prabhupāda says that Lakṣmī-Nārāyaṇa accept the worship, it can be said that Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa also accept our worship. We are worshiping Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa in a regulative way as training to worship Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa in the spontaneous mood.

If this were not true, Prabhupāda’s statement would naturally affect our whole philosophy. If Prabhupāda wanted us to worship Lord Viṣṇu, then naturally we would become Viṣṇu *bhaktas*. Prabhupāda never told us that we were aspiring for Vaikuṇṭha. The only reference he makes to worshiping Lakṣmī-Nārāyaṇa is in his discussions about Deity worship. Therefore, we can understand that the heart of Prabhupāda’s statement is that because we are unqualified to worship Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa in the spontaneous, Vraja mood, we should offer our service to Lakṣmī-Nārāyaṇa to train ourselves for something higher. As further proof, Prabhupāda did not ask us to chant Viṣṇu mantras. He told us, even in this letter, to chant Hare Kṛṣṇa, prayers from the *Brahma-saṁhitā,* and “Govinda *jaya jaya.*” All of these are prayers to Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa in Vṛndāvana.

When we perform formal *arcana* to Lakṣmī-Nārāyaṇa, then our *bhakti* is expressed in regulative activities: cleanliness, punctuality, attentiveness, and gorgeous arrangements for the Deity. That is how the regulative devotees show their devotion, and these things are in our power to perform.

Also, if Prabhupāda had wanted us to worship Lakṣmī-Nārāyaṇa, he would have installed Lakṣmī-Nārāyaṇa Deities.

His phrase, “The worship is accepted by the Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa Deity,” further points to the fact that Prabhupāda is discussing the mood of the worship, not the ultimate destination. Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa worship is done in the “spontaneous mood of eternal feelings.” We are not qualified, and that was especially true in 1969 when we were just learning how to practice *vaidhi-bhakti.*

Of course, we had all read descriptions of *vaidhi-bhakti* and *rāgānuga-bhakti* in Prabhupāda’s books. We understood early in our devotional lives that we were aspiring to worship Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa in Vṛndāvana. We also understood that in the beginning, we would have to practice the rules and regulations under Prabhupāda’s guidance. Gradually, we knew we would develop spontaneous attraction and the quality of our devotion would change. We aspired to go to Vṛndāvana and to play with Kṛṣṇa.

Prabhupāda encouraged our aspirations. One time Śaradīya asked Prabhupāda whether we could become *gopīs.* Prabhupāda replied, “Yes, and Kṛṣṇa will kiss you.” The devotees were thrilled. Yet while enlivening our aspirations, he kept us grounded in the practical reality of our disqualification and told us to remain fixed in practicing the rules and regulations.

When I asked Prabhupāda how we follow a particular Vṛndāvana resident’s mood in worshiping Kṛṣṇa, Prabhupāda told me not to “try for that now. It will come automatically.” He didn’t tell me that this practice was not in our line or that it wasn’t to be our aspiration. Rather, he encouraged our aspirations even though we were completely neophyte.

When I received this letter, I remember the devotees gathering together to discuss the philosophical points it contained. Our understanding was simple. We had no other information with which to contradict or qualify Prabhupāda’s statements. That was true not just of this topic but of any topic. Therefore, we did not worry about the implications of Prabhupāda’s instructions.

Our “philosophical discussion,” therefore, was not based on questions about *vaidhi-bhakti* or *rāgānugā-bhakti,* but on how Prabhupāda clarified our ignorance. I think in some ways we were fortunate that we didn’t have much else to consider. The questions about the different stages of *bhakti* didn’t trouble us then. I would like to recapture some of that simple acceptance of the spiritual master as absolute and of everything outside his instruction as irrelevant. This is a particular kind of attentiveness to the guru’s order. It may appear sectarian and may even embarrass us when we’re exposed to other Vaiṣṇavas, but the real heart of a spiritual movement is the members’ faith in the spiritual master. There is nothing more important than that.

ISKCON continued in this simple way until gradually more and more devotees began to go to India. Sometimes new ideas entered the movement from Prabhupāda’s Godbrothers who had spoken to some of Prabhupāda’s disciples. Often those Godbrothers didn’t appreciate Prabhupāda. Sometimes they criticized Prabhupāda because he had been a householder. They accused him of not knowing how to practice Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Prabhupāda saw these “ideas” as a contamination in his society; he treated them as a threat to the movement.\*1

We can appreciate the mood that whatever Prabhupāda says, we should do. We don’t have to become mental about it and weigh all the implications according to other ideas.

That was how we were in 1969 in approaching this Deity installation. Life was simple: I received the Deities and asked Prabhupāda how to install Them. Prabhupāda wrote back and directly instructed us on what mood we should have, how we should perform the installation, what the essence of our worship should be, and what songs we should sing. Then I and the others accepted what he said with complete faith and tried our best to follow it. Thus the installation became an internal affair between guru and disciple.

Prabhupāda continued this simple mood toward Deity worship even in ISKCON’s later years. For example, when Prabhupāda installed Kṛṣṇa-Balarāma in Vṛndāvana in 1975, he commented that he had hired the *brāhmaṇas* only to satisfy the local people. The real installation procedure was the chanting of the *mahā-mantra.*

When people accuse Prabhupāda of not introducing necessary aspects of Vaiṣṇava culture because he didn’t know what they were, I think of the actual example that he set. He was streamlined because he was elevated. He could exclude what was not appropriate according to time and place because he was an *ācārya*. He didn’t even *try* to institute all the Vaiṣṇava practices. He served on the spontaneous level and centered everything on preaching. This is not his defect, but his glory. He didn’t weigh ISKCON down with rituals because he knew that Kṛṣṇa’s acceptance of our service was not based on material considerations. If we were sincere and chanted Hare Kṛṣṇa, Kṛṣṇa would accept us.

Another aspect of my own exchanges with Prabhupāda was the ongoing typing and editing work I did on his manuscripts. In this letter he writes, “Don’t stop them, it makes me stop also. Unless I get back the typed copies it doesn’t encourage me.”

Service between guru and disciple is a personal, two-way exchange. This is especially true when we worked on Prabhupāda’s books. He always wanted to know what was going on and he responded so humanly. When he saw progress, even if a book was still only at the typing stage, he felt encouraged to continue writing.

I remember taking that statement to heart when I read this letter. I examined myself to see where I had made a mistake. Then I decided to finish the work as quickly as possible and send it back to him because I didn’t want him to become discouraged. I shared his enthusiasm to get his books printed and distributed, and I now saw my exchanges with him as important to his writing. After all, this writing was his life’s work. It was important that we show our interest.

We cannot know the mind of the spiritual master, but if he tells us we are not working in a timely way and because of that he doesn’t see why he should write the next chapter, it’s almost as if he is asking for some loving reciprocation. The spiritual master is not a machine. Prabhupāda did not churn out his writing without our reciprocation. He occasionally commented on that point throughout the years.

Of course, a disciple feels close to his spiritual master when he makes such statements. It makes us realize how intimate our service is and how important it is to the greater mission. “Don’t stop or you will make me stop.” If we don’t continue our service, the spiritual master may not be encouraged to carry on. We are his limbs. If we don’t function, he can’t function. Prabhupāda allowed us to think of ourselves in this way. As he said to me in 1966, “If you love me, I will love you.” The service exchange is not automatic; the disciple must reciprocate.

Los Angeles  
20th February, 1969  
My Dear Satsvarupa,  
Please accept my blessings. I beg to acknowledge receipt of your letter—undated—I’m very glad to learn about the installation ceremony. I’m also glad to learn that Boston center is improving and people are taking interest more and more. More important feature is that by Krishna’s grace you are given the facility of lecturing in various Universities.  
Next Spring by the first week for certain I shall be in New York and from New York I shall be glad to visit Boston, say for 15 days and you can give me a tentative program of lecturing in different Universities at that time. It is very engladdening to hear that you have now secured an eight weeks seminar in yoga at Emerson College. I’m also glad to learn about Jadurany’s health and by Krishna’s grace she’s improving. I do not give my permission just immediately for regular work but she can come to the temple in the morning and chant her beads silently. So far envy is concerned it can be used only upon the non-devotees. In the transcendental world a devotee is never envious of another devotee on account of his excellence but on the contrary if a devotee finds some excellence in other devotees he eulogizes the devotee admitting his own subordinate position. Although in the spiritual world there is no such concept of subordination still devotees on account of being very humble and meek think that way.  
In the material world the same thing is expressed in a perverted form. But in the Spiritual world to accept one’s inferior position does not mean envious mentality upon the other. Unhappiness experienced by devotee on account of feeling himself inferior is not unusual rather such mentality is impetus to further development of devotional service.  
I’m in due receipt also of the Krishna manuscript for the tape no. 3 & 4 and I’ve also received back the tapes concerned.  
You can keep the curtains of the deity room open during Kirtan.  
Lord Chaitanya’s advent day is on the 4th of March 1969. On that day you should keep fasting up to the Moon rise in the evening, and the whole day may be utilized in performances in Kirtan and reading of Lord Chaitanya’s teachings. In the evening after ceremony of Kirtan is observed, light refreshments like fruit and milk, boiled potatoes may be taken and the next day general feasting and distribution of Prasadam to public may be observed.  
I hope this will meet you in very good health.  
Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

Prabhupāda mentions a proposed visit to Boston in this letter. It wasn’t easy to follow Prabhupāda’s itinerary even in those days. We relied on the ISKCON grapevine, which was already quite active by 1969, for information on his whereabouts, but the information we received wasn’t always accurate. Devotees often said what they thought should happen rather than what was going to happen.

That fact becomes obvious to devotees who read Prabhupāda’s letters and see how much time he spent dispelling rumors and misinformation. I remember one time I heard that Prabhupāda had removed me from the editorial board of *Back to Godhead.* I wrote to Prabhupāda, who assured me it wasn’t true. Another time I heard I wasn’t supposed to write Prabhupāda letters anymore. Prabhupāda corrected that also. In one letter, Prabhupāda finally told me, “Unless you hear firsthand from me, don’t believe these stories.”

In this case, I had heard through the grapevine that Prabhupāda intended to spend the spring on the East Coast. I knew he would go to New York and that he would also come to Boston, but then the devotees in Buffalo asked him to come to their center too. Later, New Vrindaban also asked. Unless I heard directly from Prabhupāda about his plans, I had no way of knowing for sure what they were. I needed to know in advance so I could book college engagements for him.

I seem to remember that this year we were one of the first centers to invite Prabhupāda. He told me he would go to New York first, but they didn’t even know yet that he was coming. If we arranged a month’s worth of engagements for Prabhupāda, however, it would mean that he wouldn’t be able to visit any of the other centers and the devotees would get upset. Of course, they would go to Prabhupāda and ask him to change his plans. Later, Prabhupāda told me he would go to Buffalo after Boston, but he actually went there first, so he arrived in Boston a day later than we expected him. I had scheduled him to speak at an engagement the day after his proposed arrival, but because he was a day late, I had to stay back from the airport and give the lecture myself.

It always took a lot of arranging and shuffling to finally settle his itinerary. Although Puruṣottama was traveling with Prabhupāda as his secretary, we arranged everything directly with Prabhupāda.

At the end of the letter, Prabhupāda explains to us how to celebrate Gaura-Pūrṇimā. We had never celebrated Gaura-Pūrṇimā with Prabhupāda. (In 1967, Prabhupāda had been on the West Coast for that festival.)

I still remember that 1967 Gaura-Pūrṇimā. Prabhupāda had instructed us to wait until the full moon had risen before breaking our fast. We didn’t have a newspaper by which to check the time, and we couldn’t see the moon from the storefront. Rūpānuga decided to run up to a tenement roof while we waited, the feast ready. I can still remember Rūpānuga running down the stairs yelling, “The moon is up! I saw the moon! *Prasādam*!”

In 1968, Prabhupāda was again on the West Coast for Gaura-Pūrṇimā. Therefore, we planned to follow the program we had followed in New York under Prabhupāda’s direction—reading about Lord Caitanya throughout the day and fasting until moonrise.

In this letter, Prabhupāda also refers to the improvement of the Boston center and states his criterion—that “people are taking an interest more and more.”

I don’t remember exactly what I said to Prabhupāda to receive this response, but I do remember that after the installation ceremony, the temple seemed to burgeon with new life. No doubt I had reported that more people were coming to the Sunday feast and that in general, things were going well.

It is difficult to look at Prabhupāda’s letters to his disciples without seeing the other side of the correspondence because Prabhupāda always responded to what we wrote him. Sometimes he may have directly seen the situations about which we were reporting. Prabhupāda had last visited the Boston temple in May of 1968. Now almost a year later, we had installed Deities and were working to increase the preaching. But we couldn’t bluff him. Prabhupāda knew us and our individual capacities. Because we were eager to please him, we often told him about anything that looked hopeful or was still in the thinking stage. His replies made such things look more substantial than they were. In a sense, he called our bluff. Then we would gulp and tell him in the week it took to receive his reply that whatever idea we had presented had already been canceled.

An example of that appears in this letter. Prabhupāda refers to an eight-week seminar on yoga we were to teach at Emerson College. It sounded impressive. In those days, universities had what they called “free universities” where colleges would allow outside groups to come in. All kinds of strange courses were taught, and a student’s whole curriculum could be built around courses taught by these outside organizations. It wasn’t difficult to get into a college and draw students, but only a few students attended our course. As soon as they realized we weren’t going to sit in leotards and do *āsanas,* most of them dropped out. We canceled the course long before the eight weeks were over.

Still, Boston was filled with university students and other young people and Prabhupāda considered university preaching the future of ISKCON Boston. His lectures and letters are like legal documents and are nondifferent from *śāstra.* In this letter, he seems to list his responses from least important to most important: he is happy to hear that we have successfully installed the Deities, he is glad to hear that more people are becoming interested in our center, but most important, we should preach at the universities.

Prabhupāda considered the students good candidates for devotional service, but we found that university preaching required patience. Students go to college either to please their parents or to prepare for a career. They tend to spend their time either enjoying or studying. In either case, it is difficult to convince them to renounce material life and to take to Kṛṣṇa consciousness. There were students who were looking for more in life, but it took determination and patience to find them. Girirāja Swami was one such example. Because Prabhupāda had spent a month lecturing at Boston’s many universities in 1968, he was aware of the potential.

Other centers were also preaching at the universities. The Buffalo center had an established program at the university there. We tried to follow their example, but never managed to establish such a program in Boston.

A point from this letter that devotees often question is Prabhupāda’s mention of silent chanting. Of course, Jadurāṇī was ill and he had still not given her permission to start painting again. Now, however, he gave her permission to attend the temple program and to “chant her beads silently.”

Up until this time, Jadurāṇī had been too sick to come to the temple. On Prabhupāda’s order, she did nothing but stay home, drink barley water, and chant. Now she was showing a small indication that her health was improving. She immediately told me to ask Prabhupāda whether she could do her service again. We both realized that she was under Prabhupāda’s personal care and that neither of us could make that decision. Prabhupāda remained conservative.

Chanting silently was not a new technique Prabhupāda was introducing, however. Rather, he implied that she should not lecture or participate in *kīrtana. Japa* is chanted quietly. By “silently,” he meant “quietly.” When someone is chronically ill, he or she can chant quietly to conserve energy.

I asked the question about envy on Jadurāṇī’s behalf. Again, we were helpless children turning to Prabhupāda. We turned to him to pay Pradyumna’s hospital bill, and we turned to him in our struggle to free ourselves from our *anarthas.*

When Prabhupāda answered such questions, we accepted his responses as *śāstra.* Actually, we considered them better than *śāstra* because they taught us how to apply the Absolute Truth to our own lives. His letters contained a mixture of encouragement and personal attention, and of doses of practical philosophy and instruction on how to cope with our *anarthas* (and each other). We certainly had our share of difficult or envious dealings with each other. Envy is insidious. It infiltrates all our relationships. It turns us into offenders.

We all begin devotional service full of hope. We hear about the goal of Kṛṣṇa consciousness and think it’s almost within our grasp. As soon as we begin serious practice, however, we are forced to face our *anarthas.*

Prabhupāda gave us a philosophical solution. He defined envy as thinking ill of someone in whom we see excellence. Prabhupāda didn’t analyze it beyond that, but instead pointed us toward ideal behavior. His letters usually emphasize that we should understand ideal behavior rather than how-to techniques.

This is Prabhupāda as teacher. Sometimes devotees wonder whether it isn’t more difficult to settle issues now that Prabhupāda has disappeared, because so few devotees seem to have the same faith in authority. Faith in authority is extremely important to our ability to improve in spiritual life. At least we should still accept Prabhupāda’s authority. We can turn to Prabhupāda’s letters and apply the instructions we find in our lives even now. Why should we think that Prabhupāda’s instructions would be so different now than they were in 1969?

We have to approach the spiritual master to have our disease diagnosed. Then we should recite his instructions. In this case, Prabhupāda says to praise other devotees’ good qualities, maintain our integrity, and understand that everyone is serving Kṛṣṇa.

Such instructions don’t immediately wipe out all traces of envy from our hearts or our institution, but Prabhupāda intended to give us hope. The intelligent can learn simply by hearing. Those who are less intelligent learn by experience. Those who are unintelligent never learn.

Prabhupāda knew that if we were sincere, we would come to understand how to apply Kṛṣṇa consciousness ourselves. If we wanted to become devotees, we would have to give up envy. Therefore, he didn’t give us academic answers to our questions. We already knew that envy was not something we wanted. We also knew that we should appreciate other devotees. By Prabhupāda defining it for us, however, we gained the strength to try and push the *anartha* from us.

In this case, Prabhupāda not only gave the ideal behavior, but he explained what to do with the feelings of inferiority that result from another devotee’s success: he told us to use those feelings as an impetus for further development.

I remember appreciating the phrase, “Unhappiness experienced by a devotee on account of feeling himself inferior is not unusual.” Such feelings are normal and not pathological symptoms of a diseased condition in spiritual life.

Often we hear devotees talking about self-esteem and how to overcome feelings of inferiority. Prabhupāda gives us another angle. He writes that feeling inferior is normal when we meet more qualified devotees. There is nothing particularly wrong with the feeling. Rather, it can be used to express humility. He certainly doesn’t recommend that we overcome our feelings of inferiority by asserting ourselves over others.

We all feel inferior from time to time, and most of us will occasionally feel envious too, but we shouldn’t let those feelings paralyze us. Many devotees consider low self-esteem bad for their health. We are supposed to like ourselves. I agree. A devotee of Kṛṣṇa should have self-respect *because* he’s a devotee of Kṛṣṇa. At the same time, he should be as humble as a blade of grass.

How can we simultaneously maintain self-respect while practicing humility? Ironically, it’s only possible if we are humble. Humility is not the same as low self-esteem. Low self-esteem is paralyzing; it depresses the mind. Humility makes us eager to offer service. Envy kills the service attitude, and humility cultivates it. If we want to develop further in spiritual life, we have to serve our superiors and all devotees, and that means we have to practice real humility and give up envy.

Prabhupāda was adept at psychology. He understood that the psychological dimension is part of human existence. When he talks about the sense of inferiority not being unusual, his psychology is transcendental. It would be nice if those who practice psychology could draw more from Prabhupāda’s teaching methods.

## 3: March 1969

Hawaii  
8th March, 1969  
My Dear Satsvarupa,  
Please accept my blessings, and offer the same to my beloved daughter, Srimati Jadurany. I am in due receipt of your letter dated Feb. 2, 1969, as well as of Feb. 5, 1969. I plan to go to New York by the first week of April, so you can arrange your scheduled meetings on the 24th by which time I shall be ready to go to Boston.  
This climate of Hawaii is most healthy, with sunshine and fresh sea breeze, so I think if it is possible for Jadurany to come to this climate she may improve her health nicely. Consult together on this proposal, and you can correspond with Goursundar and Govinda dasi here in this regard.  
Regarding your question: I do not remember if Rayarama ever chanted very loudly in my room, neither he ever lived in my room, nor I had ever asked him not to chant loudly. I do not know how these news are spread. There is no such requirement that Japa should be silently and chanting should be done differently. Loudly or silently, everything is all right. There is no such restriction. Only thing is that we should chant very attentively, hearing the vibration very distinctly.  
I have received the typed copy of tape number 6, and I am sending tomorrow tape number 7.\*  
I hope you are well and that Jadurany’s health is somewhat better; she must rest nicely. Please convey my blessings and best wishes to all the devotees there.  
Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami  
\*enclosed herewith

Here is more discussion on *japa.* I wrote to Prabhupāda that Rāyarāma had told me that he was once in Prabhupāda’s room chanting loudly and he had told him that *japa* should always be chanted silently. I wanted to know if this were true.

I used to look up to Rāyarāma. Therefore, I was willing to chant silently if that’s what we were supposed to do, but I wanted to hear that instruction directly from Prabhupāda. By questioning Prabhupāda, I was not challenging Rāyarāma, but attempting to verify his instruction. In this letter Prabhupāda confirmed that “loudly or silently, everything is all right.”

There are some Vaiṣṇavas who chant in the mind. Prabhupāda didn’t discount them, but sometimes those Vaiṣṇavas discount us. This letter is important evidence for us when we are accused of not knowing how to chant *japa.* There are some people who insist it should *only* be chanted silently, but from this letter we can see that Prabhupāda approved of both chanting aloud and quietly, and that he didn’t consider it an important question.

Prabhupāda’s main emphasis on chanting was that there are no hard and fast rules. The main point is to be attentive and to distinctly hear the sound of the holy name. In order to hear the holy name distinctly, the devotees would have to vibrate some sound. In his purport to *Ādi* [17.32](https://vedarama.com/en/books/cc-adi/17.32), Prabhupāda writes that chanting is simple, but it has to be taken seriously. He mentions how the lips should move and we should hear our own sound vibration. Prabhupāda used the word “silent” to indicate “soft.”

He did say, however, that loud or soft, our chanting should not disturb the spiritual master. Prabhupāda became annoyed on occasion with his servants, secretaries, and other devotees for chanting too loudly when he was trying to work. When we chant, we have to learn to be simultaneously attentive and considerate toward others.

In this letter, Prabhupāda invites Jadurāṇī to Hawaii to recover her health. ISKCON had such a nice communal spirit in those days that no one considered it a burden to take care of a sick devotee. Gaurasundara and Govinda dāsī were welcoming her, and because Jadurani and Govinda dāsī were good friends, her going to Hawaii would give them an opportunity to spend time together.

An interesting point to note is that even though Prabhupāda had married us, he still maintained control both over our marriage and over us as individuals. Jadurāṇī was Prabhupāda’s daughter. If he wanted her to come to him in Hawaii, there was no question of my saying, “Thank you for your advice, but I want my wife to stay here.” Prabhupāda could step in at any moment and tell us what to do.

Prabhupāda was not just a figurehead in the background, but the director of our activities. This is a special feature of Prabhupāda’s preaching. No *sannyāsī* in India would become involved in arranging disciples’ marriages, and no *sannyāsī* would, after the woman was married, continue to claim her service. Prabhupāda saw everything he did, including the relationships he had with both husband and wife, as service to Kṛṣṇa. There was no question of impropriety.

There is a funny story connected with Jadurāṇī’s illness that illustrates something interesting about Prabhupāda. When Jadurāṇī first became ill, she was living in the Boston temple as a *brahmacāriṇī.* Prabhupāda asked me to care for her because I was the temple president. I heard later from Govinda dāsī, who was Prabhupāda’s secretary at the time, that Prabhupāda asked Govinda dāsī about Jadurāṇī’s illness. Govinda dāsī told Prabhupāda that Jadurāṇī had said her illness was caused by a birth defect.

Prabhupāda immediately became upset. He didn’t understand the words “birth defect” and thought it meant she had become pregnant. Govinda dāsī quickly explained what “birth defect” meant, but it remained a kind of joke between her and Prabhupāda.

It was those words, “birth defect,” that started Prabhupāda thinking Jadurāṇī should get married. Often Śrīla Prabhupāda acted in ways that went beyond reason. He would receive some wrong information, become upset by it, then even after it was corrected, wonder how to prevent it from happening. In this case, he decided to get Jadurāṇī married.

Hawaii  
19th March, 1969  
My Dear Satsvarupa,  
Please accept my blessings. I am replying your letter of March 14, 1969. My present program stands as follows: I am starting on the 31st March to S.F. and then on the 7th of (approx.) April I am starting for New York. And from N.Y. around about the 23rd of April I am going to Boston. So your fixed program on the 24th, and the 29th of April, and 3rd and 4th of May may be fixed up immediately.  
So you can arrange for the meeting at the entertainment club The Ark, and make it as big as possible, as you say that the place can hold up to 1800 people and they saw me in San Francisco, and are eager to see me and have a talk there. On the 6th of May, I think I shall have to be present in Buffalo. And around about the 10th of May, I shall have to be present in New Vrindaban or Columbus, Ohio.  
Regarding Jadurany: I think she can come here alone and stay with Govinda dasi for around two months. The present place will be changed probably by the middle of May, and things will have to all get set up accordingly. So you can arrange things directly in correspondence with Govinda dasi, and she will be happy to receive her when all is ready. So far as yourself, you have to remain separate from your wife for maybe two months, because you cannot leave Boston.  
Regarding the tapes, you have sent me tape no. 6, so the no. 7 is there, and no. 8 is also there. I am glad to note your arguments with the Yoga meeting people. Actually this so-called yoga system and meditation that is so much popular in your country is bogus. But if we speak the naked truth to the people, sometimes they may get angry. Because unpalatable truth is not tolerated. If we call a black man black, he will be angry because it is unpalatable. So we have to present our case very carefully. The best way of presentation will be like this: (1) Yoga system is recommended in the Bhagavad Gita, (2) It is approved system, (3) But it is not suitable for ordinary man, especially in this age of Kali, (4) The so-called yoga system practiced by the people of this age is not bonafide. They cannot follow all the rules and regulations of yoga practice, (5) Therefore it is conclusive that so called yoga followers are simply cheated and they are wasting their time. I have already explained these points in the Samkya yoga chapter of the Bhagavad Gita As It Is, so you read them carefully and present it, point by point, in suitable occasions.  
I have already replied Jadurany’s letter. Regarding her offenses, I do not remember when she committed offenses, and even though she might have done so, I excuse her 100 times, without any hesitation. So she has nothing to bother about it. I have given her instruction not to work in her last letter, and we shall discuss it further when I shall arrive there. When she shall come to this place Hawaii she will have ample opportunity for Krishna Kirtan on the beach, and the fresh ocean air and warm nourishing sunshine will be good for her.  
I hope this meets you in good health, and please continue caring for Jadurany nicely and we shall see the position when I shall be there very soon.  
Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami  
N.B. For Dr. ̮̮̮̮̮̮̮ letter you may ask immediately Purushotam at L.A. The letter is ̮̮̮̮̮̮̮̮̮ ̮̮̮̮̮̮̮ ̮̮̮̮̮̮̮̮̮. ACB

Now Prabhupāda has given me his itinerary, although he ended up arriving a day late because he went to Buffalo before he came to Boston. Because I expected Prabhupāda on the 24th, I scheduled an engagement at Boston College. When I realized that he would not be able to lecture, I decided not to inform the sponsors of the program, but went and gave the lecture myself. The sponsors didn’t seem disappointed—they had no idea what they were missing. None of the other devotees attended, however, because they all went to greet Prabhupāda at the airport.

I remember that it was raining when I returned to the temple. No one was there. Everyone was at Prabhupāda’s apartment, which was on the ground floor of a nearby building. I ran over to the apartment and saw Prabhupāda sitting behind a low desk surrounded by devotees and visitors. He immediately asked me why I had not been at the airport. I explained that I had just given the lecture he had been scheduled to give. Then he asked what verse I had chosen to speak on. I told him that I spoke on the verse where Kṛṣṇa states that no one is equal to or greater than Him. Prabhupāda recited the Sanskrit and was pleased to hear that I had chosen to speak on that verse. He even said that I should take over the lecturing and he would retire and write his books. It was such a nice feeling to walk in late and be welcomed by Prabhupāda.

That Prabhupāda was a day late to Boston was unusual. It was unusual for him to go anywhere late. I still remember the anxiety I felt for the people who were planning to attend his program at the college, but there was nothing I could do for them. They missed Prabhupāda and I had to accept the anxiety. When we serve the spiritual master, we don’t always have control over how our arrangements will turn out. We can only try our best and go on.

An important point related to this is that when we perform service, we cannot become attached to the spiritual master utilizing everything we offer. In this case, Prabhupāda was late and couldn’t give the lecture. That didn’t invalidate my service offering. Whatever devotion I had in performing the service did not go in vain.

If we cook a meal for our spiritual master but he doesn’t eat it, that service is not wasted. If we advertise a program that he wasn’t able to attend, what is the harm? We have to learn to concentrate on the offering and not on the outcome of the service. Only devotion is reality in spiritual life.

As the movement expanded, devotees had to learn this lesson. It just wasn’t possible for Prabhupāda to attend every program that devotees arranged or to fulfill all their expectations in his travel and preaching. He was normally considerate toward our offerings, but it is also true that sometimes our offerings were ignored or neglected.

This apparent rejection of our service can whittle down our false egos. Prabhupāda had more to do in this world than to accept *our* particular service. So many servants were competing for his attention. The *Bhagavad-gītā* states that there is no loss or diminution in devotional life. The essence is the devotion.

Even when Prabhupāda couldn’t attend a program or accept an offering, he tried as far as possible to acknowledge it. We should do the same among ourselves. We should be careful never to neglect or disrespect another’s offering of service. Prabhupāda acknowledged a bookmark a young girl made him or a flower a child gave him. He even acknowledged or accepted awkward gifts, such as the Western-style, wrong-sized shirt devotees gave him in San Francisco.

Sometimes devotees offered Prabhupāda things that he could not accept. Still, he acknowledged their service and accepted the relationship behind it. That was Prabhupāda’s personalism.

One of the six principles of loving exchange is to give and to receive. We should give graciously and receive graciously and express thanks. Śrīla Prabhupāda was always gracious.

We can appreciate the importance of receiving more when we become givers. That is true of loving exchanges and it is also true of apologies. If we write a letter of apology to someone and open our hearts to them, but they don’t acknowledge it, we can become hurt. To not acknowledge an apology (or even a letter) is an obvious neglect. People need reciprocation. An entire relationship can remain frozen for years until that reciprocation is there. Therefore, when someone approaches us and we see we need to apologize, we should apologize. If someone asks forgiveness of us, we should not remain cold to their expression or consider their feelings inconsequential. Both require humility.

It is especially true that we have to accept whatever dealings the spiritual master has with us as absolute. The disciple should approach the spiritual master in humility and love, not to contend with him on philosophical or other points. The spiritual master expects a disciple to ride the inevitable waves of exchange. It cannot be that if the guru fails to make eye contact with the disciple during a meeting that the relationship becomes strained. Rather, the disciple has to be confident in his relationship with the spiritual master, because without that confidence, the disciple unduly worries about the spiritual master’s affection.

Disciples should become mature enough not to read neglect in their spiritual master’s exchanges, but to trust in his affection. We cannot expect our spiritual master to keep up on every minute of our feelings. If we feel trust and intimacy toward the guru, then naturally we will feel his affection in return. We will know that he cares for us even if he doesn’t always speak to us. If the disciple is too sensitive, the spiritual master cannot fulfill his mission through him because he has to spend too much time on the externals of reciprocation.

Prabhupāda was a military leader and the devotees were his soldiers. If he told them to go and preach, then that order should have been sufficient to create their enthusiasm. If instead he had to ask them first about their families and apologize because he didn’t acknowledge their presence in his *Bhāgavatam* class, he would be handicapped in his attempt to fulfill his mission.

In 1969, Prabhupāda knew all his disciples by name. Some years later, he had thousands of disciples and often didn’t recognize them all. Does that mean that Prabhupāda didn’t accept each devotee’s service? We simply had to trust him.

All through our years with Prabhupāda, we were competitive for his attention. That was equally true in 1969 when all the devotees wanted him to stay in their temples. We knew that the way to attract Prabhupāda was to provide him with many opportunities to preach. This was how we served Prabhupāda in those days, until he got older: we invited him to our centers and arranged speaking engagements for him. We also ensured that *prasādam* was distributed and that his books were made available for people to buy. Prabhupāda wanted to show people that Kṛṣṇa consciousness was not “dry philosophy.”

Prabhupāda preferred engagements where he could speak to the same people over a period of time. He referred to one-time engagements as “casual” lectures, and he didn’t consider them valuable. He wrote one letter to me that he would be willing to make special plans to stay in Boston if the students would pay for him to come and be willing to hear from him repeatedly: “So far my going to Boston, unless the students agree to hear patiently for some time, Bhagavad-gita, Teachings of Lord Chaitanya, the casual lectures will not be very much beneficial. So they are not very much interested, otherwise they would have come to our classes. But I told you previously that I am prepared to go provided they pay the expenses. But I think that is also not possible. So why should you take the risk? Of getting me there and with great difficulty you shall maintain my expenses, I think it is not very much favorable, so we shall see later on what is to be done; for the time being let it be dropped.”

Prabhupāda had also felt that way about his own casual lecturing in New York when he lived on the Bowery. He would lecture at the Tagore Society and then at a university and then at a church, but because there was no place people could go to hear from him again, his preaching did not create followers. It wasn’t until he opened the storefront at 26 Second Avenue that people began to hear seriously.

Of course, casual lecturing was traditional in academia and Prabhupāda agreed to do such engagements whenever he visited Boston. These lectures did have some effect because people came to the temple after and some became devotees.

Although it was a good way to introduce people to Kṛṣṇa consciousness, there were difficulties. Some people were not receptive to too much philosophy. Others wondered why they should accept Prabhupāda’s authority. Others felt strange to find themselves at a Hare Kṛṣṇa meeting. Still others could not understand Prabhupāda’s accent. Therefore, Prabhupāda always liked to supplement his lectures with *prasādam* and books. In a quieter atmosphere, he hoped they might actually read the philosophy and seriously consider it.

Our mood in Boston was to push Prabhupāda forward when he would attend an engagement. “Here he is!” It was an expression of our faith as disciples. We knew that if the people in Boston could just *hear* Śrīla Prabhupāda speak, they would become purified and the whole city would be blessed.

And of course, Prabhupāda encouraged us: “I am ready to lecture every day. As many lectures as you can get, I will attend.”

Śrīla Prabhupāda mentions The Ark. The Ark was a psychedelic nightclub, named after Noah’s Ark. It was housed in a warehouse and it had bizarre paintings on the outside walls. The owner of The Ark had attended the mantra rock dance in San Francisco and he wanted to repeat the experience in Boston. He even wanted to invite Allen Ginsberg to attend.

I called Allen Ginsberg from the window seat in the Boston storefront. He was living in Cherry Hill, New Jersey at the time. He sounded favorable, but as he inquired further and realized that his itinerary conflicted with Prabhupāda’s, he told me he would have to get back to me.

I remember that I suddenly decided to tell him how much I liked his writing. I may have even mentioned a recent book he had published. He told me that he was busy writing now, but that he didn’t have people to type his writing like Prabhupāda did. Later, I thought, “If Allen Ginsberg wants people to type his work, he should join the Hare Kṛṣṇa movement. After all, Kṛṣṇa makes all arrangements for His devotees.”

I don’t remember what happened after that except that everything fell through from their side. Prabhupāda was willing to go, but as the date fluctuated, Allen Ginsberg backed out, and finally the owner withdrew his offer.

Here again we had presented something quite tentative to Śrīla Prabhupāda, to which he responded immediately,  
“ . . . arrange for the meeting at the entertainment club The Ark.” Now the incident has been immortalized in Prabhupāda’s letter collection and we can all read about his mention of The Ark.

My favorite line in this letter is “you cannot leave Boston.” I loved it when Prabhupāda assumed control over my life. I found it so relishable. Both guru and disciple have to be sensitive to each other, but even more so, the disciple has to be sensitive to the guru’s mastership over his life and must reciprocate with that mood. Prabhupāda didn’t have to apologize that I couldn’t leave Boston. I was his man in Boston. I had to stay there to fulfill my responsibility.

It was on Prabhupāda’s order that I had gone to Boston in the first place. Now I was obliged to the *saṅkīrtana* movement, just as he was. I couldn’t go to Hawaii when Prabhupāda was there—I couldn’t even go to Montreal. I had to stay in Boston. It was as if Prabhupāda had made me a co-worker in his movement, although I was his disciple. He was fulfilling his responsibility to his spiritual master and I had to fulfill my responsibility to him. Even on the plea of wanting to be with him, I couldn’t leave the duties that he had assigned to me. He didn’t give me that freedom.

Therefore, I liked to think I was on his mind. I imagined him listing off his centers and naming the devotees responsible for running them: so-and-so in San Francisco, so-and-so in Los Angeles, so-and-so in Hawaii, so-and-so in New York, so-and-so in Columbus, and Satsvarūpa in Boston. I also imagined he might say to someone, “Why don’t you do something like what those others are doing? Just like Satsvarūpa. He’s in Boston and he’s married, so-and-so is here or there and he’s married.” Although these duties took away my freedom, they gave me the freedom to surrender to Prabhupāda.

Part of my service to Prabhupāda was to maintain my job. I never thought of myself as working at a *karmī* job, and I certainly wasn’t attached to it in a material way. My job was service. Prabhupāda wanted me to work. That job was what tied me down more than anything else. I couldn’t go anywhere except on weekends, and when Prabhupāda went to India and was calling different disciples to accompany him, I couldn’t go.

Sometimes I feel twinges of regret when I remember how much of Prabhupāda’s association I missed, but I have also learned to remember and taste the kind of freedom he did give me in that order, “you cannot leave Boston.”

Once during a lecture in the Glenville Avenue storefront, Prabhupāda gave the analogy of a building on fire to describe our attachment to our bodies. He said that if this building suddenly caught fire, we would all vacate immediately and our attachment to the building would be broken. Only the proprietor would remain attached, but even he would not stay in a burning building.

I identified myself as the proprietor of the Boston temple on Prabhupāda’s behalf. Boston was *my* place. I wasn’t materially attached to it, but I accepted the weight of responsibility to keep the center open and running smoothly. Prabhupāda encouraged us to think that the center was ours, not his, and that it was our responsibility to take care of it. A real disciple will be grateful for the burden.

Another big part of my service in Boston was, of course, preaching. The TM people were quite a challenge to us, and in this letter, Prabhupāda mentions that he is happy I was arguing with these “yoga people.”

What had happened was that the leading American disciple of Maharishi Mahesh Yogi, Jerry Jarvis, had come to Boston. Devānanda attended a meeting and then challenged him after his lecture. Later, I too challenged him. The meeting I attended was held in a large hall at Harvard University. Jerry Jarvis gave a slick presentation of impersonal philosophy and he quoted the *Bhagavad-gītā* to establish his points.
