When he asked for questions, I raised my hand and said, “You seem to accept the *Bhagavad-gītā,* but your presentation of Kṛṣṇa is improper.” I then went on to explain the proper understanding of Kṛṣṇa as the Supreme Personality of Godhead. When Jarvis attempted to answer, I challenged that too. The audience booed me for creating “bad vibrations” at the meeting. Jarvis took the floor back and after awhile, I left. I do remember a member of the audience approaching me later and telling me that he had appreciated what I had said.

When I told Prabhupāda about this exchange, he congratulated me and then gave me a five-point formula on how to answer anyone who thinks they are practicing yoga. I really loved receiving that from him. I memorized it and thereafter always used his five points. This is an example of how our correspondence with Prabhupāda brought the philosophy to life for us.

Yoga was quite popular in the ’60s and ’70s. Prabhupāda was often questioned about it. His *Bhāgavatam* purports written at this time also reflect the kind of questions he was commonly asked. Prabhupāda even wrote *Kṛṣṇa Consciousness, The Topmost Yoga System* to prove that yoga was bona fide according to the *Vedas,* and also to show what practicing yoga actually entailed.

Prabhupāda was certainly in touch with modern society and how people were reacting to Kṛṣṇa conscious instruction. In this letter he writes, “If we speak the naked truth to people, sometimes they may get angry. Because unpalatable truth is not tolerated. If we call a black man black he will be angry because it is unpalatable.” Therefore, he presented me with this five-point program so that I could provide a reasonable explanation and avoid angering people. The underlying truth of his position didn’t change, however. Yoga as it is practiced in the New Age movement is cheating and a waste of time. He didn’t bother to differentiate between the various types of yogas or the purposes for which they were practiced, although he told us that if people were practicing yoga for health, they were not practicing yoga but were exercising. Yoga only referred to spiritual practice.

When the devotees took to practicing *haṭha-yoga* as a health measure, Prabhupāda didn’t say much, but he definitely discouraged us from practicing *haṭha-yoga* as a form of meditation. He was firm on this point. Neither did he allow us to study any books on yoga that were not Kṛṣṇa conscious.

At the end of his letter, Prabhupāda mentions Jadurāṇī. For some reason I no longer remember, she thought she had committed an offense to Prabhupāda and she asked me to write to him for forgiveness. Prabhupāda was magnanimous: “I do not remember when she committed offenses, and even though she might have done so, I excuse her 100 times, without any hesitation. So she has nothing to bother about it.”

Prabhupāda was always magnanimous—whatever offense we committed in the execution of our duty, he automatically forgave. We treasure such statements. When Lord Brahmā prays to Kṛṣṇa after stealing the cows and cowherd boys, he says that a mother tolerates the kicking of the baby in the womb and does not become offended. Similarly, although Prabhupāda’s disciples made so many mistakes out of ignorance, Prabhupāda tolerated them all and simply blessed us.

It’s not that Prabhupāda was unaware of our mistakes. In one lecture, he addresses this point. He says that because his disciples don’t know how to pronounce Sanskrit, they call Prabhupāda *goru* (cow) instead of guru. “Sometimes you say *‘goru.’ ‘Goru*’ means cow, and guru means spiritual master. So the difference of meaning is vast. The spiritual master is not a cow. Or a bull,” he laughed, “ . . . it is not your language. But that doesn’t matter.” (Lecture, June 10, 1969, New Vrindaban)

I remember that the devotees were surprised to hear Prabhupāda say this—they had no idea that they had been mispronouncing the word. Prabhupāda even sounded shy when he told them about it. There must have been so many small mistakes committed out of ignorance that even today, we cannot imagine them all.

Like a father, Prabhupāda again asks me to continue to care for Jadurāṇī. Prabhupāda continually reinforced in my mind that taking care of my wife was service to him. Even before we were married, he expected me to care “for this poor girl.” I considered it a duty, even though she didn’t want to be restricted by me. It was almost as if he considered it my fault that her health wasn’t improving. If I was taking proper care of her, she wouldn’t be sick.

It is not *māyā* or weakness for a husband to care for his wife. The husband should understand that it is his duty to care for the spiritual master’s daughter. The *gṛhastha-āśrama* is not simply a list of “do nots.” Married couples should not indulge in illicit sex, they should not become attached to living in a big house, they should not unnecessarily pursue material prosperity, but the marriage vows include a list of dos. They should have a relationship and they should fulfill the promise to care for and serve each other. Besides the responsibility we share to care for a spouse, Kṛṣṇa is pleased whenever we maintain His devotees. Prabhupāda emphasized this point again and again in his letters to me.

Hawaii  
23rd March, 1969  
My Dear Satsvarupa,  
Please accept my blessings. I thank you very much for your letter. I am very much pleased to note the list of engagements you have submitted, and I can see you have been working very hard to secure so many opportunities for spreading K.C. Yes, within those days stated in your letter of March 20th you can add more engagements as you like. I have no objection. I have not yet received word from Rupanuga, in this connection, but you can plan on your program as it is set up. In N.Y. we have got engagement and they are paying $100 for a meeting, so you try to settle fees not less than $50 per lecture. So you can engage the whole duration of my stay there, and I shall deliver every day one lecture.  
Regarding Jadurany’s coming here, Govinda and Goursundar will consult with you, and when things are duly set up, she may come here for some time. They will write you in this connection.  
I hope you are well, and that Jadurany is resting nicely; I am hoping she will soon be recovered and be able to again feel in good health, so let us see.  
Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

Prabhupāda praised me at the beginning of this letter: “I am very much pleased to note the list of engagements that you have submitted and I can see that you have been working very hard.” So many things come to a disciple’s mind when the spiritual master praises him. It is also possible that the disciple will become proud and not accept the spiritual master’s words as absolute, but consider them flattery.

Sometimes devotees wonder whether we are better devotees now or whether we were more surrendered to Prabhupāda in those earlier days. When I try to analyze that question, I can only look at it with hindsight. How do I take Prabhupāda’s words now? Prabhupāda wrote so many letters. Can we say that because he wrote similar expressions to encourage his increasing disciples that his words lost their precision? Were they spoken as flattery? Are they hackneyed? To think so would be blasphemy.

If the spiritual master tells us he thinks we are working hard, then we have to accept his sincerity in expressing that. We should accept such statements as they are.

The disciple can think, “My spiritual master has been kind to say that I am working hard, even though it’s not really true.” Or, the disciple can decide to acquire the attribute the spiritual master is praising.

Once, Prabhupāda told me that he needed a good typist. I suggested Jayādvaita. Prabhupāda told me that Jayādvaita was a good typist and that he was also humble. When I relayed this to Jayādvaita, he said, “I am not humble, but now that I have heard this, I must try to become humble.” The disciple may accept the spiritual master’s praise in this way, not as flattery, but as an expression of what he wants to see in his disciple. Because we want our spiritual master to think of us as humble or as competent in some way, we will choose to become more humble or to work harder in order to fulfill his estimation of us. The guru is so liberal that he may acknowledge our little bit of hard work as bigger than it is. Therefore, we can take his praise as an impetus to improve.

Why does the spiritual master praise a disciple? He wants to encourage him. If the disciple accepts the guru’s words simply, then he will become encouraged and naturally increase his service. There is no reason to be foolish or to refuse to accept the spiritual master’s sweet and encouraging words just because we may not be the only one he speaks them to. This is part of giving and receiving between Vaiṣṇavas. We should receive the spiritual master’s encouragement openheartedly, and we should distribute that encouragement to others.

In this case, I had been trying to get engagements for Prabhupāda. I always felt confident that I was making the student groups and professors a good offer. Prabhupāda was the *ācārya* of the Hare Kṛṣṇa movement. If they accepted him as a lecturer, it would give them an opportunity to taste something rare.

I didn’t find it difficult to ask people whether Prabhupāda could lecture to their group. Even when they refused, I didn’t lose my confidence. I would approach philosophy clubs and they would often send me to the India club. It did take some work to find the right club and then the right organizer of the club to book the engagements, but it wasn’t what you would call hard work. Prabhupāda was kind enough to call it that.

When Prabhupāda’s disciples gave lectures, we often had to go to extremes to find sponsors. We went to sociology classes and offered to lecture on the Vedic concept of the family or to science classes and offered to speak on some so-called related topic. We had to choose our topics to book the engagement. Otherwise, very few would sponsor a Hare Kṛṣṇa speaker. With Prabhupāda, however, we didn’t have to mask his preaching like that. We leaned on his qualities and scholarship and presented him as he was. Neither did we want to burden him with a list of topics from which he had to choose. We presented him in a straightforward manner.

Prabhupāda wrote a letter to Brahmānanda on the same day as he wrote to me that he wanted $100 per lecture. To me, however, he wrote something different: “In N.Y. we have got engagement and they are paying $100 for a meeting, so you try to settle fees not less than $50 per lecture.”

Someone may wonder why Prabhupāda stipulated a fee if Kṛṣṇa consciousness is to be distributed freely. ISKCON needed the money. $50 would almost pay for Prabhupāda’s transportation to the next temple or the rent for his apartment while he was visiting. However, it was customary to pay renowned speakers a fee. Was Prabhupāda so insignificant that he should not be offered a speaker’s fee? I wasn’t prepared to grovel, “Please let him come and speak to you. We don’t want anything other than that you favor us and allow him to speak.” I approached the various groups knowing Śrīla Prabhupāda’s value. I expected them to pay him to speak. Receiving a speaker’s fee implied respect.

By the time I received this letter I had already lined up many engagements. Not every engagement offered an honorarium, but I didn’t feel bad about that, even though I didn’t follow Prabhupāda’s policy in every case. The engagements were often good in other ways. Neither could I go back and renegotiate the terms under which the organization had agreed to sponsor him to speak.

In this letter he mentions that I can get even more engagements as I liked. I already knew Prabhupāda would appreciate this from his visit the previous year. In fact, he often wrote to centers praising Boston for the number of engagements we had arranged, and he encouraged other centers to do the same.

Another point about praise from the spiritual master: by the spiritual master expressing his pleasure at our service, we learn what the spiritual master likes. Then we can try to offer that service again.

Being a public lecturer was an aspect of Prabhupāda’s preaching, and during these years, it was one of his main forms of outreach. The devotees even considered hiring an organization to arrange lecture tours. Because such organizations take a percentage of the speaker’s fees, they ask for large honorariums. We even corresponded with a few companies, although we never actually hired any of them.

Prabhupāda’s lectures were always of a good quality, although he never prepared them. Usually he went to the engagement without having chosen a topic, although if a club or organization requested a topic, he would agree to it. Neither did he ever appear nervous or uncertain of what he would say. Up until minutes before he would leave for an engagement, Prabhupāda would be talking to devotees in his room about his centers and how to develop them or be alone in his room answering mail. When it was time for the lecture, he would simply get into the car and go.

Prabhupāda’s preparation was his total commitment to Kṛṣṇa consciousness. In that sense, he was more prepared than any speaker on any topic. He lived and breathed Kṛṣṇa consciousness twenty-four hours a day. There was no question of his speaking about anything else. In one lecture at Northeastern University in 1968, Prabhupāda began, “Thank you very much for allowing me to take this opportunity to glorify the Supreme Personality of Godhead.” That was always his topic—glorifying Kṛṣṇa. His speeches always appeared well thought out because he had such realization of his topic.

Prabhupāda’s main emphasis was to present Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Therefore, he was sometimes concerned about his audience and he was prepared to address their particular concerns. At other times, however, he was not. When reporters asked their hackneyed questions, Prabhupāda often springboarded off their questions to his own points of interest in the philosophy. Similarly, he would give lectures to particular groups, but remain the irrepressible Prabhupāda. He was independent.

Once, Prabhupāda was offered a thousand-dollar honorarium to speak at the Florida State University at Tallahassee. He spoke philosophically on the Supreme Lord and His expansions Baladeva, Saṅkarṣaṇa, Pradyumna, and Aniruddha. He didn’t address the students’ concerns at all. Another time, Prabhupāda spoke to lawyers in India, and his lecture was very much centered on the fact that they were lawyers. It all depended on his inspiration at the time. He was unpredictable.

The results of Prabhupāda’s lectures were disappointing in terms of people joining, however. Of course, devotees such as Girirāja Swami came from university lectures. When such persons joined, we realized that the entire value of college preaching had to be measured against such persons. In terms of mass interest, however, the lectures had disappointing results. His lectures didn’t move the students out of their sense gratification or complacency. Very few of the students were ready or fit to hear Prabhupāda with submission.

In arranging speaking engagements, we usually approached Indian or Asiatic societies. These groups often wanted him to speak on India. Therefore, we titled Prabhupāda’s lectures according to the interests of the group, but once Prabhupāda started speaking, he spoke as he liked.

Still, Prabhupāda sometimes liked to assess his audience before he spoke to them. When we were on our way to an engagement at the Unitarian Universalist Church in Braintree, Massachusetts, Prabhupāda asked me about their philosophy. I told him that they believed in everything. Either he suggested or I suggested that he speak on *sanātana-dharma* and how it applies to everyone.

And he was always grave. If someone wanted to hear him speak about something other than the Absolute Truth, he wouldn’t. One time in Jagannātha Purī, Prabhupāda was asked to come and inaugurate a book. The inauguration organizer was not interested in having Prabhupāda speak about Kṛṣṇa consciousness; he wanted Prabhupāda to draw a crowd. Prabhupāda refused to cooperate. He gave a full lecture on how since Lord Jagannātha was the Lord of the universe, the Western devotees should be allowed into the temple to see Him. When he was finished speaking, the author of the book approached him, handed him the book, and asked him to speak about its merits. Prabhupāda glanced down at the book, read the title aloud, and said, “The book is inaugurated.” Then he walked off stage.

There was a lot of competition between New York and Boston, and later Buffalo, to book engagements and to attract Prabhupāda to spend most of his time at our temples.

It was hard to part with Prabhupāda. There is a letter dated April 3rd, San Francisco to Brahmānanda. Brahmānanda wrote Prabhupāda that he was sad that now the engagements were coming up, Prabhupāda was only planning to spend eight days in New York. Prabhupāda said that Brahmānanda’s sadness made him sad too. Then he wrote, “When Rūpānuga wanted me in Buffalo, I did not directly confirm it, but I asked him to consult with you. So after his consultation, you have agreed to his proposal and should not be sad at all.”

Prabhupāda went on to say that he understood Brahmānanda’s sadness at Prabhupāda’s leaving New York and that he also wanted to be in New York because “I like New York more than all other cities. This is because my first attempt was begun there, so I have got some affection for your great city.” It was the best city; it’s where he went in the beginning to start his movement.

Then he wrote something memorable: “There is no cause for becoming sad. Our meeting and separation in the material world is like the flowing tide of the river. During the flowing tide of the river so many different floating articles meet together and with the flowing they again become separated by the movement of the waves. That is the way of material life. But our separation, although it resembles exactly in the material way, it is completely different. In the spiritual world, separation is more relishable than meeting. In other words, in spiritual life there is no separation. Separation is eternal and meeting is also eternal. Separation is simply another feature of meeting.”

Prabhupāda took the discussion up to a description of *vipralambha,* service in separation, to console his disciple. He then went on to assure Brahmānanda that he could come to Buffalo when Prabhupāda was there.

Prabhupāda was aware of our anxiety. He knew we all wanted to spend time with him, but he had to spread himself out over all the centers. I remember when Prabhupāda came to Boston in 1971, he stayed only a few days. I was at the airport seeing him off on his way to New York. Some of the New York leaders were there along with some of the international leaders. Karandhara, Rūpānuga, and Śyāmasundara were also there waiting in the lounge. It was a sweet farewell. We sat at his feet and then one by one, got up to dance.

Then Prabhupāda stood up to board the plane. My wife said to me, “You should go with Prabhupāda to New York. You should spend as much time as you can with him. You are able to do that now. You’re the temple president. You never know when you will be able to see him again. You should always try to get his association.”

I wanted to go with him, but I had mixed feelings. Her words made me feel almost as if I was doing something wrong, that I wasn’t taking something that I was later going to lose. Maybe I lacked the enthusiasm to want to be with my spiritual master. It disturbed my normal sense of thinking that his association would naturally come by itself and that my main service was to take care of his center in Boston.

As I remember it now, I don’t have a neat lesson to draw from it. I still have mixed feelings. Sometimes I feel the urgency that I should have been there for every possible second of his association because the opportunities were so limited. At other times I realize that association with Prabhupāda was most possible when I performed my service. Real association means to give our entire lives to Prabhupāda.

There is no easy solution to this dilemma. We should be eager to get on the plane and be with him as much as possible, and we should understand that service in separation is not less solid than meeting.

## 4: April 1969

Hawaii  
10th April, 1969  
My Dear Satsvarupa,  
Please accept my blessings. I beg to acknowledge receipt of your letter dated April 5, 1969, and I have noted the contents carefully. I shall reach Boston Iskcon on the 23rd of April, and you can make arrangements accordingly. I have read your transcriptions of the *Krishna* tapes, and you are doing it very nicely. I am very much pleased with your efforts.  
Regarding Jadurany’s question, hearing the vibration of Hare Krishna automatically reminds one of Krishna’s Pastimes. So both of them arise simultaneously in the mind when one is sincerely chanting. So you cannot make any distinction between listening to the sound and thinking of the Pastimes. But the process is to hear, and then Krishna’s Pastimes, Form, Qualities, etc. will automatically come to mind: That is very nice.  
I hope this will meet you in good health and cheerful mood. I will be looking forward to seeing you once again.  
Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

This is the last letter I received before Prabhupāda arrived in Boston on April 24 (although he had planned to arrive on the 23rd).

I remember that during his visit, an article came out in the Sunday *Boston Globe* covering the Hare Kṛṣṇa movement. The article had been photographed and written before his arrival. There were many photographs and it was for the most part favorable. Near the end of the article, there was an exchange between the reporter and a friend he had taken with him to the storefront. The friend remarked, “There but for the grace of God go I,” implying that it was a misfortune to join the Hare Kṛṣṇa movement. It was one of the only sour notes in the article.

I showed this unfavorable comment to Prabhupāda. I also pointed out that some of the pictures showed our congregation to be mostly hippies. I was worried that it didn’t present a good impression of who we were. We didn’t want people to identify us as hippies. Now I understand that we can’t expect news articles to be written with devotion. I’m sure our ISKCON Communications experts would have considered this article quite good. In those days, however, if we didn’t receive perfect and favorable reports, we would become angry.

I was angry when I showed the article to Prabhupāda, and we discussed it as we came back from the morning walk. All Prabhupāda said was that we were not hippies and he asked why the reporter had thought that. He agreed that it wasn’t good that they had portrayed us as a movement of hippies.

On that same walk, I told Prabhupāda more about our negotiations for a new building and how when the article had come out, the owner had decided he didn’t want hippies using his property. Prabhupāda became disturbed when I told him that. He said anyone could become a member of our congregation—we didn’t ban hippies from our classes and feasts—the reporter should have seen that the devotees were clean and followed regulative principles.

He then suggested that I write to the newspaper and countermand what they had said, and he offered to give me some points for the article. As we stood on the street at the end of the walk, I asked Prabhupāda when I should come back to get the points. He invited me in right then and I took his dictation.

I did write the author of the article. He eventually wrote back a letter saying that I shouldn’t have complained because he had written a favorable article.

Prabhupāda and I also discussed why I wanted to move the temple from the present location. I told him how our lease was expiring and that the landlord wanted to raise the rent. We were also paying rent on an apartment across the street from the storefront. There were many teenage hoodlums in the area and they regularly broke our windows. It was a bad neighborhood. Prabhupāda assured me that these were good reasons to move if we could find a better place.

Whenever Prabhupāda came to Boston, devotees from other centers would come to see him. I remember Haṁsadūta and his wife coming from Montreal, and Trivikrama from Buffalo. Although the population swelled when Prabhupāda was present, it became depleted as some of our Boston devotees would leave to follow him to the next center. I remember accompanying Prabhupāda to the airport at the end of his stay and counting the devotees who would be left in our temple. By then, Devānanda, a Boston devotee, had managed to become Prabhupāda’s traveling secretary. I told Prabhupāda that too many people were leaving Boston. I was in the mood of a temple president and manager. I think he may have even told Devānanda that he should stay in Boston and help out.

In this letter, Prabhupāda writes that he is pleased with my typing. Whenever I see these praises from Prabhupāda, I look at the other letters he wrote on the same day. My letter is just one of many written on April 10. Whenever I see those other letters, I feel humbled because I can see that I wasn’t the only person on his mind on April 10. It also makes me glad that I was part of his movement, that for a few minutes he turned his attention toward me.  
It would be unfair to myself and to Prabhupāda to be unhappy that he also turned his attention toward others one after another, or that his thoughts of me were relatively brief. I am simply grateful for whatever attention he gave me because he is such a great person. Therefore, when he refers to my typing of the *Kṛṣṇa* book and says, “I am very pleased with your efforts,” it feels satisfying. His words are proof that I was making a place for myself in a corner of his heart.

Prabhupāda recognized me for a particular service. We want such recognition from the spiritual master more than anything else. We’re not looking for words to string around our necks like garlands, but the awareness that someone as great as Prabhupāda remembers us and keeps us in his heart. Now and at the time of my death, Prabhupāda will remember me because I exchanged some service with him. I’m not the only one who exchanged with him, but he will keep me also. I treasure his statements.

Prabhupāda’s comments on chanting in this letter are interesting. Jadurāṇī had been chanting a hundred rounds a day because Prabhupāda had told her only to chant and rest, or at least, he told her that she couldn’t paint. It was her decision to chant a hundred rounds. I can almost remember writing this letter to Prabhupāda when I came home from work one day. Jadurāṇī told me that while she was chanting, she wondered whether she could meditate on Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes. I may even have ventured an opinion on it, but she wanted me to write to Prabhupāda and ask him. Therefore, Prabhupāda addresses it as Jadurāṇī’s question.

Prabhupāda has made other statements to the same effect to other devotees, including me and Śivānanda. His instruction to Śivānanda is almost identical to his instruction to Jadurāṇī that when we hear the chanting, then the pastimes will automatically come to our minds. In Śivānanda’s letter, Prabhupāda responds to the question, “Is it offensive to think of the Lord’s pastimes while chanting?”

Regarding your first question, is it offensive to think of Krishna’s Pastimes while chanting, I think you should know that it is not offensive, but rather it is required. One must try for the point when he simply hears Krishna and immediately all of Krishna, His Pastimes, His Form, His Quality, are in his thoughts. So to always be immersed in thoughts of Krishna this is our process. When we are full in Krishna then where there can be any chance for maya in us? So this is our duty to remember Krishna’s Pastimes. One who cannot remember Krishna, let him always hear Hare Krishna and then when he has perfected this art, then always he will remember Krishna, His Activities, His Qualities, etc.  
—Letter, December 4, 1968

Devotees in those days sometimes tried to bring such states of remembrance about in artificial ways. There was one devotee, for example, who developed a mental exercise of chanting his rounds and simultaneously reciting, in his mind, the details of Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes that he had memorized from *Kṛṣṇa* book.

Prabhupāda told us not to do such things, but to concentrate on the sound of the holy name. He encouraged us not to distinguish between the sound and the pastimes. “When you hear, then the pastimes will come.”

This is a simple instruction, and he doesn’t give more detail of *how* the pastimes will come. He simply assures us to remain faithful to the holy name and that meditating on Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes is part of *japa*. Real *bhajana means* thinking of Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes. How do we practice *bhajana*? Start with chanting and hearing. Meditation on Kṛṣṇa will naturally result.

Someone who really wants to think of Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes and who thinks he should be doing that now could study Prabhupāda’s words and think that he is telling us *not* to do so. That would be a misunderstanding. Rather, Prabhupāda is saying that it is required to think of Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes when we chant. When we ask *how* we can think of Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes, however, Prabhupāda gives the method.

Therefore, if we want to add meditation to our *japa,* we have to start by hearing the holy name with intense attention. Prabhupāda assures us that if we do this, the panorama of *kṛṣṇa-līlā* will appear in our minds. If we are trying to hear now and are still not seeing Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes, then we have to be patient. We cannot force the holy name to reveal Himself. “Both of them arise simultaneously in the mind when one is sincerely chanting.”

The great rewards of Kṛṣṇa consciousness may not yet be within our grasp. We may not be able to think of Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes, but at least we should be cheerful. Our future is “brilliant,” as Prabhupāda liked to say. Kṛṣṇa consciousness should make us happy. Prabhupāda writes, “I hope this will meet you in good health and cheerful mood.”

## 5: May 1969

Columbus, Ohio  
14th May, 1969  
My Dear Satsvarupa,  
Please accept my blessings. I beg to acknowledge receipt of your letter dated May 12, 1969, delivered by Arundhuti here in Columbus. This evening Arundhuti will be married to Pradyumna, and they appear to be a very nice couple. Regarding your questions about Samkirtan Party, I think you should try to always have Samkirtan going on. All other things are subsidiary. This chanting is our life and soul, so we must arrange our program now so that there will be as much chanting on the streets and at college engagements as possible. On May 12th, we had a very successful engagement at Ohio Sate University, and over one thousand boys and girls were chanting and dancing along with us. So this policy should be continued as far as possible.  
I hope this will meet you in good health.  
Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami  
P.S. What about the Omega watch? I have sent you one tape. Have you received any check for the meetings? You paid me two checks (75 & 25) but if there is any more please send. I want to purchase one compositor ($3000.00 Down payment $600.00) ACB

Arundhatī went to Columbus to marry Pradyumna because Pradyumna had recently moved from Boston to study Sanskrit at Ohio State University. At that time, Hayagrīva was running the Colombus temple and teaching English at the same university.

There is a history to Prabhupāda’s arranging Arundhatī’s marriage. When Prabhupāda had visited Boston, he noticed that all the young girls living at the temple were always sick. Their illness, he said, was caused by their not having sex. He said it was unnatural and unhealthy for young girls to practice celibacy. Of course, he wasn’t referring to illicit sex, but just to the normal life of marriage and childbearing. He didn’t make this remark publicly, but it was passed around until everyone heard that he had said it. Therefore, he decided that all the girls should immediately get married. These girls had given up material life to come to Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Like a father, he wanted to protect them and give them whatever they needed to be happy in life. In our typically obedient and innocent mood, we rounded up enough husbands for all of them and Prabhupāda performed a triple wedding. Jāhnavā, Rukmiṇī, and Śāradīya were married in the Boston fire sacrifice; Arundhatī went to Colombus to marry Pradyumna.

In those days, Prabhupāda often arranged marriages between his disciples. He was like our village grandmother. Often the girls expressed their desires to him and he would himself discuss it with the man. If the man agreed, he would arrange the marriage. Although the temple presidents also sometimes suggested arrangements, marriages were rarely arranged without Śrīla Prabhupāda’s direct involvement

Prabhupāda was fairly simple about his marriage arrangements. He didn’t interfere in budding relationships nor force people to marry if they were not attracted to one another. He thought that since both partners were devotees, they didn’t need to consult astrologers to draw up compatibility charts. Kṛṣṇa consciousness would become the basis of their compatibility. Weddings became a regular occurrence in ISKCON in those days. Hundreds of guests would attend the ceremonies because everyone knew that a Hare Kṛṣṇa wedding meant a feast.

Śāradīya was only seventeen when she was married. As soon as she heard that Prabhupāda wanted her to get married, she went to him and told him that she wanted to marry Vaikuṇṭhanātha. Prabhupāda assured her that he would arrange it. Prabhupāda then told Vaikuṇṭhanātha that Śāradīya wanted to marry him and he agreed to the proposal.

Similarly, Rukmiṇī expressed her desire to marry Bharadvāja. Bharadvāja was not from Boston at all, but from Montreal. He had just come down with the Montreal devotees to see Prabhupāda. He was an interesting person and we discovered later that he was also a good artist. He had a real “artist’s” personality. Before joining the movement, he had played harmonica in a blues band. I still remember his fancy vests and long hair and his innovative *kīrtana* singing.

The third couple was Nanda-kiśora and Jāhnavā. Nanda-kiśora was also from Canada, and he had come to marry Jāhnavā.

Nowadays, devotees would be aghast at how these marriages were arranged. The arrangements were made quickly and were based on the girls’ attractions. Of course, Prabhupāda considered that the men were devotees. He was optimistic. Some devotees even say that he didn’t know who he was dealing with. He didn’t realize that we were capable of breaking vows and of unfaithfulness, but whether he knew it or not, he remained hopeful that these marriages would become lifetime commitments.

We set up the fire *yajña* in one corner of our storefront temple room. Usually Prabhupāda lectured from the dais, our equivalent of a *vyāsāsana,* but for the wedding, he sat on a pillow in the couples’ midst. He taught the men how to apply *kuṅkuma* to the parts of their brides’ hair and conducted all the other details personally.

Because I was the temple president, I was asked to act as father and give the girls away. I still remember Rukmiṇī thanking me after the wedding for having taken care of her before her marriage.

It’s incredible that Prabhupāda could visit a center for a few weeks and within that time, arrange and perform three marriages. Because we have had such bad experiences in our movement with marriage, we prefer to think more carefully about compatibility in terms of Kṛṣṇa consciousness, lifestyle, desires, and personality. Still, there is a lesson to be learned from how Prabhupāda approached marriage. He expected both partners to remain committed to their vows and to make the marriage work. He didn’t think it mattered so much who the devotees were, as long as they kept Prabhupāda and Kṛṣṇa in the center of the relationship. He also expected devotees to put aside personal quarrels in order to maintain the integrity of their marriage relationship and to lead simple lives.

And they did live simple lives. The women didn’t demand much material facility, but they all lived as they had always lived, simply, in the temples. What would happen in the future when they had children didn’t seem clear to any of us at the time. We all trusted Kṛṣṇa to take care of us. Nowadays we might call that naive, but Prabhupāda expected basic surrender and obedience to the sacred marriage vows.

Unfortunately, this wedding meant that all three girls would eventually leave Boston. Arundhatī left too.

Prabhupāda mentions in this letter that he had done a program where over a thousand students chanted and danced. Hayagrīva had organized it and Allen Ginsberg had also attended. This was a high point in Prabhupāda’s preaching. In the 1990s, we hear of events where thirty thousand people chant and dance in stadiums in Russia. In 1969, however, a thousand students was the biggest Prabhupāda had seen.

We could say that Allen Ginsberg attracted the students to the program, but the students chanted so wildly that we can understand that whatever had attracted them, it was Prabhupāda who made it wonderful for them. Later, some of them wrote letters about what they had felt while they were chanting and some of those letters were printed in *Back to Godhead*. It gave us all a glimpse of how Kṛṣṇa consciousness could become popular. Therefore, all the devotees in ISKCON were excited by Prabhupāda’s report of this program. Prabhupāda was certainly enthused. “So this policy should be continued as far as possible.”

During his Columbus visit, Prabhupāda met several times with Allen Ginsberg. Allen asked Prabhupāda whether there should be a more universal mantra—he didn’t think the Hare Kṛṣṇa mantra was suitable for Americans, and he didn’t like the fact that the devotees wore Indian clothes. But Prabhupāda insisted that Kṛṣṇa was universal. He would not compromise on this point, although he was still liberal about the devotees wearing *dhotīs* and *sārīs.* In most cases, the devotees wanted to wear devotional clothing, however, and almost all the men shaved their heads. Some of the men with jobs even defied the dress code and wore shaved heads to work.

Then Prabhupāda addresses my question about *saṅkīrtana.* I don’t remember what I asked Prabhupāda, but obviously we had started holding *saṅkīrtana* in Boston. It’s likely that I asked him whether *saṅkīrtana* had priority over other services, and perhaps I asked how often we should go out.

*Saṅkīrtana*—chanting on the street, handing out BTGs, and collecting donations—began in some of the other centers before Boston took it up. Of course, Prabhupāda started *saṅkīrtana* himself by going out to Tompkins Square Park in 1966. We didn’t understand the importance of Prabhupāda’s chanting in the park either for ourselves or the preaching, and after he left New York, we didn’t continue it except occasionally.

As the years went on, however, we came to understand that Prabhupāda wanted us to go out regularly with a *harināma* party. Almost all of the devotees in the temple would go out with the party and we would chant sometimes for hours on the street. By 1969, almost all the centers were engaging in this new definition of *saṅkīrtana*. Reports started to come in, especially from places that were organized by Tamāla Krishna, that simply by asking for donations on the street, the temples were collecting $50–$70 a day. That was a lot of money for a small center. The Boston temple was run almost entirely on my paycheck, about $125 a week. If we did *saṅkīrtana,* we realized we could make that much in a day.

We accepted his statement as *śāstra* that *saṅkīrtana* was the main priority. I received this letter in the middle of May, just as spring was taking hold of the city. We felt the call to get out of the temple after the long winter inside and to preach on the streets.

The beginning of this kind of *saṅkīrtana* shows something interesting about Prabhupāda and his disciples. This was the first time that the devotees had taken Prabhupāda’s lead and tried to develop and manage it. When Prabhupāda wanted something done, he would give emphasis to whatever it was. Prabhupāda had told us that he wanted us to go out on *harināma,* but he may have wondered whether the Americans could or would agree to it. *Saṅkīrtana* was done under his order, but he never insisted. Rather, he allowed his disciples to develop it into something that would support them and fan their enthusiasm.

When we first began going out on *saṅkīrtana* in Boston, we went out only once a week. Now we were contemplating going out every day. Gradually, the devotees became more aggressive in how they stopped people to ask for donations. The public called it “begging.”

Our public image began to change. Originally, we only went to parks to chant, and we followed Prabhupāda’s system of sitting down with our instruments while a few devotees passed out magazines to the crowd. In the middle of the *harināma,* one devotee would stand and address the crowd, then there would be more chanting.

As we opened more centers, we started to chant on the streets as well as in parks. We became known for our loud singing, our louder drums, and our *karatālas.* People were often annoyed by our chanting, and sometimes the devotees were arrested for disturbing the peace. Although people claimed to be outraged by the noise, they were more outraged by the strangeness of the devotees in their saffron *sārīs* and *dhotīs*. As soon as we became identified as a religion and our *harināmas* as religious activity, the public began to accept us. And not everyone was outraged. When we didn’t go out on *harināma* for awhile, people would ask us where we had been.

That public image persisted until devotees became more serious about book distribution. Then the devotees decided it was more profitable to wear Western dress. Selling books became more important than singing and dancing on the street; the devotees felt they could reach more people if they didn’t look so exotic. Gradually, the devotees have disappeared from the streets and people have wondered what has happened to us.

Prabhupāda specifically mentions chanting on the streets and at college engagements as our life and soul. He wanted the Hare Kṛṣṇa movement propagated by chanting the holy name, and he wanted us to have faith that the people would benefit just by hearing it. We believed that if people heard the Hare Kṛṣṇa mantra, they would get great spiritual benefit. We may have had our immature or dogmatic versions of what would happen to them, as if simply by hearing Hare Kṛṣṇa they would immediately go to the spiritual world, but we were idealists and strict followers of Prabhupāda. We practiced this *yajña* in earnest.

We also realized as we began to have more dealings with the public that we should have someone sober—a temple president or a *saṅkīrtana* leader, someone with the proper vision of how we should present ourselves on the *saṅkīrtana* party—out on the street with the rest of the devotees. Such a person would ensure that the devotees walked in a neat double file, that they kept to one side of the street if they were doing a walking *harināma,* and that people didn’t become unnecessarily offended*.* Such a person would also keep track of the trouble spots. For example, if the devotees held *kīrtana* in front of a particular store, they might be arrested, whereas another area may not be troublesome at all. Despite such precautions, some of the devotees were wild. In Boston, we discovered that the best place to chant was in a particular spot on the Boston Common (the devotees still chant there). We would go out on shifts from morning till night. Girirāja used to be our best donation collector and I gave many of the speeches.

Prabhupāda sent the devotees out to chant when he visited the centers, but he rarely went out himself. It was too rough on the street. Sometimes people pushed us around or cursed at us, and it wasn’t abnormal to be stopped by the police. We couldn’t allow Prabhupāda to face all these risks and affronts, what to speak of walking around for a few hours, stopping at intersections, then suddenly running across a busy street to the next corner. By 1969, the only *kīrtanas* Prabhupāda held publicly were at universities and in the temples during public lectures.

1969 was the beginning of ISKCON’s *harināma* heyday. Viṣṇujana was leading parties in Los Angeles. He took parties out at 8 p.m. and brought them home at 1 a.m. The later it gets, the more demoniac a city becomes, but the devotees would chant with strength and bounce and rhythm because Viṣṇujana Swami had such strength. He wore gloves over his taped hands so he would be able to play fast *mṛdaṅga* for hours without hurting his hands. He had real endurance. It was around this time that Prabhupāda started inviting the devotees from other centers to visit Los Angeles. He considered it a model temple.

In this letter, Prabhupāda mentions an Omega watch. He was given that watch by Candanācārya, a Canadian devotee who visited Prabhupāda once in Boston. The first time he saw Prabhupāda during a *kīrtana,* he broke down crying. He was so moved to have found a perfect spiritual master and so grateful to Prabhupāda for investing his life with meaning that he gave Prabhupāda an expensive Omega watch his father had given him. In those days, this watch was being advertised as a very high-class watch, a valuable piece of jewelry, and “an exquisite time piece.” It was the most valuable thing Candanācārya owned, and he decided to give it to Prabhupāda.

Prabhupāda immediately accepted the watch and gave Candanācārya his own watch. Those watches then became the basis for a variety of exchanges. Later, the Omega watch stopped working and Prabhupāda called Candanācārya to have the watch repaired. By the time both of them left Boston, the watch had not been returned by the jeweler and Prabhupāda is asking me to find out if it is fixed.

He also mentions the honorariums he should have received for his university lectures. He kept careful track of what was owed to him. I had already given him two checks by the time he left Boston, but he seemed to think there should have been a third.

In his letter he tells me he wants to use the money to buy a compositor. A compositor is a typewriter with the capacity to print typefaces and to justify margins. It took a lot of work to set margins with this machine, but that’s all that was available to us at the time. It was quite expensive—$3,000—but we thought it was wonderful to get such a machine.

This was the beginning. Now we will hear Prabhupāda’s discussion of how he wanted his own press. The compositor was just one of a number of machines that were needed to produce and print a book. Prabhupāda didn’t like working with printers; he had had a bad experience in working with Macmillan, who printed his *Bhagavad-gītā.* Although he was now working with Dai Nippon in Japan and had published *Teachings of Lord Caitanya* with them, he hoped the devotees could learn how to do the work themselves and produce his books.

New Vrindaban  
26th May, 1969  
My Dear Satsvarupa,  
Please accept my blessings. I beg to acknowledge receipt of your letter dated May 17, 1969, and I have noted the contents carefully. I also have received the watch, and it appears to be working properly. Regarding your idea of purchasing a house, if it is possible, do it immediately. When you pay big rent anyway, purchasing a house is nice. Now Murari and his good wife, Lilavati, are there, so this will be a great boon to your Sankirtan activities.  
Please convey my blessings to the others. I hope this will meet you all in good health.  
Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami  
P.S. I have received your check for $100.00. Please let me know if you have received your money from Columbus?

Our desire to buy a house was motivated both by finances and the trouble we continually experienced at the hands of the local teenagers. We hoped to find property in a better neighborhood. That, along with Prabhupāda’s understanding that if we had to pay such high rent anyway, we might as well purchase a house, made sense to us. I was not such a good businessman, but I could understand the sanity of this decision. Our lease was up on the storefront, and we were already paying rent on one apartment. Now because of the influx of married couples, it looked like we were going to have to rent another apartment. The apartment rents were expensive.

But it was difficult to find a suitable place. By the time I wrote my letter to Prabhupāda, I was beginning to think that we were going to have to renew our lease. Girirāja and Murāri, however, became determined to find *something*. In the end, it was Murāri who found the new house.

This letter exemplifies two things: Prabhupāda was capable of giving us relative or apparently worldly advice, and we would accept it with the same submission with which we accepted all his instructions; and Prabhupāda was quite involved in our day-to-day decisions. Of course, we wanted permission to buy property for his society, and Prabhupāda and I were exchanging letters on these points frequently. Prabhupāda’s previous letter to me was dated May 12. This letter is dated May 17. Although it seemed like very little was going on in Boston at this time, each of Prabhupāda’s letters contains a crucial point. Not all his points addressed philosophical issues, but he helped us go forward in our preaching.

In his previous letter he defined our priorities in one sentence: everything is subsidiary to *saṅkīrtana*. Now he agrees that we should move. These letters were important to us because they determined what path we would take. Whatever Prabhupāda decided, we would act upon it.

The temple on Glenville Avenue was in a tough neighborhood. Some of the local teenagers had formed themselves into a gang. They were constantly vandalizing our property or trying to fight with the devotees. They broke the windows at the storefront so often that we finally boarded them up.

When I went to New York to see Prabhupāda and to get married, the hoodlums came again and broke the windows. I decided not to repair the windows until morning. The next day, I started to remove the broken window. A large piece of glass fell from the top of the window frame and cut my forehead and arm. Blood poured out of the gashes and I went into the storefront and layed down on the floor. I remember I was chanting the Nṛsiṁha-deva prayers. A devotee came by and called an ambulance. By then, I was only half-conscious from the blood loss. The gashes required twenty-six stitches. I wrote to Prabhupāda about that accident and he wrote back anxiously, although he said Kṛṣṇa would protect me and help me “in your sincere service.”

After we boarded up the windows, the hoodlums harassed us in other ways. For example, the back door locked from the outside, so they would come around the back of the storefront while we were inside and lock us in. Sometimes they banged on the wooden walls and doors or came into the storefront, challenging us to fight. Other times they threatened us on the street.

Although we called the police regularly about the harassment, they didn’t provide much protection. They weren’t particularly interested in helping us. They weren’t rude—just lackadaisical.

These teenagers seemed to have nothing else to do. They were younger than we were, but they were tough. The devotees tended to be gentle, vulnerable people. We almost never fought with the hoodlums outside of some occasional skirmishes. One time, Devānanda and I decided to sleep in the storefront, and one of the hoodlums came late at night. Devānanda went up behind him and held him in a full nelson. Just then, the police came by and Devānanda turned him over to the police. We really wanted to move. Later when the Press came, we had more devotees with a *kṣatriya* spirit.

We didn’t have strong and dynamic leaders to build up the preaching. We were struggling to maintain the center and fulfill all our duties. We weren’t growing. Prabhupāda assured us that we were trying our best and not disappointing him, but when Murāri came, the whole temple felt an infusion of enthusiasm.

As Prabhupāda writes in this letter, Murāri would add to our *saṅkīrtana* activities. He liked to go out on the street with the others and play the *mṛdaṅga,* and he was good at organizing the *saṅkīrtana,* something I couldn’t do because I was at work all day. Līlāvatī was also a lively presence. Not only did they organize the *saṅkīrtana,* but they had many ideas about how to increase our preaching. At the time, I couldn’t imagine how we had lived without them.

When the order came that the devotees should go out on the streets, Murāri organized some of the girls in the art department to go out. Most of them became good sales ladies. Boston started growing up.

When Prabhupāda writes, “Please let me know if you have received your money from Columbus,” I don’t remember what he was referring to. Perhaps the Columbus temple owed us money—probably for Prabhupāda’s airfare from Boston to Columbus.

I do remember one incident about Prabhupāda’s flight from Boston to Colombus. Prabhupāda carried small Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa Deities with him as he traveled. In Boston, he had worshiped those Deities in a closet in his apartment and he told us that the closet had been hell, but now it was Vaikuṇṭha.

When he left Boston, he traveled on American Airlines. The Deities were packed in his luggage. The airline lost Prabhupāda’s luggage with the Deities in it. The word went out: Prabhupāda said that we shouldn’t fly American Airlines anymore because they lost his Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa Deities. And we never again packed his Deities in his luggage, but always carried Them in the hand luggage onboard planes.

Moundsville  
31st May, 1969  
My Dear Satsvarupa,  
Please accept my blessings. I beg to acknowledge receipt of your letters dated May 22 and May 26, 1969, and I have noted the contents carefully. I am very encouraged to learn of your very nice Sankirtan success in Boston. One thing though you are thinking of quitting your job, that means you are already in the job, so if in spite of your working, your Sankirtan Movement is going on nicely, why you should give up your job? But if you think by giving up your job the Sankirtan Party will be organized more nicely, then you can do that.  
Regarding Jadurany, if she does not improve in a month or two, then she will come back and live with you, chanting Hare Krishna and depending on Krishna. Don’t bother; Krishna will take care of her. I don’t think that she is very dangerously ill because her face indication was not bad. But after all, we have got these material bodies, and some temporary ailments may come and go, but we have to tolerate. But in all cases, you must find out a nice place as temple, either by purchasing or by renting. If you think that advancing $10,000 will get a nice house, then you can find out and we may find out the money. But first you must let me know how you shall repay. If you purchase one house, even you make down payment of $10,000, you have to make monthly payments of $300 or $400. So these things are to be considered. But if you can find a nice house which will accommodate all the householders and brahmacharies, then that will be very good. But do not take on any big responsibilities now. Concentrate on organizing Sankirtan very nicely and try to sell *Back To Godhead.* That is our main propaganda. It is very encouraging that you are daily collecting about $50. Now you have got a nice carriage, and thus you can go anywhere you like. Try to keep nice peace with the police as far as possible, because our method is to be tolerant more than the tree and humbler than the grass on the street. We don’t mind for so-called prestige. If keeping peaceful we can execute our Krishna Consciousness duties, that is all we want.  
Regarding the manuscripts which Pradyumna requested you to send him, first of all let Pradyumna get the machine. Then I shall advise. For the time being you keep it as you have been doing. I shall let you know in due course. Two *Krishna* tapes have been recently sent to you, so you shall receive them soon, if you have not done so already. I hope this will meet you in very good health.  
Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

Prabhupāda writes that he was encouraged to learn of our *saṅkīrtana* success. That success consisted of increasing the number of days we went out on *harināma.* It’s likely I had presented him with some figures, and obviously, I had told him how much money we were collecting.

I had also told Prabhupāda that I thought I should quit my job and go out full-time on *saṅkīrtana.* Prabhupāda had already told me that *saṅkīrtana* was the first priority; everything else was subsidiary. I would never have dreamed of quitting my job unless I could make up the income in some other way. I saw the opportunity to make at least the same income by doing *saṅkīrtana* while simultaneously participating directly in the *harināma* and preaching. That was the kind of leadership we expected from the devotees, that they would participate in preaching and *saṅkīrtana* and enthuse the others to do so also.

This is the second time I tried to give up my job and the second time Prabhupāda told me it wasn’t necessarily a more advanced or surrendered position not to work. Both times Prabhupāda refused to let me quit. Here he presents his logic: I had already told him the *saṅkīrtana* was going on nicely, but I said I wanted to quit my job so that the *saṅkīrtana* could go on. “So if in spite of your working, your Sankirtan Movement is going on nicely, why you should give up your job?” Having a job was a definite financial boon. He had used my income to manage the Boston temple for three years. Not only that, while working I was still able to function as temple president. Why give it up?

Prabhupāda saw it as an idea I had not thought out clearly. I like the insight this letter gives us of Prabhupāda. We may think of a transcendentalist as someone with his head in the clouds. Prabhupāda was realistic and practical. Financial considerations were important to the smooth running of his temples. In the same mood, he was concerned for his disciples’ health and happiness. Therefore, he arranged marriages and concentrated on other human factors, he encouraged a healthy competition between individuals and temples to increase enthusiasm, and at the same time, he was completely spiritual.

Therefore, his questions were simultaneously transcendental and practical. Sometimes he questioned someone’s eligibility to perform a particular service. In this letter he pulls apart my reasoning, refuses to accept it, and then leaves me with the final decision and the criterion to make it: quit your job if you think it would help organize the *saṅkīrtana* even better.

Prabhupāda was always ready to reconsider his opinion. Often, though, he wanted us to think our own opinions out more carefully and to respond to the points that he raised. His letters helped us think things through to their probable conclusions. In that sense, he was our friend. Our interests were identical because we both wanted to do whatever was good for the well-being of the temple, ourselves, and his preaching. When that was our common interest, everything went smoothly.

This is Prabhupāda’s generosity as a manager. Usually when someone goes to the trouble to calculate what should be done, he expects his subordinates to accept his conclusion and support it. Prabhupāda was magnanimous enough to work with us according to our common interests with him, but he often left the final decisions up to us.

I think he managed like this partly because he wanted to acknowledge that we had to offer our money and service voluntarily. Therefore, he always allowed us a certain amount of independence, even when he expressed his opinion. If we chose not to follow his suggestions, we didn’t lose our standing as his disciples. Rather, it is similar to Kṛṣṇa saying to Arjuna at the end of the *Bhagavad-gītā,* “Thus I have explained to you knowledge still more confidential. Deliberate on this fully and then do as you wish to do.” (Bg. [18.63](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/18.63))

We might think that the only proper response was to surrender our independence to Prabhupāda. That was the natural conclusion of many disciples, but it was also true that Prabhupāda didn’t like yes-men. He expected us to think about what he said and, keeping our common interest in mind, present him with our conclusions.

For example, when someone wrote to Prabhupāda that the devotees in America were distributing books dressed like hippies, Prabhupāda wrote back not to that person but to the *saṅkīrtana* leader in Los Angeles, Karandhara. Prabhupāda instructed Karandhara that the devotees should not dress like hippies but should distribute books in devotional clothing. Karandhara responded carefully. He explained to Prabhupāda that the devotees were not dressing as hippies but in clean and proper Western clothes, and that because they were dressing in Western clothing, the income had substantially increased. Prabhupāda appreciated Karandhara’s clarification of the situation and said he had no more objection. Through this exchange, we came to understand that his objection was not to the Western dress, but to the hippie dress.

Prabhupāda wanted to hear from his trusted representatives what they thought in accordance with his interests. His letters always forced devotees to think deeper about whatever he or she had presented to Prabhupāda.

The *saṅkīrtana* was going better, despite the fact that I was still working. We were now collecting $50 a day because Murāri and the women from the art department were selling so many copies of BTG. At $50 a day, they were collecting more than I was earning at my job (I was only earning $20 a day at work).

It was also true, however, that my job was guaranteed income. Although the devotees were collecting money on the street, they had also begun to experience problems with the police. It was a definite financial risk to give up the job.

Prabhupāda also writes, “Concentrate on organizing *saṅkīrtana* and selling BTG.” I thought that by this definition of *saṅkīrtana,* we were able to do both. We sold BTGs and also collected money. Prabhupāda considered the *saṅkīrtana* all-important. He writes, “Don’t take any big responsibilities now.” Could he see us becoming more and more absorbed in the purchase of the new house at the expense of the *saṅkīrtana*? He didn’t want us to unnecessarily burden ourselves with a high overhead, because our main interest was to go out on *saṅkīrtana*.

We didn’t take his words as discouragement from buying the new house. We felt our news was good news: we were distributing more magazines and simultaneously increasing the income. Only because of this increase were we able to even consider buying the house. Ultimately, we paid for the house by taking the risk of increasing the *saṅkīrtana,* and Prabhupāda appreciated our boldness.

Increased income meant we could buy a bigger property. Prabhupāda stresses that it should also be nice. Prabhupāda had different centers, physical locations where his disciples lived. Therefore, the quality of those places was a reflection on him and his movement. A devotee could live under a tree and not care, but Prabhupāda was interested in preaching. If his temples weren’t nice, the congregation wouldn’t be attracted to attend them. If the buildings were well kept and beautiful, he could encourage people to give up their material lives and to move into the temples with us. That was Prabhupāda’s constant instruction to us.

In 1969, all the centers were small. Many of them were storefronts. Actually, we had purposely chosen a storefront because after living at 26 Second Avenue, nothing else felt like a temple to us. Now we were considering a house. This was the second step that almost all centers eventually went through—trying to find something more suitable to improve from our meager beginnings.

In this case, Prabhupāda even offers to help with the down payment. He maintained such help as a basic operating principle even up until his departure. Prabhupāda always kept a variety of funds. He had a maintenance fund for his travel. That was quite small. Each center was supposed to tithe fifteen dollars a month or a similar small amount to that fund. In addition, he had a book fund which later developed into the Bhaktivedanta Book Trust. As book distribution exploded in ISKCON, the book fund began to have large amounts of money. Prabhupāda maintained direct control over that money and he used it to print books and to operate the book business. He used the excess to loan to temples. Even after the BBT was established, when temples applied to the book fund for a loan, Prabhupāda would be consulted. Prabhupāda usually allowed the temples to borrow whatever money they needed, but he expected them to pay it back.

In this case, Prabhupāda did not offer to give us $10,000, but to loan it to us: “But first you must let me know how you shall repay.” Prabhupāda also calculated our mortgage payments in this letter.

Our experience in Boston was very different from Prabhupāda’s experience in New York when he first went looking for a place. Here we were, calculating how we would earn the money to make the down payment and how we would then come up with the monthly mortgage payments; Prabhupāda didn’t worry about the money at all. When he moved to 26 Second Avenue, he didn’t worry about the rent money but simply depended on Kṛṣṇa.

It is an amazing feature of Prabhupāda’s surrender and dependence on Kṛṣṇa that he came to the West completely alone with no material backing. Because the Gaudiya Math had become divided after his spiritual master left this world, there wasn’t enough solidarity among his Godbrothers to fund him from his Guru Mahārāja’s institution. If someone pressed him about what religion he was representing or about who sent him, he couldn’t claim anything other than a philosophical and spiritual connection with the Gaudiya Math.

Prabhupāda once said that we couldn’t tell the nature of someone by his dress. “Just like in the *Bhagavad-gītā,* it is said that *vāsāṁsi jīrṇāni:* ‘This body is just like our dress.’ Suppose a very learned man has come in a shabby dress. Do you think that he should be dishonored? If he’s known, of course. Just like our this *sannyāsī* dress. It is not very costly dress. It is a loincloth. It is very cheap, but sometimes people misunderstand that ‘Here is a beggar.’ And sometimes we are respected. So simply by dress we should not see any living entity.” (Bg. lecture, Aug. 3, 1966)

Prabhupāda didn’t ask his disciples to do what he did—to go alone to a new place without any income—but it inspired us to think that he founded the movement on his complete dependence on Kṛṣṇa. In this letter, it is clear that Prabhupāda wants us to carefully assess the financial situation and not be rash in the name of depending on Kṛṣṇa.

This is another interesting feature of Prabhupāda’s personality. He was a businessman. He had experience with down payments and loans and purchasing property. It’s not that he had always been a mendicant. When he came to America and lived with that total dependence on Kṛṣṇa’s arrangement as he had been living in India in the 1950s, it was a deliberate act. He had given up the normal support most people arrange for themselves. American hippies were accustomed to living as mendicants too, but their renunciation was more an act of irresponsibility and therefore simply another variety of materialism. Prabhupāda was purely renounced in Kṛṣṇa’s service.

It’s interesting to look at how *saṅkīrtana* became ISKCON’s means to raise money. As the pressures to increase the preaching, to house more devotees, and to “do something wonderful” increased, temples needed more and more money to carry out their plans.

Our *saṅkīrtana* collections allowed us to afford a “nice carriage,” Prabhupāda’s antiquated word for van. (Sometimes he called it a wagon.) It was a used van, but for a center as small as ours, it was a large purchase. “Thus you can go anywhere you like.” And we did—in a spirited way. It was wonderful to pile into the back of that van and go here or there to preach rather than going through the ordeal of packing up our paraphernalia, walking to the bus, catching the trolley, then walking again on the other side. Everything took so much longer when we were dependent on public transportation.

Prabhupāda shared all these little joys and events with us. He also shared our reverses. (That “carriage” turned out to be a problem in the end.)

Part of our *saṅkīrtana* management meant trying to keep peace with the police. I wrote him how some of the devotees tended to have a lawless attitude, probably because of their backgrounds. In the 1960s, the youth called the police “pigs.” It was part of the anti-establishment mood to be against “pigs” because the police used to break up anti-war or civil rights demonstrations. Young people often discussed police brutality at that time. (The older generation defended the police force. There was a popular bumper sticker at that time that said: “If you’re in trouble, call a hippie.”)

Some of the devotees carried this mood over onto the streets during *saṅkīrtana*. If the police told us we couldn’t chant in a particular place, some devotees would run across the street and chant somewhere nearby just to antagonize them. They wanted to outsmart them and to show their rebellion. Many devotees thought that this was part of the *saṅkīrtana* spirit.

Boston is not such a big city—and there are only so many patrol men—so as the devotees continued to antagonize the police, word got around and the police became heavier in their dealings with the devotees. I expressed my concern about this point to Prabhupāda because we were starting to have trouble.

His response instructed us not to be arrogant. After all, we had nothing to contend with the police. All we wanted to do was go out and chant. Therefore, Prabhupāda told us to be humble and not try for prestige, even through defiance. Some of the devotees thought that because we were devotees of Lord Caitanya, the police had no jurisdiction over our activities. Prabhupāda wanted us to maintain peace with them and to try and work with their decisions. Again, this was practical advice. After all, the police were much stronger than our small band of devotees.

I see that attitude still persisting among some devotees even today. They feel that because they are on Kṛṣṇa’s business, they have the right to be first in line, first into the elevator, first on the highway. It makes us look terrible. Actually, we should be “ . . . tolerant more than the tree and humbler than the grass on the street. We don’t mind for so-called prestige. If keeping peaceful we can execute our Krishna Consciousness duties, that is all we want.” That was Prabhupāda’s bottom line.

The last point Prabhupāda addressed in this letter was concerning Pradyumna. Pradyumna had asked me to send him manuscripts. Whenever devotees asked me for tapes or manuscripts, I always checked with Prabhupāda before sending them out. In this case, Pradyumna had told me he would be editing the Sanskrit and that he was planning to purchase a machine to enter the diacritical marks. Prabhupāda insisted he buy the machine before I sent him manuscripts.

## 6: June 1969

New Vrindaban  
17th June, 1969  
My Dear Satsvarupa,  
Please accept my blessings. I thank you very much for your letter dated June 14, 1969, sent along with *Krishna* tapes #11 and #12. The tapes are done very nicely as always. The first thing is that I have written to Jadurany today that she may immediately return to you in Boston. There is some inconvenience in Hawaii for accommodating so many people because the landlady has said there are too many living there. So Jadurany will be returning very soon. I have advised her that she may go on Samkirtan Party in Boston. Regarding your present difficulty with the police, things are going by Krishna’s desire, and it will be all right. Try to get recognition from the police that we are a non-profit religious organization. In Los Angeles they have got a permit for begging, so you inquire from Tamal Krishna, and get a copy from him of this permit. We are non-profit religious organization and we have so much expenditure, so try to get this permit. Please keep me posted as to how this is progressing.  
Offer my blessings to the others. I am pleased to note that Murari is such a tremendous boon to your activities there, and you “do not know how you managed without him before.” I hope this will meet you all in good health.  
Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

Again Prabhupāda mentions the *Kṛṣṇa* book tapes. Although I had so many other engagements—my job, my duties as temple president, the preaching engagements in the evenings, *saṅkīrtana* whenever I could—I continued to rise early to type and edit Prabhupāda’s *Kṛṣṇa* book dictation. I considered those tapes my first priority and I was careful not to fall behind. Some of my other duties were burdensome, but typing Prabhupāda’s tapes was my joy.

This getting up early and the pressures of all my service got me into trouble with my job. Sometimes I would go back to the temple in the afternoon to do some of my duties there instead of visiting clients for the welfare department. My supervisor called my apartment in the middle of the day and because I answered the phone, I was caught. When he asked why I wasn’t out visiting clients, I told him I hadn’t been feeling well, so I had gone home, but after that he grounded me. He changed my assignment so that all my work had to be performed in the office.

Because that restriction was never lifted, I started to feel closed in at the Roxbury office. I decided to transfer to Cambridge. I’m sure the Roxbury supervisor wrote something in my file because the Cambridge office insisted I stay in the office too.

Then the devotees started calling me at work and I would answer their calls right in the office. Eventually, the new supervisor stopped that. He told me he wasn’t running a church but a business. I tried disguising what we were talking about, but sometimes that was impossible. There were so many reasons why I should have been at the temple during the day and those reasons spilled into my job and created a strain.

I did some of the editing while I was at the office and I used their photocopy machine to copy Prabhupāda’s essays to save money. One day, I left a page about Lord Śiva in the photocopy machine and I was caught. They banned me from using the photocopy machine.

I was young and more able to go without enough sleep, but I often came to work tired. It didn’t help that the office work was so boring—all paperwork. Sometimes I fell asleep at my desk. I couldn’t seem to fight it off.

My supervisors were starting to become disturbed. First, I wasn’t doing my work, and second, I was falling asleep. I already had a mark against me from the Roxbury office, but my Cambridge supervisor, a woman, told someone else in the office that she wanted me off her unit because I was always falling asleep. She transferred me to another supervisor’s unit and it was clear that my job was at risk. I tried to be more attentive. I remember how I used to water the plants since no one else did it. My new supervisor was an older woman, a chain smoker, and she liked me because I watered the plants, so I didn’t lose my job.

In his discussion of Jadurāṇī’s health, Prabhupāda tells me that there is no room for her in Hawaii, so she was to return to Boston.

Look at how Prabhupāda applied his attitude about health. First he encouraged Jadurāṇī to take practical steps to regain her health. He even held up the *Kṛṣṇa* book production by disallowing her from painting, although he considered the artwork the basis of the book. He had already pointed out in his May 31st letter that bad health is unavoidable as long as we have material bodies.

In that letter he spoke about tolerance and dependence on Kṛṣṇa. This was Prabhupāda’s basic health plan: take serious measures to recover your health, whether it be rest or medical treatment, but ultimately depend on Kṛṣṇa. Don’t be diverted from your main services of chanting and hearing and remembering Kṛṣṇa. If your health doesn’t improve, don’t think that unlimited medical treatments will help you.

That was his general advice, and when he looked at Jadurāṇī, he saw that her “face indication” was not bad. In other words, she was not dangerously ill. Because her stay in Hawaii didn’t seem to improve the situation, we didn’t worry too much about it.

It may be that both Jadurāṇī and I were too absorbed in her health problems. He had already assured me in the previous letter. Jadurāṇī was especially worried because she wasn’t able to paint due to her ill health. Now he doen’t mention it but instructs her to go out on *saṅkīrtana.*

One of the lessons Prabhupāda taught about health was that our endeavor to attain it should include becoming more tolerant. If we are tolerant, we won’t let illness stop our Kṛṣṇa consciousness, even if we have to stop some of our outward services. Sickness is simply a part of life.

New Vrindaban  
20th June, 1969  
My Dear Satsvarupa,  
Please accept my blessings. I am in due receipt of your letter dated June 18, 1969, and I have noted the contents. The pictures you have sent are very nice, and they shall be published in *BTG.* Regarding Madana-mohana, I understand that he would like to remain in Boston, but previously he was indexing *Bhagavad Gita As It Is*. If he is able to work there on this project, I have no objection. Otherwise he may come back to New York. Sometimes, out of their own accord, devotees decide to leave a center, but this is not a good practice. If he can continue his indexing work there, then he may remain in Boston.  
I am very pleased to learn that you are planning for a nice Rathayatra Festival in Boston. Please do it nicely, and take many nice photographs for publication in *BTG.* Yesterday I received one telegram from London to send them by cable $1,025.00. They require this for their house there. This money was to be for a down payment on purchasing an IBM machine for our books, and therefore I now need some money. If you have got some extra money, you can help me. You have encouraged my writing books, and for printing each book will require not less than 6 or 8 thousand dollars. Under the circumstances, I request all centers that as soon as they have extra money, it should be sent to me. Immediately I wish to publish *Nectar of Devotion,* second edition of First Canto, and first edition of Second Canto of *Srimad-Bhagavatam.* So if you will help me in this matter, it will be very nice. After this, I wish to publish *Krishna.* In this way I want to prolong the publication work. I am going to Los Angeles to collect some money, and similarly I am requesting you also to help in this matter.
