Please convey my blessing to the others. I hope this will meet you in good health.  
Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

This letter stresses the practical help we could give Prabhupāda by donating money. When someone asks us to give money, he is testing our commitment to his cause. We may like to exchange spiritual thoughts with someone, and we may even tell a holy man we would like to serve him, but we often become suspicious if that holy man asks us for money. In this letter, Prabhupāda is testing my sincerity. I had encouraged him to write his books. I liked to speak to Prabhupāda about his book publication. I often told him I loved his *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam.* I always assured him that I found his books nourishing, and that I knew they should be printed so that the whole world could benefit from them.

Now Prabhupāda is asking for something more practical than words. A disciple has to realize that the spiritual master is present in this world and that he needs our assistance in practical ways. We may like to visit him at his apartment or preaching center and even glorify his detachment from money as a great spiritual asset, but if he is without money to buy food or pay the rent, then our glorification is not enough. Why don’t we recognize his need and help him?

Often a *sādhu* will not ask for help. It may be that he considers it more humble not to ask, or that he’s feeling too dependent on Kṛṣṇa to ask. When a little confidence and intimacy develops in the guru-disciple relationship, however, then the spiritual master may make a request, especially if the disciple is effusive in expressing that he wants to serve, that he loves the guru, that he finds the guru’s books nourishing, etc. The more he praises, the more impractical his talk becomes unless it is followed by active and useful service.

In this letter, Prabhupāda is saying, “You are always praising my books, but I cannot publish my books unless I have money. If you really want to help, make money somehow or other and send it to me. That’s what I need most from you.”

Of course, I knew Prabhupāda didn’t need “my” money for his personal comfort or interests. Rather, he was expressing something very important to the furtherance of his mission. His books had to be published. One of the reasons he chose America to begin his preaching mission was because of the facility it provided for him to print his books. It turned out that he had even less facility. On his own in India, he had been able to publish three volumes of the *Bhāgavatam* within a few years. Now he was progressing much slower despite the fact that America was the land of plenty and he had a number of disciples. He had to explain to us the importance of this aspect of his mission: “Under the circumstances, I request all centers that as soon as they have extra money, it should be sent to me. . . . So if you help me in this matter it will be very nice. . . . I am going to Los Angeles to collect some money, and similary I am requesting you also to help in this matter.”

As clear as this message was, I wasn’t able to respond much. We didn’t have money in Boston. We had just enough to get by ourselves. Prabhupāda had already collected enough money to buy a piece of book production equipment, but he had had to give it away in an emergency in London. The devotees still turned to Prabhupāda for money as a child would turn to his or her father. Later we stopped—or did we ever stop?

We did take pictures for BTG at his request. We did want to give him whatever he wanted, although often we were unable. We loved whatever orders he gave because they were another way for us to remember him and to remain engaged in his service.

I like to look at the pictures in the old BTGs. They are not technically accomplished—nowadays BTG probably wouldn’t even publish them—but they remind me of how Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura used to evaluate articles by how many times the word “Kṛṣṇa” or “Caitanya” appeared. Similarly, if the pictures contained devotees, drums, and *karatālas,* they were acceptable.

Prabhupāda liked *Back to Godhead* to have a lot of photographs. He was interested in visual presentations of “our activities.” As time went on, the essays became the mainstay and the photos were secondary, but photographs were definitely part of Prabhupāda’s original plan for BTG.

Prabhupāda took pleasure in seeing Western devotees dressed as Vaiṣṇavas, and he liked to show these photos to others. Even at the end of his pastimes when he went to Bhaktivedanta Manor, he told the devotees they looked like demigods because they were so beautiful. He said that they didn’t even look like Westerners—they were so bright-faced. He found it moving to see us chanting together. It was almost as if he could see the fulfillment of Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura’s prediction immediately before his eyes.

While sitting on the *vyāsāsana,* Prabhupāda often looked down smiling at the devotees, his eyes glowing. He was the first to see that they were not Bengalis or even Indians. He derived so much satisfaction to see us become devotees. We were Prabhupāda’s offerings to his spiritual master.

And he prayed for us. Sometimes he looked at us almost anxiously. Sometimes I imagined that he was seeing all the troubles ahead for us. It is such a juxtaposition of moods—Prabhupāda anxious on our behalf and we gathered around him happy and carefree for the moment. Prabhupāda could see that our material desires were still active and understand the implication of that.

Prabhupāda is identified with Western devotees in his prayer, *nirviśeṣa-śūnyavādī-paścātya-deśa-tāriṇe.* Even if Prabhupāda saw in the future that there would be troubles, it was certainly better that he came to the West than not come at all. He delivered us from impersonalism and voidism, from sense gratification and being misled by scientists and other voidists. He came and gave the instructions of Lord Caitanya and personally sat with us, danced with us, and chanted Hare Kṛṣṇa with us. *Back to Godhead* was supposed to reflect that spirit of the *saṅkīrtana* practice, not only in the temple, but out on the streets.

Los Angeles  
27th June, 1969  
My Dear Satsvarupa,  
Please accept my blessings. I beg to acknowledge receipt of your letter dated June 23, 1969, on which date I have reached here at Los Angeles. Regarding Glen’s contribution of $2,000, I may request you to send it to me by return of post. The house contemplated, I don’t think is suitable for our purpose. In your country, a house at $19,000, especially in a city like Boston, must not be very large and accommodating. The best thing is to rent a big house if you cannot purchase a big one. But in all cases, I shall prefer a big house which can accommodate our temple and residence quarters. If possible, the book-binding department will be included also. If you purchase a small house, then again you will have to rent some other house for other purposes.  
Regarding Modan Mohon, I have already written to him, and I am also writing again separately along with this letter that if he finds Boston a suitable place for himself, then I have no objection that he remains there. But he must continue the work of indexing very nicely the original *Bhagavad Gita As It Is.* As soon as this indexing is finished, I shall publish another revised and enlarged edition of *Bhagavad Gita As It Is* at my own cost. I was not happy to publish it through Macmillan as they have crippled the explanations for so many important verses.  
Regarding Jadurany, I am happy that she has returned to Boston again. Now let her join with the Samkirtan Party and I am sure she will improve her health more and more. For the time being, she may not revive her painting work until she gets back more strength. Regarding your Samkirtan Party activities, please continue this most important function. The nice pictures you have sent have been dispatched to Brahmananda for publishing in *Back To Godhead.* I have given instructions that in *Back To Godhead* the pictures of our activities and short descriptions should be more and more published. So continue to send such pictures as you have already sent.  
Please convey my blessings to the others. I hope this will meet you all in good health.  
Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

I don’t remember where Glen got the $2,000, whether it was a gift from his parents or the result of an investment made on his behalf, but he dutifully turned it over to the temple. Although he wasn’t yet initiated, he was not oblivious to the temple management and he was concerned with how the money would be spent. He particularly wanted it to be spent in relation to the Boston preaching.

The contribution came at a time when we were trying to purchase a house. We had written Prabhupāda a letter describing the $19,000 house and requesting that we use the $2,000 toward its purchase. Prabhupāda agreed to our proposal, but when he didn’t agree to our choice of houses and then realized that the $2,000 wouldn’t be used immediately, he decided we should send it to him for other projects. Soon after, however, we found the house into which we eventually moved. Therefore, we did not send Prabhupāda the money but again requested him to allow us to keep it, since buying a new house was imminent.

I remember that Glen and I worked together on this negotiation with Prabhupāda. It was his contribution and he wanted to have some voice in how it was spent. Neither of us wanted to be disobedient to Prabhupāda, but both of us felt we needed the money to help in purchasing the new house. We knew that Prabhupāda had agreed on principle that the money could be used for such a purpose, and we felt if we made a more solid proposal, he would still allow us to keep it despite his instruction in this letter to send it “by return of post.”

When I think of the qualities that Prabhupāda possessed that enabled him to start the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement in the West, I have to include his ability to deal with the world. It’s not that Prabhupāda got our letter about buying a $19,000 house and then consulted his disciples as to what quality house we could get for that much money in this country; he knew just by the price that it would be too small. Prabhupāda’s knowledge of prices in America, how to do business, and his practicality always impressed and enlightened me. When I received this letter, I realized how silly it was to think that we could move into a house for only $19,000. The price was good, but we would still have to rent other buildings to accommodate the devotees and projects. In this letter, we see that Prabhupāda is already considering starting a “bookbinding” department in Boston, and of course, that later became ISKCON Press. A small place would not be adequate.

Prabhupāda was dynamic in his dealings with us. He was guru and father, and he could work with us as easily in the transcendental world as in this one. He didn’t cling dogmatically to the traditional image of the other-worldly *sādhu.* If he had not been able to be grounded in his worldly dealings, the movement would not have spread as it did because we certainly didn’t have the knowledge or experience to make wise decisions in the world.

It’s wonderful to think that other *sannyāsīs* had come to the West to preach but were completely ineffective, but Prabhupāda had the loyalty and determination to carry out his guru’s order, the compassion to want to preach in the West and the ability to adjust himself to whatever circumstances he met. When he arrived in Butler, he adjusted to the fact that there was meat in the refrigerator. He later shared a room in the Bowery, lived with a Māyāvādī, and had to share a refrigerator with Carl Yeargen’s cat food. He survived those compromises of his principles in order to preach. He taught us that anyone who wants to preach has to be prepared to have similar determination. And he had such a practical understanding of how the world worked.

An example of Prabhupāda’s practical attitude is when he had last come to Boston. We were already looking for houses then, and although Prabhupāda knew about it, he didn’t go to see any of the houses we were considering. One of the devotees asked Prabhupāda, “Is it all right if we pray to Kṛṣṇa for the house?” Prabhupāda answered, “Pray and endeavor.” I always remember that answer. It’s not that we can’t pray to Kṛṣṇa for something we think we need for our service, but we also have to endeavor. In this case we did endeavor, but when Prabhupāda heard the details, he rejected our proposal in his active, protective way.

Prabhupāda’s letter increased our sense of urgency. We wanted to move for a variety of reasons—that the neighborhood kids harassed us was one reason, but we were especially concerned that our lease was running out on the storefront within fifteen days and that the landlord was raising the rent significantly. We also had to maintain two apartments to house the devotees. We were starting to earn more money due to the street selling of magazines and incense, but the money was being consumed by the rents we had to pay.

But we couldn’t find anywhere suitable. I was at the point where I was willing to sign the new lease, which would have bound us to the storefront for another two years, because we couldn’t be out on the streets. Glen and Murāri wouldn’t accept my defeat. They were determined to find a new place.

It was characteristic of Glen that he would fight for what he thought was the best deal. I was not so good at worldly dealings and I was prepared to accept our fate of being bound to the storefront for two more years at the higher rent. He refused to accept that. I admired his spirit, but thought, “What can we do?” I remember being inspired by his feeling that *something* could be done, that something *must* be done, so that we don’t have to accept such a bad deal. Glen’s insistence also inspired some of the others to keep looking, and Murāri finally found the new house, the “palace,” as he called it, into which we later moved.

Prabhupāda mentions Madana-mohana in this letter. There was a reference to him in a previous letter saying that he wanted to stay in Boston. Madana-mohana was from New York. He was a short, slight, quiet person, and although Prabhupāda describes him here as an indexer, I don’t think he ever really was. He had offered to perform that service for Prabhupāda, but he had no formal training in it and he didn’t go on to make a career in Kṛṣṇa consciousness out of it. He didn’t have a scholarly nature. Madana-mohana didn’t like NewYork, perhaps because he was quiet and the New York temple wasn’t; he seemed to prefer the quietness of sitting with his index cards and working on Prabhupāda’s *Bhagavad-gītā.*

But he wasn’t a trained indexer. This is an example of someone making an offer of service and Prabhupāda taking him up on it. Prabhupāda considered indexes important. His books would be crippled in their scholarly presentation if there were no indexes. Prabhupāda held him responsible to index the *Bhagavad-gītā.* I don’t think Madana-mohana ever finished the index.

Prabhupāda makes the point here that it is permissible for Madana-mohana to move from one center to another, but he had to be responsible in his service. I remember once being on a walk with Prabhupāda in India when I was his secretary for a few weeks. Dīnanātha, who had been serving in India for some time, requested permission from Prabhupāda to return to the West. Prabhupāda said, “Yes, but it should not be—” and he used a Hindi word that meant scandalous. “You should not leave under scandalous conditions.” He empathized with the devotee who was tired of being in India, but he wanted Dīnanātha to hand over his duties responsibly and to go to a place where he could find good service. It was a similar situation with Madana-mohana. If he couldn’t work in New York, Prabhupāda saw no harm in his moving to quieter Boston, but he should do his work there.

Often devotees didn’t take their work with them, however, and in a previous letter, Prabhupāda mentioned that he didn’t like the devotees moving from one center to the other whimsically. Soon after I received this letter, we held our first temple presidents’ meeting at Prabhupāda’s request, and one of our big points of discussion was how to prevent devotees from moving around so much. History proves that the human factor won out and devotees continued to move from temple to temple, but often the leaving was “scandalous” or whimsical. Devotees would leave one temple and show up in another. We agreed that the temple president shouldn’t just accept such devotees, but should phone the president in their original temple and find out under what conditions the devotee left. The presidents would discuss it and we thought trades should be offered for devotees so that a balance of devotees could be maintained in each center.

Prabhupāda’s real concern was that the devotees act responsibly and be reliable. If he wanted someone to do indexing, they could do it as easily in Boston as New York, but they should do it. Otherwise, how could the society function if the devotees continuously changed their services in midstream? And it is interesting to note from this letter Prabhupāda’s priority: the index must get done. Otherwise, how can he reprint *Bhagavad-gītā* “at my own expense”? His books were important to him.

Although Prabhupāda felt this way, it was rare when he would order a devotee to live in a particular place or to take up a certain service without explanation. He always appealed to our reason and eagerness to help him. He never demanded blind following, although sometimes that’s what was required.

And of course, even if Madana-mohana said, “I have to move to Boston—I can’t live in New York—but I can’t finish the index,” Prabhupāda, with disappointment, would say, “All right, just chant Hare Kṛṣṇa and take *prasādam.*” However, he would remember the incident, especially if the devotee had come forward and made himself prominent. If that same person again changed his service, and then changed it again, Prabhupāda would think of him as whimsical and not take him so seriously. If we come forward and take responsibility for the spiritual master, then it’s an important step in our relationship with the guru. If we later withdraw our offer, the guru may be hurt or think we’re whimsical. That can hinder our spiritual advancement.

In the twelfth chapter of *Bhagavad-gītā,* Kṛṣṇa gives a descending list of practices meant to elevate us in spiritual life: just fix your mind on Kṛṣṇa. If you can’t do that, then follow the regulative principles of *bhakti-yoga*. If you can’t do that, then just try to work for Kṛṣṇa. If you are unable to work for Kṛṣṇa, then surrender the results of your work and try to be self-situated. If you can’t do that, then cultivate knowledge. Initiated devotees understand that they are limited to the first two options: if we can’t think of Kṛṣṇa constantly in ecstasy, then we should follow the regulative principles of *vaidhi-bhakti.*

There is a similar descending list in our service to Prabhupāda. Prabhupāda usually allowed devotees to come forward and offer service. I don’t remember a case where Prabhupāda told devotees they should go out and distribute books, for example. When the young men and women heard his general talks about book distribution, however, and went out and distributed books, they would receive his favor. That favor may not have come in the form of individual attention, but they would know that his “general” talks were not general at all; they were meant for them. By responding to Prabhupāda’s instructions, they felt his mercy.

Often, however, as time went by, devotees found themselves unable to continue working on that highest level. They couldn’t continue to carry out what Prabhupāda now expected them to do. Although Prabhupāda might have been disappointed, he was willing to accept their stepping down to some other service, and he didn’t see it as a failure as long as the devotee was then responsible in that new duty.

We have to correct ourselves and take a responsible stand somewhere, even if we step down from the more heroic position or the first duty we accepted upon ourselves. Then we can still be successful. If we can’t be responsible *brahmacārīs,* then we can become responsible householders.

Devotees often think of this when stepping back includes stepping outside the temple and institutional management structure. Can we say that they have abandoned their service responsibilities and disappointed Prabhupāda? Is wanting to make their own decisions in their *gṛhastha* life whimsical?

Management may sometimes see it that way. Someone may try to exert the same pressure that Prabhupāda exerted, but he will see, as even Prabhupāda saw, that we cannot control everyone. Prabhupāda was flexible and ISKCON managers have to be flexible too. Prabhupāda said that a good manager knows how to bend people without breaking them. If devotees feel encouraged in their services, they will have less tendency to give them up.

If we were to answer the same question from the *gṛhastha’s* point of view, we would have to ask whether there is anything now lacking in the *gṛhastha’s* surrender. To answer that question, we would have to analyze exactly what an initiated devotee is responsible for. He is responsible for the four regulative principles and his chanting of a minimum of sixteen rounds a day. Those responsibilities can never be given up because the vows were taken before the spiritual master and Kṛṣṇa. Those vows constitute our basic commitment to ISKCON and Prabhupāda. Then we have to look at the other factors in a devotee’s life—where is it best to live? What service is it best for him to perform? Under how much managerial authority is he able to live and still remain enthusiastic? What are the basic physical necessities in his life and how will he meet them? A devotee may decide that it is best that he live on his own.

That does not mean, however, that he does not owe allegiance and obedience to the spiritual master. He has to always remember the weighty Kṛṣṇa conscious essence into which he took initiation. Sometimes that essence may take the form of a particular service we have offered to the guru; at other times, it may take the form of something even more basic, such as the strict and mindful adherence to the initiation vows. Remembering that essence and taking responsible steps in our lives to fulfill it is based on our integrity as devotees.

It takes intelligence and association with the spiritual master’s instructions to understand obedience. Some instructions are temporary. The title of this volume is *You Cannot Leave Boston*. I have left Boston, but I wasn’t disobedient. I was given permission to leave. Life is full of changes, so some instructions are temporary. Leaving Boston allowed me to increase my service to Prabhupāda and to become an even more responsible disciple. The principle of obedience is the steadying factor.

One way to understand whether we are practicing the essence of obedience is to look at our activities and to honestly ask ourselves whether we are performing those activities for Kṛṣṇa’s pleasure. When we place ourselves outside the control of a temple manager, it falls more to us to make that determination. This honesty can be very positive in a devotee’s life as well as for ISKCON. Such a devotee can find his sense of obedience intact and practice the basic principles of Kṛṣṇa consciousness, and then he can choose to offer his services to the *saṅkīrtana* movement. Such a devotee is fortunate if he can form a team with a temple manager willing to work with him and whose vision he can share. Most devotees want to trust and work with the temple leaders, and if not to live in strict *brahmacārī* obedience to their orders, at least help to establish the mission. These times no longer have the same simplicity as when a young boy named Madana-mohana was requested by the great personality, Śrīla Prabhupāda, to continue indexing. By following Prabhupāda’s direct order, Madana-mohana knew he would be protected.

The classic definitions of the *āśramas* are sometimes broken by the reality of people growing up. Some *brahmacārīs,* after serving fifteen or twenty years in a temple, no longer wish to live in that way, although they remain celibate. Prabhupāda told us not to become “bachelor daddies,” farcical *brahmacārīs,* yet we now see devotees maintaining their status as *brahmacārīs* who can no longer find a place in the temples. Such a person may have to live independently, but he shows his surrender by remaining spiritually connected to devotional service and to Prabhupāda’s movement. There is room for such people in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Prabhupāda said it takes gallons of blood to make one devotee. If we define their existences too narrowly, we may drive sincere people away.

## 7: July 1969

Los Angeles  
July 17, 1969  
My Dear Satsvarupa,  
Please accept my blessings. I thank you very much for the *Krishna* Tape #13 along with your letter of July 12, 1969, and I am enclosing herewith Tape #16. Now I shall expedite work on the *Krishna* book, as I have completed *The Nectar of Devotion.* Please let me know exactly how far you have finished the *Bhagavatam* thus far. I am glad that Giriraj is doing very nicely. Krishna has sent a good soul for pushing on this Krishna Consciousness Movement. Please keep him nicely. As of yet I have not received the money which you have mentioned in your letter. Regarding your nice Samkirtan collections, this should be kept in a separate account so that it may help in some great emergency. You should become a little spendthrift. I know that you do this, but still I am reminding you. I am so pleased that you will be holding a grand scale Rathayatra Ceremony, and in London also they are planning a very glorious function with at least 5,000 guests participating.  
I hope this will meet you in good health.  
Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

Prabhupāda wrote this letter from a house near the Los Angeles temple in Beverly Hills, California. He had just finished *The Nectar of Devotion.* Puruṣottama typed that manuscript. Since he was living with Prabhupāda as his secretary, it was easy for Puruṣottama to ask Prabhupāda questions on the work and to get immediate responses. Unfortunately, Puruṣottama didn’t know much Sanskrit at that time and the book was printed with mistakes.

This was an extraordinary and productive period in Prabhupāda’s writing. He was writing both the *Kṛṣṇa* book and *The Nectar of Devotion* simultaneously. This letter indicates that he had suspended his work on *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam,* but now he was ready to begin again.

While Prabhupāda was writing these two books, the devotees saw him only on Sundays at the feast. He seemed to be in a retired mood, but these two books were actually his main preaching at the time. As he writes at the beginning of *The Nectar of Devotion,* “Persons engaged in the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement may take advantage of this great literature and be very solidly situated in Kṛṣṇa consciousness.” (NOD, Preface, p. xii) He wrote something similar in the *Kṛṣṇa* book:

The Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement is the unique gift of Lord Caitanya to the fallen souls of this age. It is a very simple method which has actually been carried out during the last four years in the Western countries, and there is no doubt that this movement can satisfy the dormant loving propensities of humanity. This book *Kṛṣṇa* is another presentation to help the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement in the Western world. This transcendental literature is published in two parts with profuse illustrations. People love to read various kinds of fiction to spend their time and energy. Now this tendency can be directed to Kṛṣṇa. The result will be the imperishable satisfaction of the soul, both individually and collectively.  
—*Kṛṣṇa,* Preface, pp. xiv–xv

By the time I received this letter, Prabhupāda was ready to resume work on the *Bhāgavatam.* I love to think of Prabhupāda as a writer. Most devotees probably never think of Prabhupāda living a writer’s life, although we all understand that his writing was his most important contribution to the preaching. Prabhupāda was always self-assured about his writing. He took on such monumental tasks—*Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam, Caitanya-caritāmṛta,* all his summary studies—yet he didn’t seem to think of himself as a writer. Therefore, to hear the details of how he wrote his books, how he wanted them illustrated and designed, is to meditate on an important aspect of Prabhupāda’s service to Kṛṣṇa.

I also like to think about how full Śrīla Prabhupāda’s life was because of his writing. That is, he was given such a wonderful opportunity to express Kṛṣṇa consciousness through his books. It confirms the point that Kṛṣṇa fully engages and empowers a sincere devotee. Prabhupāda had no desire other than to serve Kṛṣṇa and to spread Kṛṣṇa consciousness on his guru’s order. Therefore, Kṛṣṇa empowered him to pursue this goal by writing so many books.

Although we could call his writing of *The Nectar of Devotion* and *Kṛṣṇa* book a peak period in his writing career, he never had to wonder what he would write on next. The *Bhāgavatam* stood throughout his life as an ever-present work always awaiting his attention. The *Bhāgavatam* fed his other writing. Because Prabhupāda was worried that he might not finish the *Bhāgavatam* before he left this world, for example, he wrote *Kṛṣṇa* book.

Prabhupāda began traveling almost nonstop in 1970. Despite that fact, he managed to write sixty volumes of authoritative translations and commentaries, along with several summary studies of sections of the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* and the *Caitanya-caritāmṛta.* How can someone simultaneously travel nonstop and write sixty volumes of Sanskrit translation with English commentary? Those two feats seem contradictory.

People sometimes doubt that Vyāsadeva single-handedly composed millions of *ślokas* and they wrongly assume that “Vyāsadeva” refers to many authors. However, Vyāsadeva was the empowered literary incarnation of Kṛṣṇa. Similarly, Prabhupāda was empowered to simultaneously travel and write. He didn’t have a home base from which to write, but somehow he always found time and space to write.

Pliny gave this writing advice: “Never a day without a line” *(nulla dies sine linea*). Prabhupāda had a similar motto: “Little drops of water wear away the stone. In this way I have written all these books.” Although he traveled, he was a disciplined writer. He would usually spend two hours a day (sometimes less) writing, starting around one o’clock in the morning.

Writers often discuss environment and how important it is that a writer’s surroundings be conducive to creativity. Of course, “environment” can pertain both to our external and our internal surroundings. Prabhupāda carried his external environment with him. He had his book of Sanskrit commentaries, his typist, his Sanskrit editor, his Sanskrit and English dictionaries, and his desk-model Dictaphone. The devotees always provided him with a low desk and a quiet room. Because he wrote early in the morning, he had solitude. Therefore, his “environment” was simple and portable and could be established in any part of the world.

As for his internal environment, Prabhupāda was deeply dedicated to serving his spiritual master and to spreading Kṛṣṇa consciousness. He was so utterly convinced that the big *mṛdaṅga* was the best way to preach that he didn’t become uninspired or feel stale or tired of writing. He was always prepared to go on.

I like to talk about Prabhupāda as a writer because I also write. Another writer can understand a little of the sacrifice that was required of Prabhupāda to write. That doesn’t mean, however, that we can understand the depth of what Prabhupāda was able to do. To follow the writing discipline and at the same time manage a fledgling and later maturing institution would be impossible for anyone else.

The weight of the word “discipline” should not be underestimated even when we think of Prabhupāda. To go on doing something day after day, year after year, requires conviction that the activity is important. What may start out as an idea becomes a life’s work for a devotee because he realizes that this is what Kṛṣṇa wants him to do.

Prabhupāda said the word “discipline” comes from the same root as the word “disciple.” A disciple is one who has disciplined himself. Prabhupāda was a disciple of Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura. His writing was part of his discipleship, and he did it with the same obedience and dedication with which he did everything.

When I try to compare what I do with what Prabhupāda did, the writing itself aside, I have to stop at the simple environment. For example, I get up around midnight to write and so did Prabhupāda. I sometimes use a Dictaphone and so did Prabhupāda. However, the similarity stops there. Prabhupāda wrote all his books while opening himself up to *intense* management of his society. Although he didn’t like the management, and although he said he wanted to free himself of it so that he could do more writing, he never gave it up. How qualified he was to be able to both manage and write.

Just imagine getting up at one in the morning and suddenly remembering the letter you got about the temple that was closed by the police in Argentina, and that other letter about the devotees in jail in Germany. And what about the letter you read about the leader on whom you were counting to maintain the temple running off with all the money or falling down? What to do? Who could write with all that on his mind?

Prabhupāda got up and spoke about Dhruva Mahārāja or Prahlāda Mahārāja. His intense involvement in spreading Kṛṣṇa consciousness went into his writing of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* purports.

For example, besides involving himself in ISKCON management, Prabhupāda traveled and preached the philosophy to whomever he met. We can see in his purports how much he tries to convince us that God exists, that God is Kṛṣṇa, and that human life is meant for going back to Godhead. As he preached during the day, he would preach in his books in the early mornings. Then he would again meet the nondevotee mentality in his room in London or New York or at some public festival in San Diego. As a preacher, he was able to incorporate all his direct experiences into his written preaching. His books have no ivory tower attitude toward the world; they are direct and relevant.

When Prabhupāda was asked on one morning walk, “What is taste?” Prabhupāda referred to his writing. He said he wrote because he liked to: “I am writing books. I am not being forced by anyone. Everyone can do that. Why one does not do it? Why I get up at night, one o’clock, and do this job? Because I cannot do without it. How one will do it artificially?” (Morning walk, November 2, 1975, Nairobi) That statement reveals his incredible dedication.

We can have even more appreciation when we think of his age and physical condition. Although we know that Prabhupāda was transcendental to bodily pains, he spoke of himself as an old man. He told us that we would all have to face these miseries eventually. He said that when one becomes old, there are so many physical complaints. He sometimes mentioned his complaints in his letters—his heart, the buzzing in his ears—but these different ailments didn’t stop him. Later, he was diagnosed as having multiple illnesses brought on by too much stress and travel. We often see Indian *sādhus* living well into their nineties. Although they travel and preach, most of them don’t travel overseas but remain in India where they feel comfortable with their surroundings and where they are able to follow a routine and live in an environment conducive to their piety and *bhajana*. Prabhupāda preached in Western countries, surrounding himself with the vibrations of passion and ignorance and traveling by air from place to place. Perhaps he could have stayed longer in this world if he had not subjected himself to so much inconvenience, but he was compassionate. Despite the inconvenience, he wrote sixty volumes.

Prabhupāda had to organize his time to write his books and he carried that organization over to the rest of his day. Sometimes devotees have a superficial or sentimental idea that spontaneous devotional service means that we don’t know what time it is and we are just flowing from one Kṛṣṇa conscious service to the next. That isn’t how Prabhupāda expressed his service. He wore a watch and kept careful track of time. He followed a strict schedule. He wrote for two hours in the morning because he dedicated those two hours only to writing. Then he went about his other activities—*japa,* temple attendance, preaching, *prasādam,* and rest. He didn’t waste a moment. He expected the same attentiveness from his disciples.

The next point Prabhupāda addresses in this letter is Girirāja: “I’m glad that Giriraj is doing very nicely. Krishna has sent a good soul for pushing on this Krishna Consciousness Movement.” Up until July 1969, there had only been three devotees living in Boston. We were preaching, but no one was joining. Boston has many colleges, so naturally our preaching was aimed at the large student population. Many of the people who attended our programs on a regular basis had a sentiment for spiritual life, but they weren’t interested in participating in a spiritual movement and following the four rules. Even Lord Caitanya experienced that it was difficult to attract students to devotional life. I had been idealistic when I first went to Boston. I imagined flocks of people becoming devotees. But that isn’t what happened. The ’60s was a time of change and idealism. It’s a wonder that any of us took to Kṛṣṇa consciousness at all during that era.

Finally a few people joined, but they weren’t serious. It was natural, therefore, that when Girirāja, who was serious and well-mannered, joined, I wrote to Prabhupāda and appreciated him. Girirāja was always willing to do the needful. He would go out and collect money or distribute *Back to Godhead* magazines, and he liked to rise early in the mornings. He was also submissive and interested in the philosophy. Having Girirāja at the temple gave us relief.

Girirāja personified our ideal. Prabhupāda wrote that “Krishna has sent a good soul for pushing on this Krishna Consciousness Movement.” We accepted his words as absolute. It didn’t even occur to us that Prabhupāda was using religious rhetoric to encourage us, that Girirāja didn’t come because he was looking for spiritual life or because he was attracted by our expertise as preachers; we simply accepted that Girirāja had been sent by Kṛṣṇa.

Prabhupāda’s words gave us faith. If we simply preached, Kṛṣṇa would respond by sending people. Even though we were neophytes, by Prabhupāda’s grace and on his own conviction, we accepted and worked according to this principle. Whatever is accepted on faith and practice can be realized. We governed the whole movement on this principle: simply serve and Kṛṣṇa will, in His own time, send devotees to help us increase our service. Perhaps we repeated these principles to each other dogmatically or without full realization, but all of us could point to Girirāja and say, “Prabhupāda said that Kṛṣṇa sent Girirāja,” and we would see the truth in the principle.

Preaching was difficult in Boston. I even felt embarrassed before Prabhupāda as I tried to compete with centers such as New York. Why couldn’t we become a big temple? It’s natural for the temple president to see the lack of growth as a reflection of his own inadequacy, and I was no exception. I felt inferior. Prabhupāda always encouraged me, however. He always assured me that we were doing our best and that we shouldn’t be disappointed. I tried not to be disappointed, but it was difficult. Having a devotee like Girirāja join and then hearing Prabhupāda’s analysis helped. It gave me back my faith in preaching and in Kṛṣṇa consciousness itself.

It’s interesting how because I was the temple president, I wrote to Prabhupāda about how all the devotees were doing but rarely about myself. I didn’t have time to worry about myself or how I was doing in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Still, I never felt that my letters to Prabhupāda excluded my personal needs. Whatever selfishness or needs I had would be consumed by having so much service to perform and other devotees to care for.

Prabhupāda instructed me to “please keep him nicely.” I was still working at this time, so I was away from the temple all day, but I remember coming back in the evening, remembering Prabhupāda’s words, and trying to develop a relationship with Girirāja.

Prabhupāda’s request to “keep him nicely” indicates how Prabhupāda was now the founder-*ācārya* of a growing institution. He couldn’t be there to take care of Girirāja himself, although, of course, he did care for him through his instructions. He expected me to do that service. It was another way in which Prabhupāda depended on his disciples to help spread the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement. If we didn’t care for the devotees, Prabhupāda said, the movement would fail. I heard him say once that if the devotees in ISKCON were not sincere, then people would come and remain unaffected. He depended on the devotees to be sincere and to train new people by always setting a good example. That was how he planned to nurture his movement’s growth.

Prabhupāda trusted us quickly. He was serious about what he was doing and he would always talk to us from the depth of his seriousness. If we responded, he trusted us and entrusted us with work on his behalf. “Take care of him nicely.” Every line in Prabhupāda’s letters is in that mood. He was a serious person, and he trusted us to understand and adopt his serious mood and become sober followers. He expected us to take care of the money, to take care of the devotees, and to take care of the buildings we used as temples because all these things were of such importance. One professor later said that Prabhupāda converted drug-addicted hippies into servants of Kṛṣṇa and humanity. We became responsible because he gave us so many duties.

Prabhupāda never gave us the impression that everything depended on him only. Of course, spiritually it did depend on him only—it still does—but he expected us to become fit helpers in spreading the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement.

For example, Prabhupāda personally trained the devotees who went to England in the summer of 1968 by inviting them to spend time with him in Montreal. He personally taught them to sing and to play *mṛdaṅga* and *karatālas.* At this time, he began to speak of an international *saṅkīrtana* party that he would eventually send to travel the globe. He wanted to train devotees and then send them to foreign countries to preach. They would then train the local people as devotees and turn the management of the temples over to them. The *saṅkīrtana* party was supposed to keep moving from place to place. Prabhupāda was not thinking that he had to direct everything personally and that he was the only one with the potency to preach. He wanted to empower us with his potency so that we could act in his service, according to our capacity and under his ultimate direction. Therefore, he expected his newer disciples to take shelter of his older disciples even when he was present with us.

This is an important point that is often overlooked in our society. Śrīla Prabhupāda saw the preaching potential as coming through him, not from him. Prabhupāda thought it was important that his disciples were sincere. That implies that Prabhupāda’s disciples could also develop their own potential as preachers. If the disciples weren’t sincere, then new people would either not be attracted or would leave. Prabhupāda’s followers are meant to become reservoirs of Prabhupāda’s preaching potency.

Another nice point in this letter is Prabhupāda’s instruction that I become “a little spendthrift” with the *saṅkīrtana* collections. He wanted me to keep the money in a separate account so that “it may help in some emergency.”

I remember how the word “spendthrift” piqued my interest. The devotees even joked about it when they read Prabhupāda’s letter. I took it to heart. I didn’t really know the definition of the word. Was a spendthrift a person who squanders money or a person who saves it? The dictionary defines “spendthrift” as “one who spends improvidently or wastefully.” I took it that what he actually meant was that I should not waste money.

Those personal touches have created “Prabhupāda-isms” for his followers. It’s almost as if we speak an insider’s language with him. We didn’t care what the dictionary said; we only wanted to know how Prabhupāda defined the word.

I can almost picture myself in the little kitchen at the Glenville Avenue storefront, delighted that Prabhupāda had given me such personal advice, not just about temple management, but about dealing with my own character. “I know that you do this, but still I am reminding you.” I was pleased with Prabhupāda’s affection, his personal touch. He could have said, “Don’t waste money.” Instead he said, “I’m not sure about you. Maybe you do waste money. That’s why I’m telling you.” I could have become defensive: “What? I don’t waste money. Why are you saying that?”

Prabhupāda was an expert psychologist and he knew how to disarm us. “I know you don’t waste money, but listen anyway because this is so important that I want to say it even to someone as qualified as you. *Don’t waste money*!” We were flattered by him, but at the same time, we were willing to hear him seriously.

Prabhupāda was usually gentle in the way he gave instructions. That didn’t mean that he didn’t speak the truth. For example, he lambasted the materialism of American culture to his American disciples. Although we were practicing Kṛṣṇa consciousness, we weren’t completely detached from what we saw as the amenities and progress of our country. We liked the highways and had grown up among the skyscrapers. We had also been taught throughout our education that American science was the best in the world. Prabhupāda tore at our attachments and then said, “Don’t mind if I say this.” It touched our hearts.

In this letter, Prabhupāda also mentions our Boston Ratha-yātrā: “I am so pleased that you will be holding a grand scale Rathayatra Ceremony, and in London also they are planning a very glorious function with at least 5,000 guests.”

There was no principle more sacred to Prabhupāda than our spreading Kṛṣṇa consciousness. He was willing to use anything to accomplish that mission. He didn’t exploit people or money, but he used everything he could in Kṛṣṇa’s service.

One of the tools he had at his disposal to spread Kṛṣṇa consciousness was his relationships with his disciples. He wanted us to serve Kṛṣṇa purely, and he worked hard to change our minds and hearts. That meant presenting Kṛṣṇa conscious propaganda and sometimes taking advantage of particular situations.

For example, sometimes Prabhupāda quoted mundane authorities when normally he would call that authority nonsense. Or he might say that five thousand people would be attending the London Ratha-yātrā at a time when the devotees in London hadn’t yet figured out how to stage a Ratha-yātrā. Or he might say that there were fifty devotees in a temple when really there were only twenty-five. If it suited his purposes, he might appear to exaggerate, but we can’t look at his statements as exaggeration.

His power to use “the ends justifies the means” could overwhelm his disciples. Sometimes people think that the “ends justifies the means” is a corrupt principle, but in the hand of Kṛṣṇa’s pure devotee, it is a weapon against ignorance. As Prabhupāda’s disciples, we came to trust that he knew best and his judgment could even supersede mundane morality.

I mention this because Prabhupāda refers to our “grand scale Rathayatra” in this letter. I was the one who told him it would be grand, so if that was misleading, I was at fault. Prabhupāda was so strong in his encouragement, however, that he always made us feel that whatever small thing we did was significant in Kṛṣṇa’s eyes. By asking for something big or by thinking big, we would be encouraged to try for it. If we wrote to Prabhupāda and told him we were going to have a grand Ratha-yātrā, he would write us back and tell us about another temple that was going to have an even grander Ratha-yātrā—five thousand guests! Then we would try that much harder so as not to be embarrassed. “Prabhupāda thinks we’re going to have a big Ratha-yātrā. I guess we had better have one.”

He also always took us at our word. If we said we would produce results, he expected us to do it. If in a moment of extravagance we said we would donate $100, he would accept our offer and we would be forced to carry through with it. He let us back down if we had to, but by getting close to someone as powerful as Prabhupāda, we realized that we would have to be prepared for surrender or be exposed as fools. Prabhupāda meant business.

This is why I don’t worry about the accuracy of his facts and figures—was London Ratha-yātrā really attended by five thousand guests? It doesn’t seem important. The abiding truth was so great and perfect that we simply wanted to surrender to that.

This was especially true of the 1960s and 1970s where the predominant philosophy was, “If it feels good, do it.” Prabhupāda was trying to establish something solid; he needed firm commitment from valid followers.

That was probably one of Prabhupāda’s greatest obstacles in managing the movement and spreading Kṛṣṇa consciousness: he had sincere followers who were sensually weak, who weren’t so qualified as devotees, who had engaged in all kinds of sense gratification. These were the same people he was trying to turn into responsible *brāhmaṇas* and *kṣatriyas—*managers, “spendthrifts,” festival organizers, preachers. From Prabhupāda’s viewpoint, it was a difficult obstacle to overcome. It became more and more obvious as time went by that the quality of his recruits left something to be desired.

## 8: August 1969

Los Angeles  
5th August, 1969  
My Dear Satsvarupa,  
Please accept my blessings. I am in due receipt of both of your letters, one dated July 31, 1969 and the other August 2, 1969. The letter of August 2nd appears to be a practical proposal. The calculations for the house are nice. But everything should be done very carefully. If the landlord allows you to take possession of the house on payment of $12,000 on the terms and conditions as stated by you, then you must enter the house immediately, and we take it for granted it is Krishna’s offer. As far as you having to pay $6,000 down payment by October, from your calculation it appears that you shall be able to pay it. So in that case, the money paid by Giriraj may be deposited in a separate bank account for this purpose. If things go on according to your calculation, this opportunity must be taken; but I am always afraid of persons like Mr. Payne. You know the incident in New York how the real estate man, Payne, entrapped us by $6,000. I think Giriraj’s father is a lawyer, so he can help you in this connection, or any other lawyer friend. So if things are done very carefully, this scheme is approved by me. If they will give you immediate occupation of the house, and if there are no other tenants there, then it is all right. But if there are tenants, it will be botheration. We cannot deal with tenants, so if they are there, you may not accept it. But if the house is occupied by ourselves only, then it is all right. I think Giriraj is a very intelligent boy, so do everything carefully, and let me know the result. If this house can be occupied as our own, then the press department may be established in Boston immediately. If I go to New York on my way to Europe, then most probably I will stop at Boston also to see the new house. So do everything very carefully, and I shall await your further report in this connection.  
Please offer my blessings to the others. I hope this will meet you in good health.  
Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami  
P.S. I have also received another letter by Special delivery. So everything is Krishna’s Grace. Take the risk for Krishna but do everything very diligently. I sanction it.

We were moving forward to purchase the new house. It seemed like such a great deal. Prabhupāda liked the idea of a bigger building and in this letter, mentions that perhaps the ISKCON Press would be moved to Boston. It’s obvious from this letter that he went over my calculations on how we would pay for the house, and of course, he reminded me of the time we were cheated in New York by Mr. Payne. Prabhupāda trusted us to negotiate this deal on ISKCON’s behalf, but he was also aware of how easily we could be cheated.

Our real estate agent’s name was Mr. MacDonald. He was a cheater, an Irish-American, crass and materialistic. He came from South Boston, and he reminded us of a tough, cheap, foulmouthed gangster. The owner of the house was an undertaker. He had used the house to showcase his coffins and to store his supplies. (I still remembering finding bottles of embalming liquid in the basement.) They pretended to be interested in us, but we could see through their artificiality. They seemed willing to risk us as buyers, even though it was obvious that we were all young, that none of us had money or a business, and that all we did to support ourselves was to sell magazines on the street. They probably thought they could suck some money out of us until we defaulted and then foreclose and keep their profits. When Girirāja and I met with MacDonald and the owner during a pre-purchase meeting, they suddenly told us to wait and they went outside to confer. I found it odd how they withdrew from the room and then returned in a positive spirit, ready to accept our offer.

Girirāja was careful and cautious. We expected to be cheated, so we decided to show the contract to Girirāja’s father. As far as we could see, however, it was a good deal. The house was selling for $75,000. They were giving it to us with a $12,000 down payment.

I told Prabhupāda we could earn the money by increasing our book and magazine distribution. I had held an *iṣṭa-goṣṭhī* with the devotees—the few of us that were there—and told them that my paycheck was the sole support of the temple. To pay for this house, we would have to increase our magazine distribution dramatically. This was at a time when the devotees *had* dramatically increased their distribution, but we needed still more. It was a risk, but the devotees were spirited.

We had a sweet group of devotees in Boston in those days—Nanda-kiśora and Jāhnavā, Śāradīya and Vaikuṇṭhanātha, Jadurāṇī. All of them agreed to do the needful. It was such a good deal that we didn’t want to pass it up.

Our mortgage payments were to be over a thousand dollars a month. When we finally did move in, we were late with our payments a few months in a row. Even though Mr. MacDonald had been nice to us when we were still potential buyers, he would come by and curse us with foul language when we were late.

Finally, he and the owner called us in for a meeting at the owner’s funeral parlor. (Actually, the owner used to sell us old flowers.) The owner and Mr. MacDonald were of similar character, so it was a hard-nosed meeting. The owner told us that because we had been late twice, he was going to add an extra thousand dollars onto the purchase price as a penalty. If we didn’t agree to the increase, he would evict us immediately. “But I very much like your group and what you are doing,” he said, “and that’s why I am adding on the extra thousand dollars. You have to learn discipline. It’s for your own good.”

We assured him that we would pay on time in the future and we presented our excuses for being late, but we only gave the appearance of accepting the thousand-dollar penalty.

As soon as we returned to the temple, however, I called an ACLU\*2lawyer named Friedman, who had helped us with problems in the past. He was young and idealistic, and when I related the details of our meeting with MacDonald and the owner, he immediately became angry and told us that MacDonald had no right to charge us an extra thousand dollars, that it wasn’t legal, and that he would do something about it. I’m sure he saw us as idealistic kids being cheated by tough businessmen.

MacDonald was furious that we had dared approach a lawyer. To make matters worse, we publicized the event by speaking about it with a reporter from *Boston* magazine. I spoke candidly about the initial terms under which we had purchased the property and told him about our dissatisfaction with MacDonald’s dealings. MacDonald became even more furious.

We never did fail in making our payments, although we were late once in awhile. I appreciated that even from a distance, Prabhupāda knew to warn us not to trust these men despite the fact that they were so well dressed. He knew they were only interested in cheating us. At the same time, Prabhupāda encouraged us to take the risk.

We were so naive in those days about how to deal in the world. Although we had more legal help with this deal than we did in New York, we later realized by talking to others that our down payment was high and that our mortgage rate was inflated. Many people thought the building was overpriced, although in our first meeting with MacDonald, he assured us with all eloquence that because we were a religious movement and we were so nice and everything, he could price the building far below the current market value and give us a low down payment.

We only saw that the house was a mansion. Later we found out that it had been on the market for some time. Prabhupāda accepted my presentation and assured me that it was a good deal. I think he expected me to decide for myself whether I was getting a good deal or not.

I don’t remember feeling sensitive about Prabhupāda’s mention of my involvement with Mr. Payne. This was a new chapter in my life and I wasn’t about to repeat that mistake. I felt positive. Prabhupāda wanted us to go forward and I felt such camaraderie with the small crew of devotees and such faith in Prabhupāda’s words—“ . . . we take it for granted it is Krishna’s offer. . . . Take the risk for Krishna but do everything very diligently. I sanction it”—that I didn’t worry too much about whether or not it would be successful. That was the power of our faith in Prabhupāda. We didn’t worry about whether Prabhupāda knew Boston real estate. We simply accepted that he had consulted Kṛṣṇa and that Kṛṣṇa had approved. We knew that Kṛṣṇa had made this offer, not Mr. MacDonald.

Still, Prabhupāda warned us to be careful. Therefore, we entrusted Girirāja’s father to look over the paperwork and expose any cheating he found. We trusted the law, that regardless of their intentions, the deed was a legal document and everything had to be stated in print. Girirāja’s father studied the deed and assured us that it was all right.

Prabhupāda mentions Girirāja’s money in this letter. I had different exchanges with Prabhupāda about that money. Prabhupāda had asked for the money in his letter of June 27. Now it’s August 5 and we hadn’t sent the money. I wrote to Prabhupāda telling him that I wanted to keep the money to buy the house. At that time, we were looking at a different house, one that sold for around $20,000. Then I told him I was going to buy a house for $17,000, but Prabhupāda didn’t like that house either. Girirāja kept delaying in sending the money. He was always very careful in thinking out all the implications of something before he acted on it. He didn’t want to disobey Prabhupāda, but he knew we needed his money to make the down payment. He was sure that Prabhupāda would understand our need if we could present it to him properly. Therefore, there were three references to his money in my letters. First he told me to send the money, then he told me he hadn’t received it, then in this letter, he told me we could keep it and use it toward the purchase of the house.

The Boston devotees had a spirited approach to the purchase too. Śāradīya, Nanda-kiśora, and the others all pledged to earn money, and I wasn’t an autocrat. We discussed things together.

Girirāja’s father, Mr. Teton, helped, but he wasn’t able to do too much for us from Chicago. Still, we didn’t work through a lawyer in Boston on the purchase but accepted Mr. Teton’s advice. The deal was closed on Janmāṣṭamī and I spent the day at the court buildings taking the documents through the final stages and paying the closing fees.

Girirāja Swami has a charming nature, and that was especially true when he was young. No doubt he worked that charm on his father too. Mr. Teton’s biggest concern was that his son not be drafted into the Army. He didn’t really understand what ISKCON was about, but his relationship with his son was more important to him than the fact that Girirāja didn’t want a career. He was doting, and we felt his tangible hope that everything would be all right. He maintained his interest in ISKCON and spoke to me as Girirāja’s authority to make sure Girirāja was getting enough to eat, that he had warm clothes, and that he didn’t get drafted. Now Girirāja was going to use his money to help purchase this building. Well, that was all right too, he guessed, so he was willing to look over the paperwork. He wasn’t really sympathetic toward Kṛṣṇa consciousness and he kept his distance from the philosophy, but he wanted to have contact with his son.

In those days, we expected people to like us. Prabhupāda encouraged us to show people that instead of being drug-addicted hippies, we were nice people, clean, religious, preachers of God consciousness, helping society. People would see the obvious differences between what we were and the promiscuous and dirty youth walking around the streets. We were remarkably different. People would be astounded.

At least that was our idealistic hope. People didn’t always see us as clean and religious. Rather, they saw us as following strange beliefs, wearing strange clothes, begging on the streets, and not always so clean.

People often looked at Girirāja in the way we imagined they would look at all of us. Girirāja was also shrewd. I remember being with him at the government buildings dealing with some officials. When we got in the elevator—two state officials and the two of us—he told them the story of baby Kṛṣṇa and the fruit vendor and how if we give charity to God, we will receive so much in return. The officials were amused. I forget exactly what we were trying to accomplish there, but after Girirāja told them his story, the officials waived a small fee and gave us special treatment. Girirāja had that power over people.

Prabhupāda mentions in this letter that we shouldn’t buy a house that already had tenants. I think he was considering this situation according to Indian law. In India, if you buy property and there are already tenants living there, it is almost impossible to evict them. He later had that experience in Bombay. There were no tenants in this particular building, of course, but I remember questioning the real estate agent about it because Prabhupāda was concerned.

The building was surrounded on one side by a nursing home for the mentally retarded, and on the other side a fast food store. The house was right near the corner of a busy intersection in the commercial district of Allston. Although it was closer to Cambridge than to Boston, it was still quite busy.

Devotees sometimes look for houses in commercial areas rather than in residential areas because no one complains about the parking or the loud morning *kīrtanas*. We didn’t discuss these points with Prabhupāda except to inform him that the house was close to a busy area. Prabhupāda trusted us to find the best place.

When I think of it now, it’s amazing that we bought this place without a lawyer. We could have been trapped in so many ways, and it was a commercial property, which indicates how much renovation work we had to do.

I felt close to Prabhupāda while negotiating this purchase. Later, he told me that it was a daring and progressive move. Boston suddenly became prominent among all the temples. It was so wonderful to try and capture the spiritual master’s attention, to prove to him that we weren’t lazy and that we wanted to do something to develop his center. We liked to remember the instruction he had given us on his last visit to Boston. We had taken Prabhupāda to look at some real estate near the storefront. The real risk wasn’t so much getting cheated, but deciding whether we could meet the increased overhead. A devotee asked whether we could pray to Kṛṣṇa to get the house. Prabhupāda said, “Pray and endeavor.” That became our motto, and we saw immediately that we had to do both.

On that same walk, Prabhupāda encouraged us by repeating a Bengali saying: a businessman says you can spend twelve hours a day collecting a hundred dollars by travel, but if you have a good location, you can collect more just by staying in one place. He told us we could apply that to our preaching. A good location was worth the endeavor it took to maintain it.

Los Angeles  
6th August, 1969  
My Dear Satsvarupa,  
Please accept my blessings. In further reference to your letter of August 2, 1969, now that you have procured this big house, I think it will be ideal for beginning our printing department in Boston immediately. You have written that there are two big halls, so do you think one of these halls will be nice for printing machines and workshop? You may immediately write to Advaita and Vaikunthanath in New York and invite them to go there to Boston to begin printing operations.  
I hope this meets you in good health.  
Your ever well-wisher  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami  
P.S. If it is possible, I would like to stop off in Boston on my way to Europe to see this house.

By the time we received this letter, we had already purchased the house. There were two big halls, suitable for a temple and *kīrtana* hall. The house was a three-story Victorian mansion with gables and many rooms. I had described it to Prabhupāda in one of my letters and now Prabhupāda decided it would be a suitable place to set up his press.

We had just moved in when I received this letter and we hadn’t even had time to decide how we were going to use all the space. Prabhupāda had heard only a brief description of the house, but he had already envisioned how it should be used.

Prabhupāda was still printing with Dai Nippon, but he wanted his own printing press. We weren’t prepared to fulfill all his plans yet, but he at least hoped we could print his smaller runs and save a lot of money. Advaita had trained himself in press work, and Uddhava had learned how to manipulate photographs and to work with graphics. Vaikuṇṭhanātha, Śāradīya’s husband, did the bookbinding.

The beginnings of the press were in New York at this time, but Prabhupāda and the devotees involved had been discussing where they could relocate it. They had already considered New Vrindaban, but Prabhupāda later rejected that idea. Boston suddenly became viable when we purchased this large house. We were shocked. It had never occurred to us that the press would come and claim a large portion of our new building.

Some of the devotees were even disturbed. Jāhnavā presented the radical proposal that the press machines could go in the basement. We wanted to keep the halls for our own purposes, one for a temple room and *kīrtana* hall and the other for a *prasādam* hall. Then someone suggested that perhaps the heavy press machines would be too heavy to be on the first floor. Eventually, Advaita came to Boston from New York and inspected our house, and of course, he loved it. It even had a back entrance complete with a loading dock. He didn’t think the floor was a problem and said he could brace it from the basement. He not only claimed the hall, but the front room for the office and the basement for photography.

I had mixed feelings about it. I was initially disappointed that so much of the building was going to be swallowed up by the press, but I also realized that having the press in Boston would be a great boon to the temple because so many devotees would move there. The movement was small, but many devotees immediately started writing to us informing us that Prabhupāda had asked them to live in Boston and work with the press. Each couple would take up their various services in the press and do *saṅkīrtana* or other temple duties. We would become the biggest and best temple in the movement. I saw that it was to our advantage.

The others were divided in their opinions. So much energy was suddenly going into the press that Murāri expressed his doubts. He was instrumental in increasing the *saṅkīrtana,* so having the press move to Boston disturbed him more than anyone else. He wasn’t disturbed by the press using so much of the building, but mostly because of something Prabhupāda had said with which he couldn’t agree. Finally it came out. He couldn’t understand why we were producing so many books and magazines. We weren’t able to sell them all anyway and they were simply being stocked. We had not yet discovered how to sell mass quantities of books and magazines. *Saṅkīrtana* still meant chanting on the street and selling a few copies of *Back to Godhead*. The idea of producing and selling large quantities of books just wasn’t practical, according to Murāri. I can still see him trying to control his anger as he and a few other devotees told me I should take a stand on this point.

One or two of the women also complained. The women in Boston had an equal voice in our decisions in those days. They lectured and went out on *saṅkīrtana,* and we heard their opinion on all matters. Some of the press devotees criticized the Boston temple for this reason. In New York, the women could never have formed a group and expressed how they felt, and the press devotees felt the women had too much control in Boston.

Ultimately, however, Prabhupāda was the mover behind our activities. If we discuss how sincere the devotees were in wanting to distribute magazines, or how we cooperated to collect more money to buy the building, how we were naive in our dealings with the real estate man, how we did anything, we can see in those descriptions glorification of Prabhupāda. All our service was rendered only for his pleasure. We all wanted to measure up to his standard.

As the movement grew, Prabhupāda’s letters became increasingly important to us because we so rarely saw him. I often think of Prabhupāda’s letters as stones thrown into a pond. One letter could ripple out to all his centers and establish Kṛṣṇa conscious standards or instruct us on the philosophy or encourage us to beat the *bṛhat-mṛdaṅga.*

There is an important lesson to be gained from this letter. Sometimes devotees make plans to serve Kṛṣṇa in a particular way. The spiritual master may come and change those plans. In this case, we had worked hard to purchase a house to increase our preaching, and Prabhupāda decided we should use the house for another purpose. We were attached to our plans because we thought we had made the best decisions regarding the details of our situation. Now he asked us to surrender those decisions. The guru has the right to expect such surrender from the disciple.

Los Angeles  
19th August, 1969  
My Dear Satsvarupa,  
Please accept my blessings. I beg to thank you for your letter dated August 18th, 1969. I was very much anxious to receive your letter, but I did not know that you stopped writing me on account of the memo issued a few days ago. The memo was circulated just to minimize correspondence in minor affairs. I think in ordinary administration the presidents of different centers may consult amongst themselves and make certain general procedures by mutual agreement. But when there is some more important factor, that must be referred to me. I did not mean by the memo that you shall stop correspondence. If you understood like that, then I say that you can send me every day one letter, and it will be welcome. We are now growing in size, therefore, for general administration if you will act conjointly amongst the presidents, that will be a great relief for me. Besides that, you are doing editorial work of my books so you have to correspond directly with me. I never meant that about editorial work also being referred to Brahmananda. I understand that you had some meeting amongst the East Coast presidents of the centers, and I shall be glad to know how you decided to work. I have not heard anything about your joint decision.  
Regarding the press, I wish this to be in Boston because you are now getting your own house there. In your last letter you also invited many householders to live there. Because all the press managers are householders, and you are also householder, so combinedly if you manage the printing and publication of our books, that will be a great success. If we have got our own press, we can earn some money by outside work when there is no pressure of our own work. So this is very important subject matter and keep me informed about the advancement of the idea.  
Regarding my going to Boston, yes, I wanted to go there to see the press started before my going to Europe. We must have many books printed, either by ourselves, or through publishers or through any other means. This is more important than my touring in Europe. Regarding *Krishna,* formerly you were sending me the transcribed copies as early as possible, and if you continue the same process, then I shall also send you the tapes consecutively. I think this book with pictures by Jadurany will come out a unique publication. I shall be glad to know if the pictures can also be printed in our own press. That is also a very important thing. I am very glad to learn that Brahmananda, Advaita and others have gone there and you are doing the needful. If you think my presence will further help in this connection, on hearing from you I shall immediately go.  
Since Jadurany has resumed her painting work, I think Jahnava may be engaged in joining the Samkirtan Party, because she is a good salesgirl of *BTG.* Anyway, do things in good sense, without any disturbance. We have to make progress very soberly, and I am always at your service whenever required. I have not heard also what further arrangement is done regarding the house. Has everything been settled up nicely regarding the documents, etc.? You have mentioned nothing of these things in your letter under reply. Also, your telephone has been out of order for at least the past week, and I do not know why this is so.  
Please offer my blessings to your good wife, Jadurany, and to all of the others. I hope this will meet you in good health.  
Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami  
P.S. Tell Brahmānanda that press ̮̮̮̮̮̮̮̮ at New Vrindaban is most impractical at the present moment, because there is transport difficulty. For the time being first of all organize the Boston center very nicely & then we shall divert our attention elsewhere. We cannot diffuse our attention in many things all at once.

Prabhupāda circulated a memo telling the devotees not to write to him directly anymore. I felt hurt when I received the memo, as if my relationship with Prabhupāda were being threatened or cut off. In the earliest days, we had lived with Prabhupāda in New York. No one could stop us from seeing him and asking him our questions. Life changed when Prabhupāda went elsewhere. The only recourse we had if we wanted his association was to write him letters. I wrote Prabhupāda once or twice a week and he replied to me just as often. I was dependent on his letters to ease my separation from him. To suddenly think that I could no longer write to him was too much for me to take. I couldn’t become angry with him, but I was hurt.

When I say I was hurt, it implies an emotional reaction, not a cool assessment of the situation. Therefore, I stopped writing him completely. I told myself that I had to follow what he said and that I was not an exception to his rule. As Prabhupāda points out in this letter, however, he did not mean it in such an all-encompassing way. I was an exception because of the service I was performing for him. Of course, I could have inquired from him how the services would go on if I couldn’t communicate with him directly, but instead I stopped writing. I was prepared to wait for him to contact me. Perhaps I hoped that my silence would attract his attention.

Reading between the lines of this letter I could understand that Prabhupāda knew I was hurt. He responded so nicely over this incident that to this day I feel happy and loved by him. He not only invited me to write to him, but he told me that I could write him every day and it would be welcome. I felt special and touched by his love. Even though the movement was growing and he had so many disciples, he still wanted to hear from me. It was a wonderful and joyful feeling, a quiet and private satisfaction that warmed my heart—“Prabhupāda remembers me and knows me.”

I don’t really remember how the other devotees in the temple reacted to Prabhupāda’s memo. I’m sure it affected them too, because they had been accustomed to writing to Prabhupāda whenever they wanted to. But the memo stands as a historical record that Prabhupāda did want to reduce his mail. Sometimes devotees claim that they were stopped or discouraged from writing to Prabhupāda, or that the leaders got between them and Prabhupāda. Although there may be some truth in that, this is relatively early in the movement and already Prabhupāda was trying to slow down the number of letters he received from his disciples. He had already begun to expect us to draw his instructions from his books.

Brahmānanda also indicated that I should no longer send the typing and editing work directly to Prabhupāda, but to him. He would forward it to Prabhupāda himself. I couldn’t see how the work could go through Brahmānanda, and Prabhupāda assures me in this letter that I could continue to send it directly to him. Having to send manuscripts and tapes back to Prabhupāda afforded me the opportunity to enclose letters. Sometimes I asked philosophical questions about the purports I had edited or asked him about the words he had used. It was part of our regular exchange.

Just remembering this incident makes me want to write to Prabhupāda now, even thirty years later. I know some devotees do write letters to Prabhupāda now as a meditation. Prabhupāda told me that if I felt the need, I could write him every day.

Prabhupāda was so open and permissive in exchanging with his disciples. How do we qualify for such an invitation from the spiritual master? By service. He mentioned that I was editing his books, and of course, I was a temple president. If a devotee wanted to write frequently to Prabhupāda, he had to have something to say.

When Prabhupāda describes the different *rasas* in the spiritual world, he explains that in *śānta-rasa,* the worshiper sees God as great, but is so awed by His majesty that he doesn’t render service. He doesn’t think God *needs* service because he can see that God is already complete. Prabhupāda gave the example of the President of the United States.
