The President is great, but of what use to us is his greatness? We have no exchange with him. If, however, we are able to develop a friendship with him, then his greatness will become manifest to us in the exchange of practical dealings.

That’s what we need if we are going to write to the spiritual master. We need to be reporting to him, describing our service, the work we are doing on his behalf. As a by-product of the practical exchange, we will also have personal exchange. It’s not that the only concentration is our personal progress.

It was amazing that Prabhupāda had noticed that my phone had been out of order for a week. He must have asked his secretary to try to reach me. That in itself is interesting because we rarely spoke to Prabhupāda on the phone at this time.

Because there would be management questions as well as philosophical inquiries, Prabhupāda suggested we form a group to oversee the management. This request was a precursor to his forming the GBC. By 1969, there was no central authority to make decisions that affected the entire movement. Therefore, we needed to become more organized. It was only a year after I received this letter that Prabhupāda had us form the Governing Body Commission to manage the movement rather than sorting out our problems in each individual temple. Prabhupāda told us it would be a great relief for him if the leaders could cooperate. Prabhupāda even wrote to the temple president in New York, “I am getting so many letters just from New York . . . If all problems come to me, even personal problems, then it becomes a heavy task for me. I received your letter, full of problems; Gargamuni’s, full of problems; Rayarama’s, full of problems; and similarly ISKCON Media’s, full of problems. If everyone’s problems are sent by me, then who will solve my problems? I have divided these departments to solve problems, but if in the end they are all sent to me and I have to tackle, then just imagine what is my position. The best thing would be to stop all activities and simply chant Hare Krishna.” (Letter, May 15, 1969) Prabhupāda wanted the temple presidents to work conjointly and make decisions about general procedures by mutual agreement.

At the end of that paragraph, he mentions a meeting we had held among the presidents. Our meetings were infrequent, but they were our attempt to follow Prabhupāda’s instruction. It may be surprising to hear Prabhupāda ask for a report of the outcome of that meeting because Prabhupāda was completely in control of everything. When I read that line, I remember wondering whether or not he felt trepidation about it. There’s nothing in his wording to indicate how he felt about our meeting, but later, we heard that some temple presidents were overriding Prabhupāda’s authority. This has been described in *Śrīla Prabhupāda-līlāmṛta.* Prabhupāda didn’t want the devotees to replace him; he didn’t want them to second-guess his authority and to control their Godbrothers. Neither did he want them to hold unauthorized meetings or to make resolutions that wouldn’t please him. Therefore, in this letter he wanted me to inform him of the outcome of our presidents’ meeting.

He wanted us to manage things properly. That required that the different temples consult each other and make decisions for the movement’s benefit. It is a natural tendency for managers to become bureaucratic or to make top-heavy committees, decisions, and resolutions. Then both the managers and their subordinates lose the spirit. Prabhupāda wanted our managers to alleviate his burden and to increase the preaching. He set the example of what it would mean for us to alleviate his own burden by his managerial policies. He didn’t try to manage or control the devotees, but he left us to discover our sense of individual responsibility.

The committee members were to become sub-managers. They would handle the trivial, day-to-day problems, and they would consult Prabhupāda on major decisions. There were few centers in 1969, and our little committee consisted of only the East Coast temple presidents. We discussed topics such as BTG distribution quotas, philosophical questions, questions of etiquette, ISKCON Press, and the allocation of manpower. We might have discussed other topics, such as what to do when devotees leave one temple for another and how to get permission from the police to perform *saṅkīrtana.* It was a big change for us to learn how to trust Godbrothers when we were so accustomed to hear only from Prabhupāda.

A big part of management involved the publication of Prabhupāda’s books. Although some of the devotees doubted the practicality of printing many books, Prabhupāda wanted more books printed. He considered book printing more important than his upcoming tour of Europe, although preaching in Europe was part of his worldwide mission. ISKCON Press was the beginning of his disciples’ attempts to fulfill his dream.

Although he said in this letter that he would stop in Boston on his way to Europe, he never came. Perhaps he knew that things were just beginning. We didn’t even own much press equipment and the building first had to be renovated. Still, when he mentioned in this letter that he wanted to come, we realized that ISKCON Press was his project, that it was important, and that by serving in the press, we could serve Prabhupāda in a direct and special way. Prabhupāda writes, “If you think my presence will further help in this connection then I will go there. Upon hearing from you I shall immediately go.” It never occurred to me that by moving the press to Boston, Prabhupāda would make Boston his headquarters and sit and write. Neither did he suggest it. His offer to visit was a special treat.

We were making quick progress. Two weeks after I wrote this letter to Prabhupāda, Brahmānanda moved to Boston. Gradually, more and more devotees came—mostly young men and their wives and sometimes children. Every time a new devotee came, we would partition off another section of a room with blankets or plaster board. Suddenly the temple was full of potential *pūjārīs* and *saṅkīrtana* devotees.

Other decisions were made to faciliate the press. Both Jadurāṇī and Jāhnavā were good painters, but Jāhnavā was also an excellent *saṅkīrtana* devotee. Therefore, it was decided that Jadurāṇī should concentrate on painting and Jāhnavā should concentrate on book distribution. I presented this plan to Prabhupāda and he approved.

When Prabhupāda told us to do our duties without any disturbance, it was natural that we wanted to know whether our duties were pleasing to Prabhupāda. When we received a letter such as this one in which Prabhupāda said that Jāhnavā was a good salesgirl, the devotee mentioned would feel enlivened in his or her service regardless of what it was. We didn’t tend to measure one service against another; we simply wanted to know what service Prabhupāda wanted us to perform. And of course, we wanted Prabhupāda to be aware of what we were doing.

This was a nice way to manage a temple. We didn’t try to control other devotees, but we presented Prabhupāda’s instructions and the devotees accepted that they were serving Prabhupāda directly. Just to hear Prabhupāda describe us in his own words, “salesgirl” or some other term, provided the sweetness for which we were hankering. It gave us an identity in Prabhupāda’s eyes. If Prabhupāda said we were intelligent or a good preacher, everyone would respect that identity, and we ourselves would try to live up to his descriptions.

Prabhupāda also said, “We have to make progress very soberly.” The American devotees were wild. By 1969, his oldest disciples had been with him for only three years. He trained us to think everything out thoroughly. I don’t know what he had seen in his travels, but we did have a whimsical tendency to leave one temple for another without considering the repercussions either for ourselves or for the temples involved. Prabhupāda’s constant instruction was that we should act with a cool brain. In one lecture, Prabhupāda states that we come into the human form of life and see that everyone is dying. We identify with the body and live for sense gratification, which will be taken away. All these things should be considered with a cool brain. Human life means sober, *dhīra.* We have to realize that we had a young body and now it is gone. We have become old. We should consider this soberly and not be disturbed that we will die and take another body.

Part of our sobriety meant becoming properly situated in the *gṛhastha-āśrama* and becoming responsible*.* Most devotees had quit their jobs when they moved into the temple, and because the movement hadn’t yet faced the implications of this, we assumed that the many new families that were being formed through marriage would somehow be maintained by the temple. Couples accepted small quarters in the temple and went on with their services.

We can say that this mood was idealistic and naive, but Prabhupāda didn’t discourage it. He referred to marriage as “doubled strength” and asserted that the householders could certainly make a positive contribution to ISKCON. Whether the couples remained in the temple or went off to open new centers, Prabhupāda tried to harness their service for the movement.

In this letter he refers to Boston as a *gṛhastha* temple. That meant that we had twice the work force. What is the difference between a *brahmacārī* and a *gṛhastha*? A *brahmacārī* is one and a *gṛhastha* is two. No one thought of *gṛhasthas* as handicapped or unable to perform service.

This is an interesting point to note about Prabhupāda—that his movement was *gṛhastha*-oriented, and in defiance of other *sādhus,* even his Godbrothers, he saw it as a positive feature of ISKCON. He was able to bring such devotees right into temple life and give them responsibility, instead of thinking of them as congregation.

Of course, Prabhupāda also wanted *brahmacārīs.* Therefore, the *brahmacārīs* had to maintain their own spirit even as they lived among young householders. All the devotees understood that householder life was a lower platform than complete celibacy. Becoming a householder implied that a devotee was attracted to sex life. The *brahmacārīs,* therefore, had to maintain their own identity.

At the end of this letter, Prabhupāda asks me about the documents and deeds for the house. I didn’t mention anything about these papers in my letter to him, but Prabhupāda was thinking about them.

Years later, when I was the GBC for Detroit, Prabhupāda asked me to see the deed to the Fisher Mansion. I hadn’t been the GBC when the property was purchased, so I had no idea where the deed was. Prabhupāda didn’t want to know where the deed was, he wanted me to hand it to him. I remember asking Ambarīṣa for it, who asked his lawyer. Prabhupāda was always suspicious that on some technical grounds, someone was going to claim ownership of a property that we thought we owned. He was aware of how people could cheat and the dangers of the material world.

Why is a *paramahaṁsa* concerned with documents? Because he is conducting a movement in this world. This world is run under material laws and therefore he must protect Kṛṣṇa’s property. Prabhupāda was right in this case: later we had to prove that we owned the property.

Even after the press moved to Boston, Brahmānanda continued to see the move to Boston as temporary and to plan for it to eventually move to New Vrindaban. Therefore, in his P.S., Prabhupāda writes that the idea to move the press to New Vrindaban is not practical.

Hamburg  
27th August, 1969  
My Dear Satsvarupa,  
Please accept my blessings. I am sure that you know I have come to Germany via New York on Monday morning. It is understood from different sources that Rayarama has written you a letter complaining that the charge of BTG has been taken from him without any justification. But the actual fact is that he was spending 600 dollars per month for maintenance of Iskcon Press with the result that the number of subscribers was only somewhere about 300. Most of the BTGs were being sold by our Samkirtan Party in Los Angeles. Anyway, the 600 dollars were to be saved for other useful purposes. Therefore, the staff engaged in Iskcon Press was dispersed, but Rayarama was there in his original position as managing editor. But since this expenditures was stopped, he is little bit sorry; so much so that he does not come regularly and practically he is inclined to give up our relationship. So I called him when I was in New York, but his attitude is different. I did not see him in the airport on my arrival or departure, neither did I see him at the function held on Sunday. So I think in the future you will have to take charge of managing BTG in consultation with Brahmānanda when the press will be started in Boston. I have already decided to start the press, and I am glad to have your assurance that you will help in this respect, even with financial help.  
I thank you very much for this straightforward offer.  
Please let me know what is the position of the house at the present moment. You have promised to pay them 6,000 dollars down, and if it is possible, why not pay him the 6,000 down immediately. This will finish the transaction immediately instead of waiting for three months. I shall be glad to hear from you in details about this matter.  
I hope this will meet you in good health.  
Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

Prabhupāda had now left America for the second time. We didn’t understand it then, but it was the real beginning of his worldwide preaching. We still couldn’t imagine that Prabhupāda would be away for long. Mostly, we were excited for him. He was going to meet the Beatles and stay with John Lennon. The Hare Kṛṣṇa record was out on the Apple label. It was exciting even to think about it.

But events in America were on his mind. In this letter he asks me about a letter exchange I had had with Rāyarāma. This was in reference to a meeting Prabhupāda had had with Rāyarāma at New Vrindaban about BTG’s editorial policy. Prabhupāda mentions one of the points in his letter, that Rāyarāma was spending $600 a month which Prabhupāda considered unnecessary. But Prabhupāda was not only displeased with the budget; he was also displeased with some of the articles that Rāyarāma printed and the direction the covers were taking.

Rāyarāma had an independent nature. Although he lived in the New York temple, he had his own program separate from Brahmānanda’s authority. Brahmānanda was a powerful leader, the temple president, but Rāyarāma was not submissive to him. Still, Prabhupāda encouraged him. He especially wanted him to dedicate himself to BTG. In one letter Prabhupāda wrote, “I have noted with great attention your statement that you are sitting at one desk in a tiny office, room, which is ferociously hot in summer and freezing cold in winter. So people might think you mad if they heard of your crazy work. So spiritual activities are like that. Sometimes my contemporaries from India, they also write that in this age, the age of 73, when people would naturally like to retire, and I am working here. So this is all possible by Grace of Krishna. Krishna can adjust opposing elements. . . . ” (Letter, October 15, 1968)

Under Rāyarāma’s direction, BTG gradually changed from what it had been in 1966. He now printed interviews with groups such as the Beach Boys and book reviews of mundane authors. At one time, we had printed only pictures of Kṛṣṇa on the cover, but now many of the covers were nondevotional. One cover in particular seemed to bother Prabhupāda. It was a picture of Arjuna, but Prabhupāda thought Arjuna looked like a monkey.

The other devotees had no idea that Prabhupāda was displeased. We assumed that Prabhupāda liked what Rāyarāma was doing because Prabhupāda never expressed his discontent. Rāyarāma was intelligent. It was obvious to us why he made the covers look less devotional because we were selling them on the street and in the head shops: he wanted to compete with the other magazines on the market. He wanted to attract attention to BTG and to reach a bigger audience.

Prabhupāda had a valid point on the discussion of budget, too. Rāyarāma had decided that BTG should be supported by subscription. Therefore, he engaged some devotees to go out and sell subscriptions. The $600 a month Prabhupāda mentions in my letter was to maintain those devotees. Prabhupāda saw it practically. The fact was that the magazines were not being sold through subscriptions but on the streets of Los Angeles by the *saṅkīrtana* devotees. Why waste money?

When Prabhupāda tried to discuss his complaints with Rāyarāma, Rāyarāma was defensive and refused to surrender. He felt that he was working as hard as he could on the magazine, that it was his project, and he showed no willingness to take the role of a disciple in this exchange. Everything changed when Rāyarāma would not accept Prabhupāda’s reprimand.

It was not unusual for Prabhupāda to reprimand his disciples even this early in the movement. What was unusual was for a devotee not to submit. We were naturally submissive to Prabhupāda just as children are naturally submissive to their fathers. If Prabhupāda showed even a small amount of displeasure, we immediately wanted to rectify the situation. Rāyarāma didn’t exhibit this mood.

In *The Hare Kṛṣṇa Explosion,* Hayagrīva writes that when Rāyarāma came out of Prabhupāda’s room, he was pale and looked as if his “soul has been picked up, spun around and thrown back in his body.” That meeting and his refusal to surrender was the end of his relationship with Prabhupāda.

Later I heard of another meeting in New York in which Rāyarāma again argued with Prabhupāda. I heard second or third hand that Prabhupāda had told Rāyarāma that he should serve unconditionally and that Rāyarāma had disagreed. Rāyarāma argued that if the lights went out in a room, we would not be able to work. This proves that it is not possible to serve unconditionally. Prabhupāda replied that we can see Kṛṣṇa even in the dark. Then I heard that Prabhupāda said, “You are young now and the boat is leaving. You had better get on board.” Rāyarāma refused to surrender.

I had heard all these things, but not directly from my friend, Rāyarāma. Therefore, I wrote him a letter. We all looked up to Rāyarāma because he was one of the most senior devotees. He was also the most learned in the philosophy. When Prabhupāda first went to San Francisco, only Rāyarāma was competent enough to give the evening lectures. He could stand off the whole Lower East Side crowd and their sometimes wild questions. He impressed us.

He and I were friends. We had lived together in my First Street apartment in 1966, and although he joined only a few weeks before I did, he was confident in his relationship with Prabhupāda. He helped me ease into the life of a devotee and to deepen my connection with Prabhupāda. I wrote him out of concern.

His letter was full of criticism. Besides the discussion about BTG, for example, he criticized the fact that we had recently attended a peace rally in Washington, D.C. and collected hundreds of dollars. He said our collection was fraudulent because the devotees had claimed they were collecting for the peace movement when they were actually raising money to print Prabhupāda’s books. It was a sharp and rational argument from an insider who had suddenly become an outsider and it was a little shocking. Rāyarāma said he didn’t agree that the end justified the means. Printing Prabhupāda’s book was sacred, he said, but if it were important, the money would have come in another way.

His reply tested my loyalties to Prabhupāda and ISKCON. He insisted that he had been treated unfairly and that it was Prabhupāda’s anger that had created the problem. He believed that Prabhupāda had misunderstood the situation and him.

When I look back, I realize that because Rāyarāma was so strong-minded, we overlooked so many symptoms of his lack of surrender. Rāyarāma was charismatic, and he appeared right from the beginning to be attached to Prabhupāda. He was so capable that we tended to overlook signs of disagreement. For example, he refused to wear a *dhotī* and he wouldn’t shave off his beard. He didn’t like to go out on *harināma.* Later when I asked Prabhupāda how such a strong devotee could leave, Prabhupāda said simply, “He was not serious.”

Although I say my loyalties were tested, it was more due to a sense of sorrow and loss that I felt at Rāyarāma’s leaving than a lack of faith in Prabhupāda. There was no question, however, of maintaining a relationship with anyone who criticized Prabhupāda. I never wrote Rāyarāma again. Rather, I realized instantly that I had to stay with Prabhupāda.

In his letter, Prabhupāda informs me that Rāyarāma misunderstood his position. Prabhupāda did not dismiss Rāyarāma either as a disciple or as *Back To Godhead’s* editor, but Rāyarāma no longer wanted anything to do with Prabhupāda or the magazine. “His attitude was different.” I took Prabhupāda’s letter as a gentle and indirect warning that I should protect my loyalty to him. Whatever Prabhupāda’s intention, he always looked after my spiritual life.

It is common that when a leader leaves, others may question their commitment to Kṛṣṇa consciousness, but our relationship with Prabhupāda was so strong that no one left. By the end of 1969, however, many leaders had left—Kīrtanānanda, Hayagrīva, Rāyarāma, and Puruṣottama, and then Brahmānanda, Subala, Viṣṇujana, and Gargamuni Swamis were asked to leave the society, although later they were readmitted. Prabhupāda was personally involved with each of these devotees and he carefully made his position clear to the rest of us. Therefore, these devotees were unable to create doubt in Prabhupāda’s other followers.

Also, all the devotees still had direct access to Prabhupāda and could confirm their faith with him themselves. No one could say that they had not been fairly dealt with by the authority because the “authority” was Prabhupāda. Whatever the problems, the most fundamental aspect of our devotional lives was our relationship with Prabhupāda. Nothing mattered as much as that.

Rāyarāma’s leaving afforded me the opportunity to offer more service to Prabhupāda, and I volunteered to edit BTG. This was assertive on my part; Prabhupāda didn’t ask me to help in this way. In this letter, however, he writes, “I thank you very much for this straightforward offer.”

We think of a disciple as someone who waits to be told how to serve, but sometimes it’s better to go forward to offer service. Even if there was a tinge of ambition in my offer, my deeper motivation was to serve Prabhupāda. Therefore, I made the offer and left it up to him to accept or reject it.

Prabhupāda’s sentence, “I thank you very much for this straightforward offer,” still warms my heart. Sometimes we reach out for association and are instead rejected or ignored. At other times the exchange is sweet. Prabhupāda reciprocated with my offer of service immediately, so this line has become memorable for me in my spiritual life. I can understand from it that Prabhupāda appreciated my coming forward instead of waiting for him to appoint me or someone else. That I took the initiative seems to have given him some relief. Of the different types of disciples, the best is he who serves from his own initiative in a way that pleases the spiritual master. Vibrancy is created in the guru-disciple relationship when a disciple always thinks how to please the spiritual master but at the same time is not attached to the outcome of his activities. If the spiritual master doesn’t like what he has offered, the disciple is willing to offer something else.

Viṣṇujana Swami once made a similar point in an article he wrote for BTG about the Rādhā-Dāmodara party. He described the excitement of traveling around the country and of the preaching and *saṅkīrtana.* Then he said that the one feature of the service that was most enlivening was that they were always waiting for Prabhupāda’s order. They were ready at any moment to adjust their program according to his desire.

At the end of his letter, Prabhupāda again discusses the house. He is eager to finalize the details. “You have promised to pay them 6,000 dollars down, and if it is possible, why not pay him the 6,000 down immediately. This will finish the transaction immediately instead of waiting for three months.”

I can’t remember all the details, but from Prabhupāda’s letter, it sounds as if Mr. MacDonald gave us three months to raise the first $6,000. Upon paying that deposit, we would be allowed to occupy the house. Then he gave us a certain number of months to raise the next $6,000, at which time he would surrender the deed. The first $6,000 was in the bank. Prabhupāda instructed us to pay them immediately. Prabhupāda liked the essence of the proverb, “Strike while the iron is hot.”

He also instructs me in this letter to write back and inform him of the details. We felt he was watching us to ensure that we didn’t make any mistakes in our dealings. That sense of his watchfulness made the arrival of his letters even more exciting. His words changed our lives. After this particular letter, I became editor of BTG, I no longer considered Rāyarāma a friend, and we bought the house.

## 9: September 1969

Hamburg  
4th September, 1969  
My Dear Satsvarupa and Brahmananda,  
Please accept my blessings. I am in due receipt of your letters dated August 31st, 1969, as well as the telegram dated September 4th. By the telegram, I understand that by Krishna’s Grace it appears to be settled up, and you can continue your proceeding, chanting always Hare Krishna. He will always give us protection. I am very anxious to know about Uddhava’s condition of health. May Krishna protect you all.  
Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami  
P.S. Police must give us special protection on account of threatening.

Prabhupāda wrote this letter as a response to two letters from Brahmānanda and me describing an attack on the temple by a neighborhood gang. I’m sorry, but to tell this story requires that I relate a drama where Prabhupāda doesn’t appear to be the main actor. Prabhupāda was the main actor because we went through everything for him. Prabhupāda once told a story to illustrate a similar situation:

“In Calcutta, a great dramatist, Mr. Rath, who was also a very well-known government official, wrote a book, *Shah Jahan.* Shah Jahan means the emperor Shah Jahan. The title on the book is the name of the book’s hero. So one of the friends of Mr. Rath inquired, ‘In your book, *Shah Jahan,* the actual hero is Aurangzeb. Why have you given the book the title *Shah Jahan*?’ He could not understand it.

“The author replied, ‘My dear friend, the actual hero is Shah Jahan, not Aurangzeb.’ Yet the Shah Jahan book is full of activities of Aurangzeb. But the fact is that Shah Jahan was the emperor. He had four or five sons, and when his wife died at an early age, he built her a memorial. Those who have gone to India, who have seen the Taj Mahal building, that was constructed in the memory of Shah Jahan’s wife, Mumtaz, by Shah Jahan. He spent all his money constructing that building. It is one of the seven wonders of the world. Shah Jahan was a very affectionate father also. He did not chastise his sons much. He spent all of his money constructing for the memory of his wife. But when the sons grew up, the third son, Aurangzeb, came out very crooked and he made a plan how to usurp the empire. He killed his brothers, and he arrested his father, Shah Jahan. So this is the plot of the book *Shah Jahan.* But the author says that Aurangzeb is not the hero. The hero is Shah Jahan. Then he explained.

“Why? Because Shah Jahan was living, sitting in the Agra fort as a prisoner, and all the reactions of Aurangzeb’s activities—the killing of his other sons, the usurping of the empire—all these things were beating on the heart of Shah Jahan. Therefore he was suffering and he is the hero.”

In a similar way, although the action of this particular story involved his disciples in Boston, Prabhupāda is the actual hero of this story because all our activities were “beating on his heart.”

It was the end of summer and we had been living in our new house only a few weeks. There were many devotees in the building by this time because so many press devotees had moved to Boston with their wives and children. One night I suddenly woke up because I heard loud noises downstairs. We were already accustomed to people driving by the house and shouting things at us, but this noise was different. It was coming from inside the building. I pulled on my pants and ran to the top of the stairs. Then I saw that some men were in the building fighting with a few of the devotees. It was the local gang. They had broken in and surprised us. They had armed themselves with car antennas that they had apparently ripped off parked cars just before coming in. (The judge later determined that this could not have been a serious attack because this gang had armed themselves only with car antennas.)

I realized immediately that I had to join the fight. One of the women had left an iron laying around. I grabbed it on my way downstairs. As soon as I came downstairs, one gang member came forward to meet me. We wrestled back and forth, me trying to keep possession of the iron and him trying to take it from me. Finally he got it from me. Even now I remember that I had no real intention to use such a weapon—it had a heavy point to it—and I remember even as I was being disarmed that I wasn’t holding on to it for dear life. Then I hit him in the arm and the back with my fist.

The fight didn’t last long before the gang ran away. You could say we drove them out, but then two of them came back. Murāri picked one of them up and threw him out the window. He said later he didn’t know what he had done or how it had happened, but he was a good fighter and had already punched a few of the others. It may be that they left because of Murāri’s strength in fighting. They might have gotten more than they bargained for.

After they left, we were in shock. Some of the women appeared on the stairs, trying to understand what had happened and caring for the men’s injuries. I ran upstairs and called the police. While I was on the phone, the gang returned.

The second attack took place within twenty minutes of the first. Although we had watched them drive away, because we were in so much shock, we didn’t see them return until we heard the windows in the front breaking. It was again shocking to hear the glass tinkling and smashing to the floor. The gang had returned with reinforcements. This time there were more of them; some of them had clubs.

Their intention seemed to be to frighten us enough that we would want to leave the neighborhood. I looked around the building—we had been doing a lot of construction, so there were plenty of two-by-fours the length of baseball bats, but with right angles. We distributed them to the devotees and faced the gang. I also remember that we all spontaneously started chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa in unison. We were frightened, but the holy name seemed to frighten them.

Uddhava was probably our best fighter, so he fought the gang leader. At one point, Uddhava disengaged himself, ran to the kitchen, and came back with a knife. It wasn’t a big knife, but more the size of a pen knife. He stabbed the gang leader in the side while they were fighting and rolling and the gang leader scratched at Uddhava’s eyes. We thought it was going to be a fight to the death. Then the leader stood up, disengaged himself from Uddhava, said something to his gang—an order to finish us off—and suddenly quaked. Just like in the movies, he fell over unconscious.

That demoralized his followers. I had run upstairs to call the police again. Although most of the followers were demoralized, a few were still fighting when the police finally came.

I think some of the neighbors had also phoned them for us. Seeing what was happening, the police promptly arrested the people who had attacked us. They also took the leader to the hospital because he was in a dangerous condition. We worried about him for the rest of the night. Uddhava’s eyes were also hurt.

The attack devastated us. The front hall was a mess. Then almost as soon as the police left, we started hearing threats yelled at us in the night that this wasn’t the end, that they would be back. None of us could sleep after that. The devotees were certain we would be attacked again, perhaps even that night.

We wanted to protect ourselves. The police certainly offered no protection. Some of the women thought we should leave. We called Brahmānanda in New York to get his opinion because he was a strong man. Suddenly we heard a “thump-thump” as something hit the back of the house. I ran out back and saw that someone had thrown two Molotov cocktails against the wall of the house. The yard was in flames. I ran out with the hose and some blankets and put out the fire, but it was an overwhelming feeling to see that the gang wasn’t going to quit. They wanted to retaliate for their men who had been arrested. After those Molotov cocktails, however, there were no more attacks that night.

By morning, the devotees who had gone with the police to file a report returned. From them we received a report about what the hoodlums were saying in their cells: they had big plans about what they were going to do to us when they got out of jail. They all had brothers with guns, and they planned to come back and shoot the place up.

Later a policeman came by and told us that the gang leader had survived the night at the hospital. I remember that the policeman was sympathetic toward us, but he was even more sympathetic toward this gang leader because he was a Vietnam veteran who had been injured in the war. They couldn’t understand how we could have stabbed him.

Because we were afraid of further attack, Brahmānanda agreed to come to Boston with some of the men from New York. A few hours later, Ṛṣi Kumāra, Candanācārya, Gargamuni, and some others were swaggering around our house, ready to take care of anyone who came in. We boarded up all the windows and tried to build a fortress. Different people came by and gave their opinions. Even Mr. MacDonald, our real estate agent, came by. He encouraged us and said he thought we had gotten the best of them. I remember him saying, “I’ll put my money on Murphy,” his name for Murāri. Somehow it captured his imagination how Murāri had thrown one of the gang members through the window. He also advised us to get a shotgun. He told us not to be afraid. “Just go up on the roof, and when they come, point it down at them, close your eyes if you don’t think you can do it, and pull the trigger.”

We tried to go about our business as usual that day, but we were still in too much shock. I remember, though, that we even went out on *harināma* in Harvard Square. It was too much. Every time we saw someone who even resembled one of the gang members, the anxiety would creep over us again.

Then when we returned to the temple, the phone rang and a young man asked, “I’d like to know about your movement.” I knew immediately that this was not a real inquiry just by the voice. He was particularly interested in how many people lived at the temple. Knowing who I was talking to, I said, “Usually there’s about thirty people, but we just invited our friends up from New York and now there’s about fifty men.”

“Really. That many?”

That frightened me too. Then one of the gang member’s older brothers came by with his big dogs. He told us to be careful because something was definitely going to happen. They were going to attack that night and now they had guns. We called the police and asked them to stay with us during the night, but they refused, although they agreed to come by and check on us.

The devotees met and made some decisions: we would send the Deities to New York. The women and children would also go. The only devotees who should stay were the ones who were willing to fight. Otherwise, the temple should be abandoned. Everyone had opinions. Some devotees were ready to close the temple altogether and move the press to New York. In the end, however, we loaded the Deities and the women, and for some reason, I decided to go too. I don’t remember what reason I gave, but we were all in a strange state in our shock. Girirāja would also accompany us. Murāri, our hero and the best fighter, had gone off to a corner and slept all day. We were all afraid.

As Girirāja and I were driving out of Boston, he asked me, very much in the mood of a junior, whether I thought it was right that I should leave. He wasn’t challenging me, but immediately I thought, “Wait a minute, why am I going? I’m the temple president and I should be back there although I’m not a fighter. If I go, it may be out of cowardice. No matter what happens, even if I don’t do well in the fight tonight or if something happens to me, it’s more important that I stay.” I decided to go back. Girirāja turned the car just before the toll booth and we returned to the temple. The others were definitely cheered to see us back and they all agreed that it was proper that I stay.

We were ready, but nothing happened that night. We manned all the ramparts and set up watches, and at one point the police came by. I was on duty, so I came out with my two-by-four. “What the hell is that? What are you doing with that?!” I told them we were on guard. Although they told me I shouldn’t be carrying it around, they were sympathetic. Finally, around two or three in the morning, a car pulled up with some gang members. They started walking toward the temple, but just then the police car came by again. The policemen approached them, but of course they hadn’t caught them breaking any laws. Still, they effectively stopped their attack.

Because the first group of attackers were in jail, we had to go to court to testify. Brahmānanda and I wrote letters explaining the events to Prabhupāda. My letter was probably more fearful, trying to impress Prabhupāda with the seriousness of the attack, how Uddhava was injured and how we had sent the Deities and the women to New York. Probably I asked him whether he thought we should move again. Brahmānanda’s letter, however, was more objective and conservative, although he addressed the same serious points and asked Prabhupāda’s advice about what we should do. After a few nights of no further attack, we sent a telegram to Prabhupāda saying it seemed as if these attacks weren’t going to be a constant threat.

Prabhupāda’s reply was sober. He wanted to know the results. One result was that due to our insecurity after the attacks, we played a copy of the Hare Kṛṣṇa record in the large empty rooms during the long autumn weeks that followed. Together the devotees listened to Prabhupāda’s *praṇati* on the flip side over and over until it became our theme song, each line drawn out. We finally surrendered our vulnerability to Prabhupāda and felt that he was with us. And Prabhupāda assures me in this letter that we should “continue your proceeding, chanting always Hare Krishna. He will always give us protection.”

The attack raised philosophical questions in most of the devotees’ minds and we struggled to come up with an understanding that would soothe the contradiction many devotees were feeling. We were Kṛṣṇa’s devotees. How could this have happened to us? I remember realizing that Kṛṣṇa’s protection didn’t mean that we would never be attacked or lose a battle or even be killed. The Pāṇḍavas didn’t win every battle at Kurukṣetra and they appeared defeated when they were banished for thirteen years. In the end, however, Kṛṣṇa’s devotees are never vanquished. But what could that mean? In our own situation, we saw that if the gang members had killed some of us, we would have been defeated by the demons. That wasn’t supposed to happen. The incident punctured our immature concept of the meaning of Kṛṣṇa’s protection.

It also penetrated our intoxication with what we had been thinking of paradise. Here we were living in the biggest temple in ISKCON. We were elated and we considered the temple akin to the spiritual world. The attacks made us see things differently. Now the size of the house frightened us because we felt too vulnerable. We realized we were living in a real neighborhood, not in Vaikuṇṭha.

We also learned that devotees can’t always do everything they want simply because they are devotees. That is not what Kṛṣṇa’s protection means. Was it that devotees could be subdued, but not defeated? We had no idea. If anyone thought that by joining the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement they could very literally avoid misery and suffering, they soon found out that this wasn’t true.

We realized we would have to be tolerant, not only with ourselves and our immature understanding, but with the people to whom we were preaching. Prabhupāda said that if you’re going to preach, you have to tolerate like Jesus Christ, Lord Nityānanda, and Haridāsa Ṭhākura. Those great devotees underwent so many troubles. We should expect to undergo trouble too. Prabhupāda even promised us that if we went out preaching, we could expect trouble. This was our first real dose of it. Gradually, the contradictions reconciled themselves and although we remained fearful, we didn’t become resentful. We lost our naivete and became grateful to meet people who were interested in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Therefore, our preaching spirit actually increased after the attacks, by Prabhupāda’s grace.

Although there were no further attacks, the ordeal wasn’t over. Because some of the gang members had been arrested, we had to go to court. As the court date approached, people began approaching us warning us not to prosecute. I also thought that was best and phoned the police to drop the charges, but they refused because they said it wasn’t the devotees who were prosecuting, but the state. Brahmānanda agreed to stay with us until the court case was over because we were so demoralized.

Going to court over the attack was the first time I had ever attended a trial. Both sides had lawyers. We hadn’t done much advance preparation for the trial—we had met only briefly with our court-assigned lawyer. The gang members, however, had obviously gone over the case with their own lawyer. I remember glimpses of different people on the stand—Nanda-kiśora, Murāri. Brahmānanda was impressed with Nanda-kiśora’s testimony because he managed to preach. Later, Brahmānanda praised him in a letter to Prabhupāda and Prabhupāda wrote back that Nanda-kiśora was just a young boy, but he spoke so wonderfully. That must mean that Kṛṣṇa had enabled him to speak (Letter, September 19, 1969). It was inspiring for everyone to hear.

I remember when Murāri spoke, the defense lawyer exposed his belligerence. He asked, “If someone comes to the temple again and attacks you, you will kill them. Is this true?” Murāri broke into a smile and said, “No, that’s not our philosophy. We just want to give people *prasādam.*” I still remember his smile. It was what you call in the movies a “killer smile.” He looked as if he *could* kill someone.

Then I took the stand and the lawyer cross-examined me. He asked me how long we had been living in the house and what was the legal nature of our purchase. He tried to make it look as if we were squatters and had no right to be outraged by the attack. He was surprised when I told him we had bought the house and had signed the deed only two days before. This visibly shook him and he backed away. It was obvious that a large part of his defense was centered on us not being homeowners.

Then he asked me to describe what happened. I described with some emotion how I had seen these gang members attacking the devotees. I emphasized that there were women and children in the building, and I gave testimony as to how we were attacked. The lawyer also pointed to the man with the broken thumb and asked me if I realized that by our attacking them, this man had suffered this injury. I looked over at him—it was the first time I had seen what he really looked like—and it was hard to feel any sympathy.

Then they called the leader of the gang to the stand. Although he was dressed in a suit, he still looked ignorant, like an uneducated tough. At least that’s how we saw him. Others probably saw him as an average local, hometown hero. His lawyer pointed out that he had served in such and such corps in Vietnam before he asked him to relate his version of what had happened. The gang member said, “I was walking by the building and some of these people were sitting on the porch. One of them called me a Vietnam pig.” I couldn’t believe my ears.

“And then what happened?”

“Well, some of them came out, grabbed me, and pulled me into the house.”

It was just the kind of thing we would expect an audience to hear and call out, “Shame! Shame! Liar! Liar!” It stuck in my throat, but I didn’t say it. I couldn’t do that in court. He told a fantastic story and portrayed us as anti-Vietnam hippies. His story contradicted everything we had said.

I was naive. I had no idea that such lying goes on regularly in court. The lawyer reinforced to the judge that this poor Vietnam veteran was telling the truth, and that our story was wrong. This was not a jury trial, and eventually, the judge suspended judgment. He added, however, that if there were another incident within the next six months, any disturbance to us at all, then the case would be reopened and the charges reconsidered.

I was happy with the decision. If the gang members had been found guilty and sentenced to jail, other gang members might have retaliated. Still, we were annoyed at how they had lied, and disappointed, bewildered, and outraged by what had happened. Brahmānanda considered the case closed and went back to New York. Prabhupāda approved the judge’s decision.

It is interesting to note how prophetic Prabhupāda’s words were about not wanting us to be involved in anti-Vietnam demonstrations and peace rallies. In the early days in New York City, we attached ourselves to antiwar rallies and to anti-Vietnam demonstrations. We reconsidered our participation in such events when after attending a rally, a right-wing group had threatened us. When we returned to 26 Second Avenue, we discussed the situation at an *iṣṭa-goṣṭhī* and decided not to attend only peace rallies, but to give equal time to any event. This lawyer’s defense of the gang leader showed us clearly how people eulogized war veterans. It could have been damaging to us if we had been attending antiwar demonstrations and if people had identified us with such politics. We didn’t attend any such rallies in Boston—except once. After the students were shot at Kent State, there was a spontaneous protest meeting in Cambridge and we decided to go there to chant. The protesters didn’t appreciate us being there because we made a lot of noise while they were trying to make speeches.

Tittenhurst  
14th September, 1969  
My Dear Satsvarupa,  
Please accept my blessings. I am in due receipt of your letter dated September 9th, 1969, addressed to the Hamburg center. Now I have come to England. Our temple here is not yet finished, so I am staying in the house of Mr. John Lennon. He is very much kind upon us. Here also there is a nice big hall, exactly suitable for a temple, and the devotees are enjoying the opportunity by chanting there twice, thrice daily. I have also begun to give lectures here on specific days, but there are no outsiders coming. I have arrived here on the 11th September at about 2:30, and the devotees arranged for a very nice reception in the airport. There was chanting, press interviews, and nice press reports have come out with excellent pictures. Some of them are enclosed herewith for your reference. The first meeting organized by the devotees here will be held in the Town Hall tomorrow in the evening. So let us see how we can establish Krishna Consciousness in this part of the world.  
Regarding your judgment in court, it appears that the judge is a learned man, and he has given the right judgment, being graced by Krishna. We have to work sincerely, and because Krishna is in everyone’s heart, there will be no difficulty to have the necessary facilities. I hope by this time you have received the three *Krishna* tapes that were sent to you from Germany, and there will be more coming soon. I am enclosing a picture of the Los Angeles throne which Murari may hold as the standard for constructing the throne there. The throne should be 10 x 40 inches square, and 45 inches up to the canopy. Above that, up to the dome, it is another 12 inches. There should be three pillars as shown on the photograph, and they should be silver colored or silver-plated.  
I am pleased that the press arrangement is proceeding nicely, and you will be pleased to know that my Guru Maharaja drew a picture in which He gave great importance to the symbolic representation of the press next to the mridunga. Press means publication of various types of books and literatures and the mridunga means Samkirtan Party. So now your center will have both facilities, and organize in such a way that you will become the living example to the other centers. When the press is fixed up, I shall go to Boston and see how things are being done personally. I hope you will accept this program. Regarding the house, as you are consulting with lawyers, perhaps you can also take advice from Giriraj’s father, who is also a lawyer.  
Please offer my blessings to the others. I hope this will meet you in good health.  
Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami  
P.S. I am in due receipt of Giriraj’s letter, sent along with $30 for the maintenance fund. Please thank him for this.

Although young people today have practically never heard of the Beatles, “Beatlemania” was a worldwide phenomenon in the 1960s and 1970s. Therefore, it was exciting to hear that Prabhupāda was staying with John Lennon. The devotees in Los Angeles even circulated a pamphlet advertising that Prabhupāda was giving *bhakti-yoga* lessons to the Beatles, although when Prabhupāda saw it, he told them to withdraw it since it wasn’t true. He didn’t want the devotees to fabricate anything about his relationship with the Beatles.

We also received a tape in the mail—not a studio recording, but a *kīrtana* in which George Harrison was leading the devotees. It was wonderful to have that tape. I remember telling the Sunday feast congregation—mostly young people—that Prabhupāda was staying with the Beatles and as a special treat, I would play this tape of George Harrison leading *kīrtana.* It was great to capture their interest with such news. They all listened as I played the tape and it was a hit.

Later we understood that Prabhupāda didn’t like staying at John Lennon’s house. His comment, “No outsiders are coming,” is an indication that Prabhupāda didn’t lose his bearings while he was staying there. He wasn’t overcome by their wealth or their fame. He also didn’t like to live with people who were engaged in such illicit activities, and he didn’t like their attitude toward the devotees. It seemed as if John Lennon was hiring them to work. They gave Prabhupāda the servant’s quarters, which were not the best accommodations, instead of a place in the main house. Eventually, the devotees managed to prepare their new temple at Bury Place and Prabhupāda moved back to London.

Prabhupāda accompanied his letter with clippings from the newspaper. He was obviously pleased with the reception he had received from the press, although the European press and the American press had a similar estimation of the devotees. Prabhupāda saw such newspaper clippings as good preaching tools. When he had received his first big press coverage upon his arrival in San Francisco (“Swami in Hippie Land”), Prabhupāda had instructed us to make a thousand copies for distribution. This time he arranged that himself, but he sent us one copy to relish just as a father would share his success with his children.

The newspaper clipping showed an unusually large photo of Prabhupāda playing *karatālas* upon his arrival. Although the newspaper did such a large spread, however, there were no follow-up visits from the press. The article had some tongue-in-cheek humor, some sarcasm, a collection of mundane comments, and not much more. The only saving grace was that they had quoted some of the things he had said.

It was interesting to get this letter and to hear from Prabhupāda about his attempt to preach in another part of the world. It sounded almost exotic to us because we were so caught up in our affairs in Boston. Our main meditation upon reading his letter was when he would come to Boston. We weren’t absorbed in the idea that Kṛṣṇa consciousness should be spread all over the world, although we were aware that this was Prabhupāda’s mission. We mostly concentrated on our own little corner of that mission. And we had just finished dealing with the court case. Prabhupāda mentions in this letter that he thought the judge had made an intelligent decision. Then he says, “We have to work sincerely, and because Krishna is in everyone’s heart, there will be no difficulty to have the necessary facilities.”

I remember being uncertain what he meant by that. Did he mean that Kṛṣṇa was in the judge’s heart and therefore we received a good decision? It could also be taken more generally. Reading these letters again, I can see that Prabhupāda had already responded to our anxious letters and telegram during the attack. As far as he’s concerned, the situation is under control and he doesn’t continue to dwell on it. Still, each sentence is full of meaning and implication. A pure devotee has such deep realizations of Kṛṣṇa consciousness, of who is Kṛṣṇa, of how Kṛṣṇa is all-pervading, and of how He works in the world. Only Kṛṣṇa and His devotees know the details of such things. Prabhupāda kindly shared his realizations with us. We felt that according to our faith and submission, his realizations could be ours. We could adopt his sentences, start to parrot them, and gradually develop our faith. “You have to work sincerely.” “Kṛṣṇa is in everyone’s heart, there will be no difficulty to have the necessary facilities.” Eventually, we started to see the world in this way. We realized that there was nothing to worry about because Kṛṣṇa would give us whatever we needed—both the house and safety in the neighborhood. Everything is under His control. This is the profound realization of a *paramahaṁsa,* but Prabhupāda didn’t keep such realizations to himself. He shared them with us liberally.

We also realized that we had to put Prabhupāda’s words into practice. All of us had to gradually allow the tension we felt to seep out of us. I memorized the police station’s emergency call number. Sometimes when I was asleep, I would remember the number and fantasize calling them whenever I heard an unusual noise. That was on the surface, however. On a deeper level, even when the gang members threw rocks at the temple or yelled out at us, we would discuss it at *prasādam* and remember Prabhupāda’s words: Kṛṣṇa was protecting us, not the police.

Devotees should meditate on Prabhupāda’s instructions in this letter: that the Supersoul is in everyone’s heart and that Kṛṣṇa arranges things ultimately to help His devotees. We can understand this theoretically, we can even repeat what he said, but understanding deeply how the Supersoul acts through other people in the devotees’ favor is difficult. Devotees who go out on book distribution think about this a lot. When they approach someone to sell a book, the Supersoul dictates to the person whether or not to buy it. In our attempt to apply this instruction, however, we should be careful not to become sentimental or even fanatical, thinking that the Supersoul is directing us to do so many things. Best to check with our spiritual authorities.

Prabhupāda didn’t present us with a map of how the Supersoul would act. Neither did he define what “favorable for the devotee” meant. Therefore, in an immature attempt to apply Prabhupāda’s teachings, we can run the risk of misusing his words or taking them cheaply. It’s one thing to see that the Supersoul turns people away from our attempts to distribute books, but we have strong, worldly conditioning. In our immaturity at that time, we started looking at everyone and thinking, “Now Kṛṣṇa is making him do this. Now Kṛṣṇa is making her do that.” Where does it end? We might start to see positive things as Kṛṣṇa’s mercy, but how should we understand the meaning behind bad things? If the fuel man forgot to charge us the right amount on a bill and we got an unexpected discount, we would see that as Kṛṣṇa’s arrangement, but if the local gang broke into the temple and started beating up the devotees, we wouldn’t know what to think because it didn’t fit our “philosophy.”

Therefore, we had to reach a deeper assurance that Kṛṣṇa and guru were protecting us. Kṛṣṇa’s protection may be inconceivable, but guru’s protection is that he shares his realization with us and inspires us with faith and knowledge. Being blessed with faith and knowledge didn’t mean we could imitate Prabhupāda’s realization, but we could take solace in it. Prabhupāda wanted us to understand his words and to apply them according to our realization, not to imitate his realization. We were fortunate to receive his blessings and to go on with our spiritual lives, even though we couldn’t immediately perceive the depth of what he was saying.

There were other forms of immaturity that eventually had to be rooted out. Some devotees felt they were better than everyone else simply because they were devotees. In most cases, it took the form that they had the right to live on charity because we were serving all the people of Boston. This was an excessive estimation, although it was innocent and done with a service attitude in mind. After all, the people in Boston were suffering for want of Kṛṣṇa consciousness. We wanted to give them relief.

Most of the devotees in Boston were young *gṛhasthas,* many newly married, and most still in the “honeymoon” stage. They felt as if they were above the modes of material nature since they had such nice wives who were practicing Kṛṣṇa consciousness with them. Still, they knew that being married meant that they must still have lust in their hearts and they wondered how that lust would disappear. Often, they would become disillusioned in their relationships, or unsteady, because they didn’t know how to find their honest place in Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

Despite all that, however, we had a wonderfully spirited temple community. The devotees had a good preaching spirit toward the people who visited the temple and Boston temple became an exciting, happening place. Spiritual seekers who visited could sense the positive energy. Devotees were performing so many services, the press was going on, and all these nice men and women were happy. People were looking for energy and we certainly had energy. We were completely confident that Prabhupāda was the best guru and that everyone else was bogus. We were also convinced that spiritual life meant chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa.

Our temple room was beautiful. When the press moved to Boston, we had decided to set up the temple room on the second floor. Altars were becoming more common in ISKCON as devotees with carpentry skills joined. After we installed our small Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa Deities, Deity worship became more prominent. The beginning of the Deity worship created a conflict between the press devotees and the others, however, because the press devotees felt they didn’t have to attend the temple functions. I wasn’t directly involved in the workings of the machines—I was the editor—but I was also the temple president. I wanted all the devotees to take part more in the brahminical activities of *saṅkīrtana* and temple worship.

Advaita took a stand that all we had to do to please Prabhupāda was to print his books. He began to keep the presses running twenty-four hours a day. Although we didn’t mind the noise—it was actually pleasing to us—I didn’t like the fact that he thought he didn’t have to attend *āratis* and classes. For some of us, approaching the Deity in the new temple room with the beautiful altar was wonderful; others behaved as if the Deities weren’t even there and that the press was their deity. Eventually, I wrote to Prabhupāda about the problem and he gave Advaita permission not to go to the *āratis.*

The strain wasn’t only caused by his unwillingness to attend the *āratis.* While I was insisting that he and the other workers attend the *āratis,* he wanted me to become more involved in press management. But I couldn’t. There were so many technical and financial details to oversee—we had to understand what the press was capable of producing, how much it would cost, and how it compared with other presses. I wasn’t good at it, especially when I couldn’t give it my full attention. As soon as I came home from work, Advaita would be there, ready to present me with all the details and await my assessment. I couldn’t properly handle it all along with my job and the temple management.

It was both a difficult time and a wonderful time. The press pushed Boston into a new phase of existence. We had already been in Boston for two years, struggling to preach, going out on the streets, lecturing at the universities and churches, but we had had little response. Suddenly, the temple skyrocketed. We had a big property, a big project, still not so many new devotees joining, but devotees from other places moving in. Our Sunday feast became a big event.

Prabhupāda writes, “I hope you will accept this program,” and he tells how his spiritual master drew a picture showing the *bṛhat-mṛdaṅga.* He said that in Boston, we had both the *mṛdaṅga* of going out on *saṅkīrtana* and the *mṛdaṅga* of the press.

Of course, by “program” he also meant his plan to visit Boston after the press began its operations. Prabhupāda’s disciples demanded his attention in a variety of ways, but everyone wanted him to visit their temple. Of course, it wasn’t possible for Prabhupāda to visit everywhere at the same time, so he would tell his disciples what was possible for him and hope that we would accept it peacefully and continue our services until he arrived. I saw it as his gentle way of asking us to not be unreasonably demanding. He also left room for us to state our reasons, if we had any, why he should come sooner than he planned. For example, Prabhupāda and I had exchanged letters about the increasing unrest among the students in Boston. Immediately Prabhupāda offered to come and stay for several months if they were interested in hearing from him. If we could present him with a good reason to come, he would change his plans. Otherwise, we had to be patient.

Boston was definitely on Prabhupāda’s mind during this time. He mentioned in a letter to Tamāla Krishna that he wanted to concentrate on establishing Boston because we had just bought a house and were setting up the press. He also told Tamāla Krishna that he wanted him to start a press in Los Angeles. He spoke almost as if presses could be started in many different centers, but it was hardly something other centers could undertake. Almost all the devotees with any skills in production had already been sent to Boston.

The whole temple was caught up in the intoxication of growth. I remember Advaita coming to my room to show me the first photos he had run off and to ask what I thought of them. I also wrote his questions down and sent them to Prabhupāda. Most of the devotees there were new both in Kṛṣṇa consciousness and in their services and it took time for us to build confidence that we could really print Prabhupāda’s books. As our confidence increased, life became even more exciting. The devotees shared their printing successes with other devotees and everyone came to appreciate both their dedication and their abilities.

I was happy. I was in charge of the artists. They had one wing of the building on the second floor, facing the street, that was shaped like a tower. Four or five artists painted and worked together in that room, especially Jadurāṇī and Bharadvāja, who exchanged painting techniques. Bharadvāja was quite talented in his use of colors and form. I would be in my room looking at Advaita’s proofs, then wander down to the art department and see the latest paintings of Kṛṣṇa. When Jayādvaita moved in, he would come up and talk about something or other with me. There was so much happening and so many devotees. The lights were always on, guests were always coming to hear the philosophy, and we were thrilled to be part of it.

By this time, I had also taken over editorship of BTG in partnership with Hayagrīva. Between us, we would select the materials for the issues and each of us took charge of contributing to half the magazine. That usually meant he would write an article for his half and I would write an article for mine. Then we had Prabhupāda’s edited essay and perhaps I would try to arrange for a photo article on *harināma*. I wasn’t a photographer or an artist, but I would ask other devotees to do this work. We printed only one issue at ISKCON Press. After that, I did all the printing in Japan. I was looking for a better price and the press wasn’t steady enough yet to take on a monthly publication.

My greatest pleasure in Boston remained the typing and editing. I loved to type and edit Prabhupāda’s writing, and it was such a good way to learn the philosophy. Prabhupāda dovetailed my propensity for reading literature by giving me his own books to read. By working on BTG, I fulfilled my desire to write, which was a deep part of me long before I came to Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

At the end of the letter, Prabhupāda mentions that Girirāja sent money. Girirāja did *saṅkīrtana,* and he sent this $30 to Prabhupāda as our contribution to Prabhupāda’s maintenance fund. Prabhupāda taxed the temples to pay for his travels. Not all the centers could always send it, but he would always acknowledge it if we did send it and would thank us for whatever small amount it may have been.

## 10: October 1969

Tittenhurst  
15th October, 1969  
My Dear Satsvarupa,  
Please accept my blessings. I beg to acknowledge receipt of your letter dated October 10, 1969, with the deeds enclosed. I do not understand the technical language, but I can follow that there is a property in the name of the society. Yes, Krishna is the Proprietor of not only this property, but all the properties of all the universes. He is the Supreme Enjoyer of everything, and He is the most Beloved, Sincere Friend of everybody. That is a fact. People do not know it or they have forgotten it, and our Krishna Consciousness Movement is trying to understand this philosophy and make others understand it. So you are a sincere devotee of Krishna and He has given you a great opportunity of service, perhaps #1 in all the centers. You are the only pioneer center who possess your own property and press. I am so glad to learn that things are going on nicely and Krishna is giving you good income also. Please therefore manage things very nicely, both husband and wife and other assistants. You are also very fortunate to have an assistant like Giriraj. This boy has so quickly taken up the Krishna Consciousness cause and I am very much pleased with his behavior. Now Advaita should be assisted by some other intelligent man. Jadurany should be given sufficient space for her painting work, assisted by other artists. And Giriraj is the right leader of Samkirtan Party. I am sorry that Murari has left. Pray to Krishna that he may come back again soon. Yes, this Uher tape recorder is very nice. It can act both as tape recorder and dictaphone also. It has got exactly the same speed for direct typewriting, but because you have not got a tape recorder that plays at this dictaphone speed, we send you higher speed tapes.  
In BTG the *rasa leela* episode cannot be published. We are writing on the activities of Krishna and *rasa leela* is one of the most important Pastimes of His transcendental activities. Therefore it must be published in the book, but it cannot be published in any public paper. That is the instruction of my Guru Maharaja. Actually, *rasa leela* means to curb down the lusty propensities of the conditioned soul. Unfortunately, it acts differently on the conditioned soul if he is not prepared to understand what is Krishna. So do not try to print this.  
Yes, I am very much anxious to go to Boston as soon as possible, but at the same time I want to see London center well established. Since I have come here Krishna has given us our permanent place which was in dispute at 7, Bury Place. It is very well situated, and perhaps London center will also come out very successful in the near future. The Hare Krishna record is going on in England nicely, and I heard that in Australia it stands 4th on the list of 50 important records.  
I have heard also from Brahmananda that he plans to spend 3 days per week at Boston, but I have also heard that during his absence 3 devotees have left the temple. So things should be managed so nicely that our devotees may not leave us. We get a devotee after great endeavor, and we must train such devotee in a nice way so that he may not go back and be at large in the clutches of Maya. I think I shall visit Boston in December surely, if not in November, and I shall let you know the exact time and date. As you are talking of myself that I am your only shelter, similarly I am always thinking that you all boys and girls are only my hopes. When I was first in Boston in the same Commonwealth Port I was thinking how I shall be able to establish my mission in this country. Now, by Krishna’s Grace, the time has come when I see Boston is the first center and in Boston we have got so many nice devotees.  
Please offer my blessings to all the others. I hope this will meet you in good health.  
Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami  
P.S. Ask Dayananda to write me. He is so nice boy and I want to hear from him. Recently I got one letter from Nandarani. She is so nice as her husband.

In a previous letter, Prabhupāda asked me about the deed for the new house. I wrote to him and enclosed a copy of it. We were pleased and proud that it had finally come through. Prabhupāda didn’t only want to look at the deed, he wanted to keep it on file. After all, this was his property. In the space marked “owner,” I had written in “Kṛṣṇa.” I not only wrote it in, but I pointed it out to Prabhupāda. We all felt good about calling Kṛṣṇa the proprietor of this house. We had just begun to understand how present Kṛṣṇa was in our lives and how actively He moved on His devotees’ behalf.

Prabhupāda again replied in a sober way: “Yes, Krishna is the Proprietor of not only this property, but all the properties of all the universes. He is the Supreme Enjoyer of everything, and He is the most Beloved, Sincere Friend of everybody. That is a fact.” He exposed my knowledge as theoretical by expressing his own realization. God owns all of the houses on the block, although people claim them as their own. Receiving this letter made us more conscious of this point.

I also became aware that I had missed Prabhupāda’s point by pressing my own. Prabhupāda wasn’t joking. He took Kṛṣṇa’s proprietorship very seriously. Therefore, he paraphrased *Bhagavad-gītā* [5.29](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/5.29), *bhoktāraṁ yajña-tapasāṁ, sarva-loka-maheśvaram:* Kṛṣṇa is the supreme proprietor of all the planets, the supreme enjoyer, and the supreme friend.

Prabhupāda was guru, heavy, and as disciples, we tended to be light. If we joked with him in a lighthearted way, he would speak the Absolute Truth and force us to become silent and serious. Therefore, it is better to approach the spiritual master in a serious and sober mood. Sobriety shouldn’t smother our affection, however. The relationship between guru and disciple is similar to the relationship between father and son. The disciple is allowed to be himself, to say something light or something perhaps not steeped in *siddhānta* provided that when the spiritual master brings the discussion to the absolute level, the disciple becomes attentive to what the spiritual master says. That balance of heavy and light adds sweetness to the relationship. In effect, in a childlike way, I said, “Look, Prabhupāda,” and Prabhupāda as my father responded with affection, “Yes, my dear son, but actually you should know that Kṛṣṇa is the owner of everything.” This balance of light and heavy can set the stage for a regular exchange between guru and disciple.

Neither is there any harm in being corrected by the spiritual master. When we spoke to Prabhupāda, it immediately became obvious that we were speaking to someone grave, to a pure devotee who knew Kṛṣṇa. Still, we couldn’t immediately change ourselves and take on the gravity of Mahārāja Parīkṣit as he questioned Śukadeva Gosvāmī. We were who we were and Prabhupāda accepted that. When Prabhupāda gave *Bhāgavatam* lectures, for example, we would listen and be pleased. When the lecture was over, we would revert back to our absorption in the immediate. At least now “the immediate” meant service in his mission.

We didn’t expect to associate with Prabhupāda as equals. Sometimes we asked Prabhupāda questions and he would not feel obliged to answer our question directly. Sometimes he jumped off the question into what he wanted to say to us. Because we recognized that Prabhupāda was a pure devotee, we thought of him as a different category of being. He wasn’t an ordinary man who was obliged to respond to us on our level. Even externally, he was so much older than we were, and he came from a completely different culture. If he changed the nature of a conversation, we always listened respectfully and tried to follow his mood.

Few devotees can imitate Prabhupāda’s power in these ways. It also seems that devotees are more demanding now, that what they are saying is important and that the spiritual master must speak on their level in order to answer them. The spiritual master may say what is on his mind, but the disciple often comes back and says, “Yes, but that’s not what I meant.” We did not behave in this way with Prabhupāda; neither did Prabhupāda feel obliged to respond to us if we did.

Even though we cannot imitate Prabhupāda, it is important for devotees who have accepted senior devotees as their spiritual master to understand this principle and to allow the spiritual master to function as guru. If they treat him as an ordinary man, then the basis of affection and surrender cannot form itself properly in the relationship. Even if someone is close to the spiritual master’s age and level of spiritual advancement, a disciple should not question the difference between him and his spiritual master. Rather, the disciple has to give the relationship permission to take place according to the classical model.

What is the difference between Mahārāja Parīkṣit and Śukadeva Gosvāmī? Mahārāja Parīkṣit accepted the role of disciple and Śukadeva Gosvāmī accepted the role of guru. If we accept the role of disciple, we should play our role sincerely and follow the rules for disciples: inquire submissively and sincerely render service. We should be appreciative of our spiritual master’s teachings. That is how the disciple honors the relationship and the only way in which he can hope to benefit by it. If the disciple doesn’t have enough faith to accept his position relative to the guru’s position, then why accept the guru as spiritual master?

Sometimes devotees wonder about this point. They want to find a balance between natural formality and a personal relationship with the spiritual master. Can we establish a personal relationship with the spiritual master by asking him to work from our own frame of reference? Can we ask him to joke with us as we joke with our friends? A manager has to reasonably satisfy the devotees’ minds. What about the guru? Does he also have to satisfy everyone’s mind?

The disciple’s position is to submit. Submission includes respect. The more we respect the guru, the more possible it is to submit. Prabhupāda didn’t satisfy our whims or even always answer our questions. The disciple does not have the right to demand this of his guru, nor does he have the right to demand that the guru approach him through his own frame of reference. If that demand is placed upon the guru, the relationship becomes materially tainted. Prabhupāda said that there are no material solutions. We simply want to learn how to be mad after Kṛṣṇa. The guru can say nothing more than that.

Whether we understood Prabhupāda’s absolute position intellectually or not, we did know that Prabhupāda gave us unlimited opportunities to serve. In this letter he writes, “\[Kṛṣṇa\] has given you a great opportunity of service, perhaps #1 in all the centers. You are the only pioneer center who possess your own property and press.” Service to the spiritual master is not drudgery. It is our lifeblood. Prabhupāda calls it a “great opportunity.” He also uses the word “sincerity,” a word he often uses to describe the main qualification for success in devotional service. Sincerity means simplicity and honesty, unpretentiousness. Sincerity refers to what we actually feel, that we have no other motive but to act on what we feel.

Although we can’t always say we want to serve Kṛṣṇa and guru, such service has to remain the heartfelt desire of a sincere devotee. Sometimes the tests we experience in Kṛṣṇa consciousness are meant to help us see the parallel desires we may be feeling—the desire to serve Kṛṣṇa and the desire to serve our senses. A sincere devotee always ultimately surrenders the sense desires and shows Kṛṣṇa that he wants to serve Him. If Kṛṣṇa sees that we want to serve Him, He provides us with opportunities in this world and, ultimately, in the spiritual world.

Prabhupāda gave us—and is giving us—the great opportunity to be pioneers in the spreading of Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Often the word “pioneer” conjures up the image of leaving our home country and going to open a center in a different part of the world. However, Prabhupāda also encouraged us to pioneer among our own people. Therefore, he made those of us who were pioneering among the student population in Boston as much a part of the pioneering movement as those who went to Germany and London or later Japan and Africa. We always felt that Prabhupāda was with us and that we were trying to accomplish what he asked of us. We could only pray to be successful.

No one could sit down and relax. We were so caught up in the immediate preaching that we had no time to reflect on our relative position among the other temples or what the nondevotees would think of us later. To serve Prabhupāda, we were forced to live in the present. Did we have enough money to pay the bills? If another householder couple came, where would they stay? How could we pacify so-and-so’s wife? Should the press members go to *ārati*? What should we do about *saṅkīrtana* and the police? We hardly had time to think. When Prabhupāda’s letters came, we would feel the thrill of his pleasure and then immerse ourselves back into the problems. Such a life thoroughly engaged our minds and senses in Prabhupāda’s service, and we left it to him to define whether it was pleasing or not and to encourage us.
