Prabhupāda mentions in this letter that he is pleased by Girirāja’s behavior and that I am fortunate to have such an assistant. Prabhupāda refers to him as the best choice for *saṅkīrtana* leader. He was growing up in his Kṛṣṇa consciousness and taking on more of his own identity and responsibility in Prabhupāda’s movement. He was also a hard worker. I could always count on him to be steady. It wasn’t that because he was educated, he was unwilling to do menial work. He was talented in collecting money and in selling magazines, and he was willing to face the police and judges—whatever was required to help us continue the *saṅkīrtana.* He was also submissive and was able to give good advice. I’m sure Prabhupāda was responding to my appreciation of him in this letter, just as he responded to my appreciation of Murāri in a previous letter. Prabhupāda liked us to write about the other devotees, and because I was the temple president, it was natural for me to do so. He liked to hear not only what I was doing, but how the others were instrumental in preaching and how the temple was maintaining.

As Prabhupāda praises Girirāja, he also notes my mentioning that Murāri had left the temple. It is interesting, again, to note Prabhupāda’s previous sober advice that I not quit my job. After Prabhupāda wrote me about it the first time, I thought about it some more and wrote a second letter to Prabhupāda asking again whether or not I should quit my job, and in that letter, I quoted Murāri. Murāri was pushing me to quit my job because he thought that if I went out on *saṅkīrtana* and also trained the new devotees who had come to live at the temple—Murāri used the phrase, “whipping the devotees into shape” when he spoke about training and I repeated that phrase to Prabhupāda—we could make so much more money. Prabhupāda wrote back and said that Murāri can say this, but you are a more sober devotee than Murāri, so you should think it over, not just do it because he says so.

That line made me happy. I received that letter not long after we had moved into the new house. I still remember reading it on the front porch. I was aware that this line would embarrass Murāri, but it meant a lot to me. I looked at Murāri as someone who could purchase the van and get out there with the devotees. He had an aggressive nature and a strong body. I tended to defer to him somewhat, and I even looked up to him enough to quote him to Prabhupāda. But Prabhupāda wrote, *“You* are the more trusted man to me. Why are you listening to him? You’re the one who is sober.” This letter boosted my self-esteem. I didn’t take it as a criticism of Murāri, but at least it adjusted my thinking, which hadn’t been proper. I was supposed to be in charge because my spiritual master trusted me and had known me over a long period of time.

Because there was no question in those days of not showing my letters to the devotees, Murāri also saw this letter. I took care to explain that Prabhupāda did not mean anything critical of him. I didn’t want him to feel hurt. He seemed to take it all right. At the time, I didn’t notice any lack of sobriety on Murāri’s part, but by the time I got this letter, the situation was obvious. His lack of sobriety was part of the reason he had come to Boston.

When Prabhupāda made an expression, as he did in this letter, that we should pray for Murāri to come back, it was not a pious expression from a religionist’s repertoire. Prabhupāda did not make pious utterances. We took whatever he said as part of his deep realization, even it wasn’t our own realization. When Prabhupāda told us to pray for Murāri, we wondered how to do it. On some occasions upon receiving such an instruction, we would write back to Prabhupāda and ask for more specifics on how to apply his order. But there were preliminary steps, even to that. First we had to appreciate his realization as true. That required faith. Some of the instructions would make us look laughable. For example, when Prabhupāda heard that the name of the Chicago airport was O’Hare Airport, he said, “Just add the Kṛṣṇa there. Then it will be O’Har´e Kṛṣṇa Airport. Ask them to change it.”

What else could we do? We had to try and carry out the different things he said. If we were sincere, we would take his letters and try to apply his instructions regardless of how they made us look. If someone were to find us down on our knees and ask us what we were doing, we might say, “Well, my spiritual master told me to pray to Kṛṣṇa to please bring Murāri back.” Faith has to be as simple as that South Indian devotee whose guru told him to read *Bhagavad-gītā* even though he was illiterate.

There are other examples, too, of devotees trying to apply Prabhupāda’s instructions. Prabhupāda once asked Śivānanda to pray to Kṛṣṇa for him. Śivānanda wrote back, “I don’t think it will work.” Prabhupāda responded, “Yes, it will work because you are His Vaiṣṇava.”

Sometimes Prabhupāda asked us to do things that were simple, but that required more intimacy with Kṛṣṇa than we might have been experiencing. We tried to follow his order anyway in whatever way we knew how because we had the most wonderful reason to do so. Because we were Prabhupāda’s spiritual infants and didn’t know how to do whatever Prabhupāda wanted, we often had to ask Prabhupāda for further help. We were dependent. “You asked me to do something that requires a delicate and intimate knowledge of spiritual life. I don’t know *how* to pray to Kṛṣṇa. How do I pray?” Prabhupāda would then often give us further instructions.

Again, devotees nowadays cannot imitate Prabhupāda. There is pressure on the spiritual master, especially when he is conducting so many of his affairs by mail, because the disciples tend to analyze every word he says. The guru wants to say something potent and spiritual, but he knows he has to have the weight of realization behind what he says. “I will pray for you” is a phrase we wouldn’t want to say cheaply. If the spiritual master says he will pray for us, however, we cannot understand his mind. Prabhupāda sometimes said that when he prayed to Kṛṣṇa, devotees who had left would come back. “I prayed to Kṛṣṇa that such a nice boy, why has he stopped his service? Now he has come back.”

We can’t know what Prabhupāda’s prayer was. All we can know is that he used the word “prayer” to refer to what he was doing. Prabhupāda’s praying for devotees indicates that although we cannot imitate, we can share his spirit. Prabhupāda cared enough about us to pray to Kṛṣṇa for our well-being. We should care that much for others. Rather than analyzing Murāri’s lack of sincerity and dividing up his possessions—Prabhupāda didn’t even tell us to find his address and to go and see him—we should care enough to pray for him.

Prabhupāda also told us he wasn’t so surprised when one of his disciples left. Until we met Prabhupāda, we were wasting our youth, wandering around in the 1960s playing guitar and smoking marijuana. He transformed us, but sometimes our surrender wasn’t deep enough to keep us away from *māyā*.

The talk about the tape recorder was shoptalk between author and typist. I had praised the audio equipment in my letter to Prabhupāda. Here he indicates that his machine is on one speed and mine on another, and he pointed out that my machine didn’t have the same features as his.

He had been using a Grundig machine before he got the Uher. At that time, he considered my machine good. Prabhupāda always cared for and serviced his equipment. His machines meant a lot to him. He even mentioned them in *The Nectar of Devotion* as he explained the principle of *yukta-vairāgya:*

One should not give up anything which can be utilized in the service of the Lord. That is a secret of devotional service. Anything that can be utilized in advancing Krishna consciousness and devotional service should be accepted. For instance, we are using many machines for the advancement of our present Krishna consciousness movement, machines like typewriters, dictating machines, tape recorders, microphones and airplanes. Sometimes people ask us, “Why are you utilizing material products if you condemn the advancement of material civilization?” But actually we do not condemn. We simply ask people to do whatever they are doing in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. This is the same principle on which, in *Bhagavad-gita,* Kṛṣṇa advised Arjuna to utilize his fighting abilities in devotional service. Similarly, we are utilizing these machines for Kṛṣṇa’s service. With such sentiment for Kṛṣṇa, or Kṛṣṇa consciousness, we can accept everything. If the typewriter can be utilized for advancing our Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement, we must accept it. Similarly, the dictating machine or any other machine must be used. Our vision is that Kṛṣṇa is everything. Kṛṣṇa is the cause and effect, and nothing belongs to us. Kṛṣṇa’s things must be used in the service of Kṛṣṇa. That is our vision.  
—NOD, p. 115

I like to think of Prabhupāda using his dictaphone in the middle of the night to dictate the *Bhāgavatam*. It is an endearing image for me. I imagine showing someone a piece of equipment in their later years with which they are not culturally familiar, and I imagine him never becoming accustomed to it. Such a person may prefer to write with a pen than a computer. Prabhupāda came to America with an old, manual typewriter, but when he saw a dictaphone, he understood the advantage of it and switched his method of composition.

I also like to meditate on those parts of the *Bhāgavatam* Prabhupāda personally typed. They just seem so wonderful. His dictated purports are not less transcendental or literary, or less Prabhupāda, however, because Prabhupāda so thoroughly and progressively adapted to this new method of composition.

In this letter, Prabhupāda gives some specific instructions about printing the *rāsa-līlā* in BTG. Prabhupāda had recently written to Jadurāṇī that we could begin printing sections of the *Kṛṣṇa* book in BTG. In this letter, however, he writes, “In BTG the *rasa leela* episode cannot be published.” He also explains that the *rāsa-līlā* is one of the most important of Kṛṣṇa’s transcendental activities. Prabhupāda confirmed this instruction in a meeting he held with the BTG editors when he finally arrived in Boston.

There are two points to note: that the *rāsa-līlā* is important and that it shouldn’t be printed in a public paper. His point was that when discussing Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes, we shouldn’t omit any part of them. At the same time, we have to be responsible to the audience. If someone is interested enough in Kṛṣṇa consciousness to read *Kṛṣṇa* book, we can assume that he is more serious than someone who might have no interest, but who has received a magazine on the street. Such a person might glance at a page and see the picture without understanding the philosophy. Reading *Kṛṣṇa* book meant reading about all of Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes. A reader learns that He is the Supreme Personality of Godhead, hears His childhood activities, comes to understand something of the loving relationship He shares with His parents and friends, and gradually approaches the chapters discussing the *rāsa* dance. Although the *rāsa* dance is the most important and worshipable pastime and the *gopīs* are the most worshipable devotees, we should not reveal the details of this pastime to those who are ineligible to hear them. Prabhupāda said that people will take Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes as mundane sex. In a letter he wrote me on December 13, 1969, he states, “What general people will understand about Radha-Krishna Leela? Immediately they will take it as ordinary boys and girls, in spite of a thousand warnings, ‘This is not this, this is not this.’” In that same December letter, Prabhupāda outlines the different levels of Kṛṣṇa consciousness and adds, “So Radha-Krishna Leela belongs to the fourth stage of understanding, and we are publishing BTG for people in general to reestablish their forgotten relationship with Kṛṣṇa.” To this day, BTG excludes articles on *rāsa-līlā.*

That was Prabhupāda’s policy for BTG, but he did include the *rāsa-līlā* chapters in his *Kṛṣṇa* book. A Godnephew criticized Prabhupāda for being too permissive with the sacred topics. Anticipating such a charge, Prabhupāda quoted Śukadeva Gosvāmī: “Śukadeva Gosvāmī concludes this episode of *rāsa-līlā* by pointing out that if a person hears from the right source of the pastimes of Kṛṣṇa, who is Viṣṇu Himself, and the *gopīs,* who are expansions of His energy, then he will be relieved of the most dangerous type of disease, namely lust. If one actually hears *rāsa-līlā,* he will become completely freed from the lusty desire of sex life and elevated to the highest level of spiritual understanding. Generally, because they hear from Māyāvādīs and they themselves are Māyāvādīs, people become more and more implicated in sex life. The conditioned soul should hear the *rāsa-līlā* dance from an authorized spiritual master and be trained by him so that he can understand the whole situation; thus one can be elevated to the highest standard of spiritual life, otherwise one will be implicated. Material lust is a kind of heart disease, and to cure the material heart disease of the conditioned soul, it is recommended that one should hear, but not from the impersonalist rascals. If one hears from the right sources with right understanding, then his situation will be different.” *(Kṛṣṇa,* Chap. 32, pp. 222–223)

Prabhupāda sometimes excluded people as unqualified and sometimes had a more permissive spirit. In general, he decided whether or not to print information about Kṛṣṇa and the *gopīs* by measuring the audience and the nature of the publication. He definitely excluded the nondevotee or the person not interested in Kṛṣṇa consciousness from such topics and therefore didn’t allow them to be discussed in public papers.

Even a devotee practicing Kṛṣṇa consciousness had to have some qualification. “It acts differently on the conditioned soul if he is not prepared to understand what is Krishna.” We may be making an endeavor to understand Kṛṣṇa, and that may be reflected in our continual reading of *Kṛṣṇa* book, our willingness to study and serve and chant and be serious, but when we attempt to understand *rāsa-līlā* in depth, it may bring the opposite result. Prabhupāda comments here that this subject matter may have the opposite result if we are not really prepared to understand what is Kṛṣṇa.

One way to not have it act in an opposite way is to study only what Prabhupāda has given us in his various books on the topic and to be satisfied with that. Prabhupāda knew the exact dose for his disciples and it is not necessary for us to study volumes of other literature.

Prabhupāda goes on to say in this letter that he is anxious to come to Boston as soon as possible. It was exciting to contemplate Prabhupāda’s impending arrival. More devotees were joining. Bhavānanda and his wife came to Boston around this time. She was a typist and he had begun to design temples.

Bhavānanda agreed to find a place for Prabhupāda to stay. We didn’t think the temple had adequate facility, and we wanted him to have a first-class house to himself. Finally, Bhavānanda found a house in Needham, almost an hour from the temple.

Also, Prabhupāda told us that this time he didn’t want to do any outside engagements, so we didn’t arrange any for him. It was just as well because we were working hard to get the temple, the press, and the house ready for his arrival. He ended up staying less than a week, which came as a surprise to us. We had expected him to stay longer.

Prabhupāda was all-attractive. All the centers wanted him to visit them. A center came to life when Prabhupāda visited. Everyone felt purified and was willing to cooperate. Whatever sluggishness the devotees felt was overcome because Prabhupāda was a touchstone of Kṛṣṇa consciousness. It was natural for us, therefore, to always be in anxiety about Prabhupāda coming. If we had to write a letter, it meant he wasn’t with us at our center.

Prabhupāda tried to reciprocate with our requests for him to come. Now Boston had something to attract him—ISKCON Press. The most exciting part of this letter was Prabhupāda’s announcement that he would be coming soon. When Prabhupāda finally did come, devotees naturally behaved their best. Most gave up whatever grudges and unwillingness they had in order to cooperate by bringing their differences before Prabhupāda for judgment and accepting his encouragement.

Devotees felt that Prabhupāda was their only shelter. I also expressed this feeling to Prabhupāda in my letter and he responded to it by saying, “So as you are thinking of me as your only shelter, similarly I am always thinking of you all boys and girls as my only hope.”

A disciple may claim to know Kṛṣṇa, but he actually knows only what he has heard from his spiritual master. A disciple may claim to have some shelter or security other than Kṛṣṇa, but no other shelter has any meaning. Even Kṛṣṇa’s shelter is not available except through the spiritual master’s mercy. That is the reality.

Our lives as devotees were blissful, but that doesn’t mean they weren’t difficult. Our dependence on Prabhupāda was real and sustaining. By the time we moved into the new house, we had so many difficulties: difficulties with marriages, difficulties with the neighborhood, difficulties between devotees, difficulties of trying to work at a job and to satisfy a boss and at the same time, keeping our relationship intact with the temple. For me, I had to pay attention to the temple on the telephone from work and that was difficult. Finances were not easy. That was especially true in the winter because we were dependent on street collections and less money came in during the winter. We were constantly surrounded by stress and responsibility. Prabhupāda was our only shelter.

Life in the temple was sometimes rocky. It wasn’t a staid, conservative place where everything went on without upheaval day after day. For example, one day the devotees went out on *harināma*. While they were chanting, some men started to harass them. At one point, they began to scuffle with the devotees and exchange a few punches. When we finally got back to the temple, a few of them followed us and stood across the street throwing stones at the temple. What were we to do? Most of the devotees were gentle, but we had a few pugnacious ex-Marines, such as Śacīsuta. Śacīsuta ordered the thirty male devotees out onto the porch. He was sure that if the punks saw who they were dealing with, they would leave. There were only four or five of them. He was right; they did leave, and the rest of us filed back into the temple to carry on with our activities.

Suddenly, I heard loud noises and scuffling just outside the building. I ran back outside and saw a group of devotees circled around a fight. I pushed forward to see what was going on and saw Śacīsuta and Vaikuṇṭhanātha fighting. I said that we were supposed to be out facing these demons, and here we were fighting among ourselves. They were angry too. It took two men to part them.

Such things didn’t happen so often, but the fact that they happened at all shows that we lacked polish. We were often boisterous and unruly, and we did things Vaiṣṇavas don’t usually do. Our natures also came out when Prabhupāda visited, but Prabhupāda smoothed us and softened us by his presence.

Sometimes the devotees spoke harshly to each other. Advaita spoke New Yorkese and sometimes his words were unkind. He used to make Girirāja the butt of his jokes. Girirāja might ask him something in a gentle way, but Advaita and others would tease him for being talented and respected by Prabhupāda, for being intelligent, a superman. Hṛdayānanda also became the butt of their jokes when he came later.

What could Prabhupāda mean that the devotees were his only hope? I took it that Prabhupāda meant it sincerely. He expressed that same feeling a number of times over the years. Sometimes he even said that we had been sent by his spiritual master to help him and that the credit of spreading the movement was ours. He saw that he was only one person, an old man, as he described himself, without money or resources. We were printing his books and dedicating our lives to him. It’s not that a pure devotee doesn’t feel grateful to the young people who come to serve him. He doesn’t think only that he has saved them from *māyā* or that he has given them everything and that they have simply become recipients of his mercy. All that is true, but when a pure devotee sees his disciples freely giving body, mind, and words in his service, and when he sees them performing so many austerities to please him, he feels gratitude. My words to Prabhupāda were sincere, and his words back to me were sincere. “You are my hopes for spreading this movement.” Every teacher has to have such “hopes,” such followers who can carry out his orders and spread his message.

I remember years ago reading a statement by Nietzsche. He said he always hoped he would meet some ideal disciples. Sometimes gurus come to America and are disappointed by the following they attract and they die unhappy. Prabhupāda had wanted to start his movement in India, but he said that the Indians were too absorbed in politics to hear him. Coming to America provided him with the limbs he needed to carry out his mission. It’s not that he was appreciating our stature as devotees—we certainly weren’t great devotees—but he could see that we were simple and struggling, and despite our lack of qualification, willing to serve. This gave him an overall confirmation of his spiritual master’s blessing and the hope that before he left this world, he would be able to establish a movement that would gain its own momentum and thus fulfill Lord Caitanya’s prophecy that the holy name would be heard in every town and village.

His only hopes were these boys and girls who came. None of his Godbrothers helped him. Even Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura expressed his disgust at how his disciples were fighting over rooms in the *mātha* and weren’t preaching. Letters like this one express the love story between Prabhupāda and his disciples.

For us in Boston, we tried to keep alive the sense that Prabhupāda had a mission and that he had first landed in Boston on his way from India. Boston was not more special than New York, because Prabhupāda had actually started ISKCON in New York. But still, Prabhupāda didn’t forget that he had arrived in Boston, and in 1968, he went down to the pier and walked around. At that time, he told us how he had been alone when he first came, but now he had so many places. He also said that if he died, he knew we would carry on.

Prabhupāda’s meditation on Boston seemed to be a remembrance of his helplessness and loneliness and how he had prayed to Kṛṣṇa. I still remember how we found his diary among his papers and discovered the poems he had written on the ocean and then at Boston pier. When I heard that Prabhupāda had written such poems, I asked him to translate them into English and allow me to print them in BTG. He sent the translation to me in the mail.

I still remember reading it to the devotees. We thought it was fantastic. We were touched by how personal his expression was, and we felt pained by his humility when he signed off, “An insignificant beggar, Bhaktivedanta Swami, Boston Commonwealth Pier,” and the date. That helped us to appreciate Boston more and its specialness in Prabhupāda’s activities.

Another important point in this letter was Prabhupāda’s instruction to protect the devotees from *māyā.* Prabhupāda encouraged us to preach to each other and to care for each other in Kṛṣṇa consciousness. When different people came to the temple, we talked to them and tried to convince them to become devotees. The students were the most difficult, especially those who attended Harvard University. One Harvard student, however, became a devotee. He had dropped out of Harvard after his first year. He was such a nice person. His philosophy was, “Do your own thing and you’ll be king.” Going to Harvard was no longer “his thing.” Eventually he joined and became initiated as Mathureśa dāsa.

Others were also coming. I remember one man and his girlfriend coming by the temple and telling me that he had seen a vision of Kṛṣṇa. Afterwards, he heard about the Hare Kṛṣṇa movement and everything was just as he had seen it in his vision. We had to train all these people to become devotees so that they didn’t go back to their former lives. It was a heavy responsibility. This particular message was for Brahmānanda, although Prabhupāda wrote it in my letter. Prabhupāda didn’t want Brahmānanda to be irresponsible and to leave New York, but to stay and train the people who were joining.

*Māyā* was a real person to us and we spoke about her freely, but we also spoke freely about Kṛṣṇa. I remember one devotee back in the Boston days remarked that maybe we talked too much about Kṛṣṇa and didn’t understand well enough the dangers of *anarthas* and *aparādhas.* Our conception of *māyā* was that it was something threatening and dangerous, but we knew that Kṛṣṇa was more powerful. Prabhupāda told us not to eulogize *māyā,* but to praise *māyā’s* master, Kṛṣṇa. Whether or not we had a scholarly or devotional understanding of *māyā,* we recognized it as part of our lives and something with which we would have to contend. We kept a respectful distance and we tried to help others to do the same. That was part of our *bhakta* training program—to make people aware of the danger and to protect them through preaching from falling into it.

In his P.S., Prabhupāda tells me to ask Dayānanda to write. Although Dayānanda had been the temple president in Los Angeles, he was an expert electrician and we had asked him to come to our temple to do the electrical work for the press. He must have still been in Boston when Prabhupāda wrote this letter.

I still like to read Prabhupāda’s request in this letter. He was in a foreign country and he asks me to contact different devotees for him. It is especially sweet the way he expresses himself in English: “He is so nice boy . . . she is so nice as her husband.” His words caressed us and encouraged us. “Prabhupāda is thinking of us and saying we have nice qualities.” It encouraged our spirit of cooperation.

Tittenhurst  
26th October, 1969  
My Dear Satsvarupa,  
Please accept my blessings. I beg to acknowledge receipt of your letter dated October 22, 1969. I have duly made corrections on the *Isopanisad* glossary you had enclosed, and I shall send it to Brahmananda as requested by you. I want that in all of our books, magazines and other writings the scholarly presentation be given in all instances, so for every Sanskrit word there must be the appropriate spelling and diacritic marks. Regarding your question about BTG containing more than one essay by me in certain issues, you may use your own judgement in this connection. Enclosed is a tape for *Krsna,* and at the part on this tape where Kamsa is killed, that is the end of the first volume of *Krsna.* The remaining portion shall be published as the second volume. Now we must make arrangements for its publication. Please consult with Advaita as to the price for 10,000 copies on the style of *TLC.* There will be approximately 350 printed pages and 50 pages of paintings. So combinedly you determine what the production costs will be and let me know your figures. Regarding your final question, you are correct that *Garbodaksayee Visnu* and *Karanodaśayee Visnu* are not division of Paramatma. So you may change the words “divided into” into “is one of”. I am so pleased to learn that already the press is ready to begin printing some Krsna Consciousness booklet, and please keep me informed on your progress.  
I hope this will meet you in good health.  
Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

Prabhupāda writes, “I want that in all our books, magazines and other writings the scholarly presentation be given in all instances.” That was his standard. Although his Indian *Bhāgavatams* were printed with so many mistakes in India, he wanted his English editions to be perfect. So many students and professors would be reading his books and Prabhupāda wanted to present the *śāstra* without mistakes; he wanted the scholars to respect his literature and not see it as sentimental. After all, Prabhupāda was writing in Jīva Gosvāmī’s *sampradāya.* Rūpa Gosvāmī, Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura, Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura—they were all great scholars as well as devotees. Around this time, he began to establish Sanskrit standards. He could have chosen to use “sh” instead of “ś” or “ṣ” and make his books more accessible to the public. He chose in favor of scholarship because he wanted the Westerners to appreciate both his scholarly rendering from the Sanskrit and the devotional value of his translations. He wanted his books to read fluidly and the philosophy to be clear. There was considerable work involved in making it come out right.

Prabhupāda’s decisions on standards were quite specific. For example, I remember Pradyumna sending him spellings for *yogī* that had endings such as *yogin,* because college textbooks often spell it that way. Prabhupāda chose to keep the diacritics and to spell it according to the higher standard.

I had to read Prabhupāda’s books carefully if I was to edit them properly. Therefore, I caught something which Prabhupāda acknowledged as a mistake. I remember at the same time, Brahmānanda also wrote to Prabhupāda with what he thought was a mistake. Prabhupāda had written that the demigod worshipers are condemned or criticized, but worse still are the worshipers of the Absolute. Brahmānanda wondered how the worshipers of the Absolute could be worse than the demigod worshipers. In this case, Prabhupāda said it wasn’t a mistake, because it is dangerous to worship the impersonal Absolute. The demigod worshipers are at least worshiping a higher authority, whereas the impersonalists become atheists. It’s likely that we then adjusted what he had written to specify the impersonal Absolute.

I had another service which I liked very much, which was writing the blurbs on the back of the books and the introductions describing what the books were. I took a lot of pleasure in that assignment. It was an area where I could use my knowledge of Western mentality and of readers to try and attract readers who knew nothing about Kṛṣṇa. Writing those blurbs was always a challenge.

When we printed the Second Canto chapters as small paperbacks, I also titled the chapters. Those were the first books ISKCON Press printed. Prabhupāda liked them, but after awhile, our printing mistakes became obvious as the books started to fall apart. We were still in an experimental stage at that time. I chose the titles from references in each chapter and tried to use Śrīla Prabhupāda’s own expressions.

Gradually, however, these services were taken on by others. Everyone was involved with the press in one way or another because the presses ran twenty-four hours a day. Devotees worked in shifts—the girls doing the compositing and other services and the men doing the actual printing. Although there was a division between temple and press, we wanted the two to be positive and cooperative. Actually, there was such a nice spirit because everyone had the opportunity to help print Prabhupāda’s books.

Just reading Prabhupāda’s points about the *Īśopaniṣad* glossary reminds me of how wonderful it was in those days to be involved with Prabhupāda’s book production and distribution. We had such a small staff compared to how many devotees later went to work for the BBT. There was no BBT in 1969. ISKCON Press was small-time, but we were happy and confident that we were personally serving Prabhupāda.

Again in this letter, Prabhupāda addresses BTG: “Regarding your question about BTG containing more than one essay by me in certain issues, you may use your own judgement in this connection.” BTG was becoming more and more my project. Although Hayagrīva and I were supposed to work together, he was mostly sending his material to me and I would assemble the magazine. The discussion arose at one point whether there was enough of Prabhupāda in BTG. Prabhupāda says in this letter that BTG was more his students’ magazine and that we should make the decisions.

I found it interesting that Prabhupāda had told me to make the decisions after what he had experienced with Rāyarāma. Previously, BTG had been his project and he was always thinking how to make it appealing to nondevotees. Part of the issue with him was that he felt he needed to compromise pure presentation to win public acceptance.

I was also conscious of this problem and wanted to find the balance. In my own articles, I tended to paraphrase and summarize the *Bhāgavatam.* Since I was typing the *Bhāgavatam,* I knew the stories before they were even published. I wrote essays on Lord Śiva or Varāha, *paramparā* stories, stories about temple life and other aspects of our lives as devotees. I didn’t tackle worldly politics and problems like Rāyarāma did. And I encouraged articles about and photos of *saṅkīrtana,* of the devotees performing their duties, and of different forms of preaching.

When I received this letter from Prabhupāda and realized that we were on the last tape of the first volume of *Kṛṣṇa* book, I understood that it was a great victory for Prabhupāda. When we thought of *Kṛṣṇa* book being printed in 10,000 copies and that we could then distribute it to the public, we felt we would be able to share in his victory. We weren’t quite in the mood that we were going to save the world, although we had some young enthusiasm for preaching. Prabhupāda simply said that the *Kṛṣṇa* book would be a wonderful way for people to hear about Kṛṣṇa and we agreed with him.

Finishing the first volume of *Kṛṣṇa* book was exciting. It gave me the same feeling people get when they are following a leader, an older child among the children, or a Boy Scout leader, and that person is always coming up with new and exciting ideas. We were like that with Prabhupāda. We would read his ideas in his letters and we would be thrilled by his plans. “Prabhupāda’s going to end this book! He’s going to put out a volume in 10,000 copies!” It wasn’t that we had feelings or plans independent of his enthusiasm. He told us what he wanted to do and we carried it out fervently. There was no question of rethinking his decisions: “Wait a minute, Prabhupāda, why don’t you wait and put it all out as one volume?” Rather, we were always left with a sense of unending service. This volume was ending and Prabhupāda was promising us another one to work on.

Another interesting point is that although Prabhupāda ended the first volume with Kṛṣṇa killing Kaṁsa in Mathurā, he never distinguished between Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes in Mathurā and Dvārakā and His pastimes in Vṛndāvana. He did mention, almost as a technical point, that Kṛṣṇa never leaves Vṛndāvana and that the pastimes outside Vṛndāvana are not performed by the real Kṛṣṇa. Prabhupāda used the phrase, “not the real Kṛṣṇa.” He said that just as a high court judge does not reveal his real feature in court but only at home, so Kṛṣṇa fully reveals Himself only in Vṛndāvana.

Still, he never said that he was sad to finish the Vṛndāvana pastimes and that now he had to work on Mathurā and Dvārakā. He never indicated to us that he found the later pastimes less relishable. His enthusiasm continued right through the book. Kṛṣṇa remained Kṛṣṇa and He was just as inconceivable in His renunciation of His Vṛndāvana associates. The *gopīs* and His mother cried, but Kṛṣṇa left it all. That is God.

Prabhupāda continued his dictation, and we continued painting and typing and editing with enthusiasm. Kṛṣṇa was the same hero, the same boy, now performing a new variety of activities. That’s healthy. It’s not that we should not read Kṛṣṇa’s later pastimes. Although it could be called a higher *bhāva* not to read them, Prabhupāda and all the commentators continued their commentary, so why shouldn’t we relish it?

Prabhupāda doesn’t discuss any of this in this letter, but he addresses production details. He wants 10,000 copies of approximately 350 printed pages each, with fifty pages of illustrations. He asks me to give him a quote based on these specifications.

I found this difficult to do. There were only so many things I could accomplish in a day. I wasn’t Superman, but a mortal being. Still, I tried to do everything. At least I had youth on my side. But the truth was, I couldn’t handle much more. Fortunately, Prabhupāda was kind. Although he continued to ask for more and more, if I indicated I couldn’t do something, he wouldn’t necessarily retract his request, but he would see, “Oh, he can’t do this.” He never found fault with his disciples for not doing more than they were able.

Because I was the temple president, I was the figurehead press manager too. Everything seemed to have to cross my desk. I did what I could—I looked at paper samples, I discussed problems, I listened to figures—but I couldn’t double-check anything or call other printers for a better price. I did try to be serious and sit down with Advaita as I would sit down with anyone and hear what he had to say.

Prabhupāda once said that he kept me on the GBC even though I wasn’t a good manager because I did what he said. It’s true, I am not a good manager, but just as I later stayed on the GBC for many years, so I continued trying to manage the press because Prabhupāda asked me to.

But it wasn’t only because he asked me to. As Prabhupāda expressed his desire to produce his books, we wanted to respond to him. It was so pleasing to see him in this mood, and being part of his press was part of our relationship with him.

Now I produce books with my disciples and I also have a relationship with the devotees who help me. My book production is on a tiny scale, but I am following what Prabhupāda showed me. I don’t think I’m imitating Prabhupāda or taking anything away from him, because my books are about him. My books are meant for the same purpose—to spread the Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement. Devotees in other fields also take Prabhupāda’s part. For example, many of them give college lectures or do other things that at one time only he did. It’s all part of our following his lead. Those devotees are tasting the flavor of service for Prabhupāda by growing up and following in his footsteps. This is the nature of a family business. When a man’s sons grow up, the father retires and the sons run the business. It’s not that the sons have to remain perpetually immature to prove their affection. Their maturity and competence is just as pleasing to the father. Especially now that Prabhupāda has left the planet, it must be pleasing for him to see no one trying to take his place, but all his different sons and daughters doing the things he used to do in the ways he used to do it. Therefore, there are not only new editions of his books, but editions of our own books—new versions of expanding the *paramparā*.

Someone might think that this is improper. It’s one thing for me to respond enthusiastically to Prabhupāda’s book production, but I shouldn’t feel the same enthusiasm about producing my own books. I don’t see anything improper about it. I learned to produce Kṛṣṇa conscious books from Prabhupāda, and it’s a fact, producing books creates a special taste. When there are devotees who are willing to sample that taste with me, then why can’t we churn the same enthusiasm? As Prabhupāda said, the disciple becomes the next spiritual master, although he always remains a disciple. I have remained Prabhupāda’s disciple. If I write books about Prabhupāda and his teachings and people like them, then they are simply a continuation of the flavor that was there.

It was wonderful working with Prabhupāda on his book production, not only for me, but for all the press devotees. I have already mentioned that in 1969 when Prabhupāda met with the BTG staff in Boston, he encouraged us that BTG was the major *saṅkīrtana* party. He said that the other party goes out and can be heard only a few blocks, but BTG was being widely distributed. (Hayagrīva edited that out of the tape because he was afraid some of the *saṅkīrtana* devotees would become discouraged.)

The truth is, there is special reciprocation in whatever services we perform. The press devotees felt that book production was the *bṛhat-mṛdaṅga,* and the *saṅkīrtana* devotees received their own taste. The *pūjārīs* have their own taste. No one can understand the taste in another’s service unless they are performing it with the same dedication. In this case, the press workers were tasting something wonderful: the anxiety of putting a book together and finally getting it out.

In Boston, all the devotees were caught up in the flow of ISKCON Press’s activities. We were also caught up in the philosophy as it gradually unfolded from Prabhupāda’s dictation. Prabhupāda had stopped his *Bhāgavatam* translation while he was in India in 1967, and upon his return, he again took it up from where he left off at the Third Canto. I remember that he started with the verse describing how the force of Diti’s pregnancy caused the universe to darken.

As I reread these cantos now, again and again I remember my experience of reading them for the first time, and especially, the experience of hearing Prabhupāda dictate them on tape. There is such sweetness in those memories, but they also remind me of other things that were going on at the time.

For example, I remember reading with my wife the chapter entitled “Devahūti’s Lamentation.” Kardama expresses his desire to get married and then goes to perform austerities. Lord Viṣṇu appears to him and tells him that his desires will be fulfilled. Then Svāyambhuva Manu approaches Kardama and offers him his daughter, Devahūti.

There is a description in that section of how Devahūti served Kardama. My wife and I were recently married and these chapters contained a lot of good information for householders. I remember one verse and purport (3.23.2) where it describes how Devahūti served her husband with intimacy and great respect, with controlled senses, love, and sweet words.

In the purport, Prabhupāda writes that there are two important processes for the wife in serving the husband. *Viśrambheṇa* means “with intimacy” and *gauraveṇa* means “with great reverence.” The husband is an intimate friend; therefore, the wife must render service like an intimate friend. At the same time, she must understand that her husband is her superior and she must offer him all respect.

My wife and I discussed these points, but unfortunately, we didn’t take them as seriously as we should have. We could understand, however, that Prabhupāda was quite aware of what real intimacy between husband and wife should be and he was offering all householders a solution to their usual problems. Inevitably, husband and wife become so intimate and familiar that they see each other’s faults and can no longer deny that they are intimate friends. If we try to keep only the model of family life—that the wife follows all the externals, such as not using her husband’s name, seeing his superiority, etc.—how can she do that when they are such friends?

Here it is recognized that they are intimate friends more because she is his confidante. Her husband will tell her of his innermost feelings, perhaps how he feels about his Godbrothers and himself, and will perhaps express his inadequacies. She may also share with him in the same way. Hearing each other’s problems is part of their service to each other as friends, but it has to be coupled with her attitude of respect in order to make the relationship Kṛṣṇa conscious. Prabhupāda understood us in this purport. Unfortunately, we couldn’t follow it, although we tried.

Although we don’t memorize all of Prabhupāda’s expressions, when we hear them again and again, they sound like wonderful, eternal tunes or familiar music. Prabhupāda had so many perfect expressions. Here’s another one about husband and wife: “I confirm herewith my statement that the wife is the shareholder of the husband’s pious activities.” (Letter to Jadurāṇī, November 1, 1968)

Prabhupāda used that phrase in a letter to my wife and he used the same phrase in a purport to this section of the *Bhāgavatam.* Devahūti received all the benefit of spiritual life because she followed her husband sincerely. “By your principles of devotion to your husband, you have achieved and can enjoy transcendental gifts very rarely obtained by persons proud of aristocracy and material possessions. Although you have not yet experienced these achievements which are free from fear and lamentation I shall offer them all to you because you are engaged in my service. Now just look at them. I am giving you the transcendental vision to see how they are.” In his purport, Prabhupāda writes that if we really want to help anyone, we should develop love of God and give it to others.

Here’s another line I think is memorable. It’s idealistic and yet filled with worldly wisdom: *“Kṛṣṇa-prema,* Kṛṣṇa consciousness is the highest gift which can be bestowed on anyone whom we presume to love.” *(Bhāg.* [3.23.8](https://vedarama.com/en/books/sb-3/23.8), purport) If we love someone, we want to give them something. If we *really* love someone—our spouses or children—then give them love of God. In one sense, Prabhupāda is implying that it is presumptuous to claim that we love someone. If we claim to love someone but do not give them *kṛṣṇa-prema,* then our so-called love is not very valuable.

These expressions were flowing into our lives because we were producing them into books. It completely transformed our lives to be linked so directly to Prabhupāda. The information superhighway connects unlimited households by a telephone and a computer screen. People can see videos, preview computer programs, read the news, find entertainment, and gather information. People no longer have to leave their desks to shop for clothes or appliances. In Boston, we were connected to Prabhupāda’s information superhighway. We just had to plug in our headphones, turn on the transcriber, and hear Prabhupāda reciting and commenting on the *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam*. It gave us access to all the wisdom of the previous *ācāryas* and scriptures, which according to the *Bhāgavatam* are too difficult to assimilate in this age. We were such poor receptacles. We were still lusty, still low-class, still quarreling, yet we had access to a great treasure that we didn’t really deserve and didn’t know how to spend. We spoiled it to some degree by our impure attempts to take it and distribute it, but nevertheless, Prabhupāda gave us the link.

Therefore, the Boston community was lively. This is where we got our energy—from this and from chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa. Even the rough devotees, especially those who later proved not to be serious, were serious and dedicated at this time. It seemed that those who followed strictly were happy and those who didn’t, weren’t. It is also true that youth accounts for a lot of the willingness to perform austerity, because the idealism hasn’t yet been tested. At this time, however, most unhappiness and difficulties were overcome by the general enthusiasm. Prabhupāda felt close to us and the Hare Kṛṣṇa movement was just beginning to soar.

Neither did we analyze ourselves as much as devotees do today. We didn’t consider whether we were looking to fulfill our ideal goals or to be happy. Therefore, it is hard to separate what we preached and what we were. ISKCON has diminished in so many ways over the past decade, but today there is more honesty among the devotees. We could say that the self-analysis has gone to the extreme, but devotees have a tendency now to face who they really are, what they really want, and their shortcomings. We were more simple in that sense. Were we looking for happiness? We didn’t know. We lived to speak the philosophy and to live it as ideally as possible. We were so absolute in our acceptance of the philosophy that if I was asked whether or not I was looking for happiness in 1969, I would have had to say that I was only trying to please Kṛṣṇa. That was our only reality. All such questions in those days would have received a tirade of absolute answers.

For example, there was a lot of talk in the ’60s among *yogīs* about “bliss consciousness.” One person told me that a particular *yogī* had the best bliss. Well, we weren’t interested in bliss. We were interested in making Kṛṣṇa happy. Anyone who interviewed us received that answer. The absolute answers drove out whatever else was in our minds. Anything that didn’t fit into the category of making Kṛṣṇa happy was immediately labeled *“māyā.*” And we didn’t tolerate *māyā*.

That gave us a lot of energy and it helped us to not look at our problems. Happiness is so ephemeral anyway, and it was hard to measure our happiness against our unhappiness in the strain of duties. Looking back, I see myself as a sympathetic older brother might have seen me then. I had a lot on my shoulders—leadership, financial responsibility, the pressure to hold the *saṅkīrtana* and the press and the Deity worship all under one roof, the pressure to make progress and to satisfy Prabhupāda by producing his books. I was also married and had to satisfy my wife, was working a job and had to satisfy my boss, and I had to deal with the neighborhood punks. It was a lot of pressure.

I don’t regret one minute of the strain, however. We were able to be so personally connected to Prabhupāda. I got letters from him sometimes more than once a week. I wouldn’t trade the strain of those days for any kind of peace.

I give the devotees at that time the benefit of the doubt. We really thought happiness meant serving the spiritual master and that seeking something for ourselves was sense gratification. Where did we get those ideas? We responded to Prabhupāda’s preaching. I also thought that I could apply it to the highest degree, as he was asking us to do. We should always respond to our spiritual master in that way, but in 1969 in Boston, it was an especially good time to be responsive because he was so much demanding our surrender and participation in his preaching.

To work hard brought happiness too. If we worried about our own situations, sense gratification, and problems, we considered that we weren’t putting Prabhupāda and his mission in the center of our lives.

There was some fanaticism in some of those attitudes. In our attempts to please Śrīla Prabhupāda, we would work ourselves until our bodies collapsed or were neglected in various ways. We were like a big family. If someone couldn’t do a particular service, they could do something else. There were weak members and people in *māyā,* new people and people whose commitment was questionable. And there were strong people, people whose Kṛṣṇa consciousness was grounded and who became models for the others. There wasn’t much envy or tendency to measure one devotee against another. If someone was obviously the better devotee, he or she would be recognized as such; those who were lesser were treated kindly. People were allowed to indulge in their lack of seriousness and still stay at the temple.

ISKCON is different now. Some of the present honesty has come about because devotees also acted on a level of realization that they did not necessarily possess. It’s not that one stage of the movement is necessarily better than another. Honesty comes with maturity and experience. We could even ask why Prabhupāda pushed the devotees so hard and accepted service from devotees who obviously weren’t up to the standard. One of the answers is that he had to get the mission going. Prabhupāda initiated people who were barely able to follow the principles. We could say that this was the ninth offense in chanting, but Prabhupāda served the higher principle: to save the whole world. That meant he had to do whatever was necessary and work with whomever was available. Later he said that he no longer wanted to be so permissive.

Therefore, as ISKCON continues to grow and change, we can recognize that there are different times and different ways to preach. That was Prabhupāda’s own example. We cannot imitate his realization or potency, and we cannot deny that his pressure on us still exists, but we have seen so much failure, especially in the leaders, that we are forced to examine what a leader is and what he or she should be doing. Devotees want to take care of themselves now and that’s a natural progression in relation to the movement’s history. Whether that’s more or less surrendered is not important in the face of what has happened. Most devotees have had no choice but to respond in this way.

Tittenhurst  
31st October, 1969  
My Dear Satsvarupa,  
Please accept my blessings. I beg to acknowledge receipt of your letter dated October 28, 1969, with enclosures. The pamphlet sermon is not unfavorable. It is indirectly favorable because in that pamphlet the writer has admitted that the Christian church is waning and people are seeking after some new type of religion. That he has admitted. He says “Suppose the Christian church is waning, suppose even that in 10 years it will have gone out of existence. What then?” So these Christian priests are already feeling the pulse of their religious principles, and they are not very much hopeful. He writes another place that a woman being asked by her friend why she was not coming to church, the woman replied, “Oh, we don’t go to church anymore.” So far as we are concerned, he has admitted that the boy whom he spoke with was soft-spoken and polite. He seemed intelligent and had obviously been well brought up. In another place he says “What interested me most however was that here was a boy who was obviously religiously inclined. He was trying to find God and was trying to help other people find God, and he had taken up his post in front of a Christian church to preach Krishna.” Don’t you think that indirectly he is feeling the effect of our preaching work and his whole pamphlet is written as if he is afraid of the Krishna cult, which is spreading like wildfire? So we shall not be at all discouraged by such writings. Rather we should take the real fact that people are actually hankering after the real type of religion.  
Yesterday we had a very successful meeting at Oxford at the Town Hall. About 350 boys, girls, old men, ladies and gentlemen participated, and we made them all dance and chant with us, every one. After the meeting, many boys and gentlemen came to congratulate me. Out of them, one was an Egyptian gentleman. Similarly, in another meeting in Conway Hall one Chinese boy came and offered his obeisances exactly like my disciples, bowing down to my feet. Negro boys are also taking part. So it is a fact that our movement will appeal to the heart of everyone, and they will join us. If one is not very much sophisticated and overburdened by material contamination, then he must respond to our call. The only thing is that we as preachers must be very, very pure, sincere and serious. The crude example is that when a man is physically very strong, and if he has sex intercourse with a woman, she is sure to become pregnant. When pregnancy fails, it is due to the weakness of the male partner generally. Similarly, if we are full with Krishna vitality, then wherever we go the audience will be impregnated with Krishna Consciousness. Now some of our leading boys, like you, Brahmananda, Tamal, etc. you should be very careful to train your other junior Godbrothers to the right type of preacher, keeping full faith in Krishna and the Spiritual Master, and executing the routine works of chanting and following the regulations. That will make you all great preachers, and wherever you go you will come out victorious.  
Now coming to some other points discussed in the Cathedral Sermons pamphlet, we may take notice of the writer’s statement which may help us in understanding the real position of Christian religion. In one of the statements he says that Bishop Dean, the former Executive Officer of the Anglican Communion, said to the general Synod of the Anglican Church of Canada last month that he gives the church as it exists today ten more years of life. The reason the church was dying he said was because it had become irrelevant. This means that the church people no more can convince the advanced, educated men of the present day. In another place he says in discussing the Ten Commandments of the Bible about the sanctity of “human life.” Instead of exactly quoting the commandment “Thou shalt not kill,” he replaces by his own words “Thou shalt do no murder.” But he does not know how his own words reflect to the then society wherein Lord Jesus Christ was preaching. To say to his audience, “Thou shalt do no murder” means they were very much accustomed to commit murder. So what is the position of that society where the members are accustomed to commit murder, and what class of preaching can be made to such persons? As we see in another religious principle there is instruction that henceforward you shall not co-habit with your mother. So we have to judge such societies where there are murderers and those having sex life with their mothers, what kind of men they are. In the *Bhagavad Gita* the religious principles are divided into three categories: in the modes of goodness, passion and ignorance. Generally, all so-called principles are in the modes of passion and ignorance. Maybe there are some moral instructions, but moral instructions without God-consciousness is impossible to follow. In another place the gentleman quotes one book written by Prof. Charles Smith. The book’s name is “The Paradox of Jesus in the Gospels.” In this book it is admitted that all the statements in the Bible are not directly spoken by Jesus. Some of them are staged through the mouth of Jesus Christ; and specifically this passage: “I am the way, the Truth and the light. No man comes unto the Father but by me.” This gentleman admits that it is put into the mouth of Jesus because that is the literary convention of the author of the 4th Gospel. Such kinds of observations definitely suggests that there are many passages in the Gospel which are later on set up to be spoken by Lord Jesus Christ, but actually they were manufactured by different devotees. So far as our *Bhagavad Gita* is concerned, we do not find any such thing. Everywhere it is stated *sri bhagavan uvacha:* the Supreme Personality of Godhead said. And all the acharyas have accepted these words as they are spoken by the Lord. No authorized acharya has ever commented that it was put into the mouth of Krishna by Vyasadeva or Samjaya or any other person.  
I give you all these hints not for general discussion, but for your personal understanding to know the respective positions of different types of religious principles. Ours is transcendental because we are not very much concerned with the minor moral or immoral principles, although each and every devotee is a first class moralist. But our religious principle or natural occupational duty is to learn how to love Krishna. We are practicing this and we are teaching this. So let us very steadily stick to our principles and our movement will surely come out successful.  
I hope this will meet you in good health.  
Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

This is one of the longest letters I received from Prabhupāda. This is what happened.

In Boston, somebody brought me a pamphlet produced by a local Catholic church. It was an unassuming pamphlet—small, with glossy paper. The church printed these pamphlets weekly for the parishioners to present the previous Sunday’s sermon. Someone had seen a reference to the devotees in it and had given me a copy of it. After reading it, I sent it to Prabhupāda. I sent it as an afterthought, not realizing how full a response I would get from Prabhupāda and how it would impress him.

Not only did Prabhupāda send me this letter, but he also repeatedly referred to the pamphlet in his lectures. I can still remember some of the phrases Prabhupāda used in his lectures when referring to this pamphlet. He would talk about the Hare Kṛṣṇa movement and how it was becoming influential in the world, or he might speak in another context about Christian religion and its failure. Then he would say, “And in Boston, one priest has written a pamphlet that these Hare Kṛṣṇa boys who I see on the streets chanting, they’re actually our boys. They’re from Christian background but they never cared for attending the church. But now they’ve become mad after God. Why is this?”

This was the point that Prabhupāda liked about the priest’s sermon. Often in his lectures he went on to answer his own question: it is because in the church, they don’t teach them what is love of God. Again and again he mentioned this priest in Boston. It became a permanent part of his preaching. Now when I hear it in a lecture, I become happy knowing that he is referring to this pamphlet I sent him in 1969.

As Prabhupāda writes in his letter to me, the pamphlet was candid in that the priest honestly addressed the issue of why people no longer go to church. He said that in the summer, they take vacations instead of performing religious activities. He also blamed the Christian churches because they had become irrelevant in their preaching. He was looking for a solution. The incident where he mentioned the devotees was that we stood in front of his church to collect money and to sell BTG. He stopped and spoke with a devotee and learned that he was from a Christian background. Therefore, he went before his parish and told them that somehow, we have lost this nice young man with religious tendencies.

Prabhupāda took what he said in a different way. Actually, he accepted what the priest said: it was their fault that the youth were becoming devotees instead of Catholics. Kṛṣṇa consciousness was a more positive influence in their lives. Now these boys and girls are mad after God. The priest wrote candidly that the young man he met was dedicated to his religion and that the typical Christian lacks dedication.

I’m sure this letter was dictated onto tape and typed by a secretary. Prabhupāda appears to be reading the pamphlet as he dictates the letter because he goes from one point to the next. “Now coming to some other points discussed in the Cathedral Sermons pamphlet, we may take note of the writer’s statement . . . ” Prabhupāda dissects the priest’s logic. His comments enter the realm of analyzing the Church doctrine. Finally he explains that the priest quoted a Christian scholar who said that Jesus didn’t speak some of the statements attributed to him.

That could lead to a lot of controversy with the Christians, but Prabhupāda continues to draw it out: “So far as our *Bhagavad Gita* is concerned, we do not find any such thing. Everywhere it is stated *sri bhagavan uvacha:* the Supreme Personality of Godhead said. And all the acharyas have accepted these words as they are spoken by the Lord. No authorized acharya has ever commented that it was put into the mouth of Krishna by Vyasadeva or Samjaya or any other person.”

Then Prabhupāda writes, “I give you all these hints not for general discussion but for your personal understanding.” We were thrilled by any special treatment or intimacy with Prabhupāda. When I read this letter, I remember thinking that I wasn’t supposed to talk about it in public because these topics were sensitive, even controversial. I even considered not showing the letter to the devotees, although that was impossible. The devotees all knew when letters from Prabhupāda arrived. In the end, I did share it with the devotees, but I asked them to be discreet in their preaching. Many of the devotees also came from Christian backgrounds and we shared some of the concerns that Prabhupāda addressed. Some of us may have even had some doubts. Therefore, Prabhupāda points out the weakness of the Christian theology for our own understanding. He didn’t want us to find fault with them—actually, he wanted us to encourage their practices—but because we were his disciples, he wanted to tell us privately about some of the discrepancies.

That’s why Prabhupāda wanted us to be careful to keep this information to ourselves. Sometimes devotees maintain their attraction to their previous religion, and what to speak of the nondevotees. In Italy, for example, many people revere St. Francis of Assisi. It can be difficult for a person to accept Kṛṣṇa consciousness if the devotees are insensitive or offensive toward St. Francis. Such insensitivity is neophyte. Prabhupāda pointed out discrepancies in the Christian church, but he also offered respect and appreciation toward their saints.

When we first went to Boston, we made a point to speak at various churches as part of our interreligious preaching. Prabhupāda also did that kind of preaching, and when he first went to Manhattan, he liked to speak at churches. In describing those experiences, he wrote that although the people appreciated him, some of the priests were suspicious “as is expected among sectarian people.” Prabhupāda preached to the religionists because he wanted to give them *bhakti.* When one devotee told Prabhupāda that he had thought of finding him an engagement at a seminary but had changed his mind because what was the point in preaching to those who were already saved, Prabhupāda asked, “Who is saved?” No one in the material world is saved.

In this letter, Prabhupāda calls me a “leading boy” and instructs me to train others. I treasure such phrases. Perhaps I treasure them because I want to be part of Prabhupāda’s elite, but I also treasure them because I sincerely want to take responsibility for Prabhupāda. It pleased me to think that he thought of me as responsible and it encouraged me to want to live up to his estimation. His instruction to train others required work, duty, sacrifice, and surrender. Prabhupāda didn’t praise us only to flatter us, but by calling us close to receive his encouragement, we became more intimate with him. We came close enough to be given more austerities and service. That’s what being praised by Prabhupāda meant.

I was willing—we were all willing—to surrender more to Prabhupāda. We liked to be honored by him, but even more, we wanted to please him. To this day I love to be thought of in the identity Prabhupāda gave me, as one of his older students, one of his “leading boys.” If we want *any* praise, we want to hear it from the spiritual master. That is all that can make us happy. Prabhupāda once said, “Why are we distributing all these books? We just want to see our master smile.”

Achieving Prabhupāda’s pleasure meant preaching. Preaching takes faith in guru and Kṛṣṇa and strict adherence to the principles. Prabhupāda writes, “ . . . keeping full faith in Krishna and the Spiritual Master, and executing the routine works of chanting and following the regulations. That will make you all great preachers, and wherever you go you will come out victorious.”

Prabhupāda’s letters are valuable because they show how he kept us vital. Unlike the Christian father who said that his church had lost its vitality, Prabhupāda blessed us with a preaching spirit based on full faith in guru and Kṛṣṇa. We were young, but we could impregnate audiences with the desire to know more about Kṛṣṇa consciousness because we were empowered and enthused by Prabhupāda. As he was a missionary, he trained us to become missionaries.

I didn’t really think of myself as a missionary in 1969. I had a full-time job and the weight of the temple management to worry about. I would sometimes think of how Prabhupāda left India to come to the West, but I didn’t feel up to the challenge of doing something similar. All I could do was go out in the evenings and lecture at programs. Still, I shared the identity he gave us as preachers.

We also knew that anything connected with the preaching movement—whether it was lecturing, speaking to people, distributing books, or running a temple—was preaching. Prabhupāda said that preaching meant to increase the family members. All our activities were directed toward preaching. The young girls who greeted the guests with a giggle and *tilaka* for their foreheads on Sundays were preaching. Those who wandered from room to room distributing *halavā* were preaching. Everyone was preaching. In Boston, we had many, many rooms in the temple. Every time a new householder couple would come, we would further divide a big room by adding another partition. It wasn’t uncommon to walk around the rooms on a Sunday and see husbands and wives sitting down with six or seven guests in their room or to see the art department full of guests. Everyone preached according to his or her capacity on Prabhupāda’s strength.

If someone didn’t feel inclined to argue with a guest, there were other devotees who loved to smash illusions. If someone didn’t want to talk directly to a guest, he or she could chant. We wanted to make devotees and distribute books. No one thought that Kṛṣṇa consciousness should be practiced privately and that they didn’t need to preach. On Sundays, all the lights would be on in the building. It reminded me of the preaching life—all the lights blazing.

We used to engage people in service too. We asked Prabhupāda, “What if someone wants to do service but isn’t serious about spiritual life?” Prabhupāda assured us that if someone turns one screw in the temple, his life can become perfect. Asking people to turn a screw in the temple was preaching.

## 11: November 1969

London  
3rd November, 1969  
My Dear Satsvarupa,  
Please accept my blessings. I thank you for your letter dated November 18, 1969 \[sic\] and have noted all the points carefully. I can understand that the composing program is not going as planned. Therefore, I think Palika Dasi may immediately be trained to help with this task. For layout work I have already asked Aravinda to come to Boston to do that work. So these two devotees will be posted in Boston for the time being to centralize the activities. Then we shall see how to pick up further men. For editing the Sanskrit words you can send me a list of the words, and I shall send you the correct diacritic marks. You write that you shall send me your changes for the *Krsna* manuscript pages here with me, and upon receipt of them I shall do the needful. Please also send carbon copies of the tapes you have edited in the past few months. By separate post a new tape is being sent to you.  
Please offer my blessings to the others. I hope this will meet you in good health.  
Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

This letter is entirely about press business. Prabhupāda states that the “composing program is not going as planned.” ISKCON Press had a compositor, a glorified typewriter, to justify columns and set type. Not every typist knew how to use that machine. It took some training and understanding of compositing. The compositor had to do a lot of calculations in her head. She would type a line, and the compositing machine would type out “+4” or, “+6” to indicate how many spaces short of full justification the line was. The compositor then had to calculate how to fit the words in on each line to justify the type properly. It was slow, neat work.

I can’t remember exactly what the problem was that I relayed to Prabhupāda, but either there weren’t enough skilled devotees to run the machine or those who were using it weren’t using it properly. Therefore, the compositing department became a bottleneck in the production line. Therefore, Prabhupāda instructed me to have Pālikā trained to help.

Even though the compositor was not on the standard of later machines, it was still quite expensive at the time. In a previous letter, Prabhupāda told me that the compositor cost $3,000. He bought the machine before the press moved to Boston, and later sent it to us for the press work.

I don’t know how Prabhupāda discovered compositors, but I remember that as early as 1966 or ’67 in New York City he wanted one. Somehow Prabhupāda had procured a leaflet describing this particular machine and asked me to look at it.

This is an interesting aspect of Prabhupāda’s personality, that he was interested in state-of-the-art equipment for book production. When he heard that IBM was giving away free compositors to schools, he asked me to go and see if we could qualify as an educational institution. He was certainly willing to use whatever was necessary to produce his books. He didn’t hesitate to use anything simply because he was a renounced *sādhu*.

Later, it was different. Here we see him reading leaflets on compositors, but when he visited the BBT in Los Angeles in 1975, he was more like an author going to see his book trust without much technical knowledge of the equipment being used. By then, his disciples were taking care of that aspect of the press. He also sat at Rādhā-vallabha’s desk while Rādhāvallabha showed him the production charts, which defined the various stages of production. Prabhupāda said, “When I see a chart like that, it makes it more complicated for me to understand.” It is always interesting to note how Prabhupāda did whatever had to be done, and when it wasn’t necessary for him to do it anymore because his disciples were trained, he no longer pursued his interest.

I still remember Prabhupāda showing me the leaflet for the compositor in 1966. I was willing to do whatever was necessary to facilitate Prabhupāda’s writing. If he wanted me to learn how to run a compositor, I was willing to try. I also remember that it seemed odd to me that Prabhupāda, who was always talking about Kṛṣṇa, was now handing me a brochure full of the technicalities of compositing. I suppose later I became more acquainted with that side of Prabhupāda, how he could be so transcendental and so practical at the same time, but it surprised me at the time. We saw Swamijī as an authentic Indian *sādhu.* He still wore his pointy white shoes and of course, his *dhotī.* He spoke from *Bhagavad-gītā* and he knew all about Kṛṣṇa. Suddenly, he handed me a high-tech brochure from New York City and asked me whether I could learn how to use such a machine.

Now it is impossible for me to think of the compositor without thinking of Pālikā. When she came to work in Boston, we built her a cubbyhole office at the back of the house near the kitchen. The Boston temple was in a large house and it had three floors and many rooms, but every time someone new came, we would further divide the rooms. It was almost a comical aspect of that house—all the cubbyholes in it. We subdivided rooms mainly to facilitate the press. Often devotees who were working on one aspect of production needed a quiet place to work. We would simply put up a plasterboard structure—we wouldn’t even paint it—and the devotee working in the cubbyhole would hang a madras or a curtain to give themselves some privacy.

Pālikā was a great worker. She was tireless. Advaita wanted the compositor to be run twenty-four hours a day, so the devotees who were trained to use it would take shifts. Pālikā worked all night. The cubbyhole was right near the backyard. Whenever Pālikā felt drowsy, she would go out and spray her face with a hose to keep awake. Once she was up typing and she smelled smoke. Someone had tried to set fire to our house. Fortunately, she was up and able to alert us, so there was no real damage to the house.

Almost all the press equipment was on the first floor and we had built support beams to help the floor withstand the weight of the heavy machines. Almost all the machines were in one room—the printer, the binder, the collating machine. We sent most of the books out to another company for binding, but because Prabhupāda wanted us to bind at least a limited number of copies ourselves, Patita-pāvana came to Boston to help with that work. Patita-pāvana also did the collating and pressing by hand in the basement.

The photo department was in the basement, and that was run by Uddhava and Madhusūdana. The photo department didn’t just work with the photographs or illustrations, but every page had to be photographed as part of the printing process.

Prabhupāda drew resources for the press from all over the movement. Pālikā and Aravinda were sent to Boston from Los Angeles and other devotees came from a variety of temples. Prabhupāda wanted the press centralized—whoever was to work with ISKCON Press work had to live in Boston. We were all in such a renounced spirit in those days. I remember when I visited Los Angeles at Prabhupāda’s invitation to see his model center, I told him more about the press and I hinted that we could use a typist like Pālikā. He immediately called her into the room with her husband, Bhavānanda, and asked her whether she would be willing to go. She agreed and so did Bhavānanda. Prabhupāda was pleased. He said, “This is Kṛṣṇa consciousness. The marriage is not so important. Service is important,” and he made a typing gesture with his hands.

Prabhupāda’s words on this occasion sound contradictory to other things he has said, but Prabhupāda has said so many things. On the one hand, he told Pālikā and Bhavānanda that service was more important than marriage, yet he encouraged so many devotees to get married. Ultimately, Prabhupāda wanted the devotees to become renounced. Renunciation for a Vaiṣṇava doesn’t mean leaving a marriage, but giving everything to Kṛṣṇa. If someone asked him whether giving everything to Kṛṣṇa meant he couldn’t be married, Prabhupāda would say, “No, no, you should be married. You should be householder.” When we examined it closer, however, we could see that he held service as more important than simply living together and keeping a marriage.

Another service that had moved back to Boston was the Sanskrit editing. In this letter, Prabhupāda indicates that he will put the diacritical marks on the manuscript himself. Pradyumna was his Sanskrit editor, but he sometimes presented another bottleneck in the production process. Often the compositor would be waiting for Pradyumna to finish the Sanskrit editing. I was the production manager, so it would fall to me to go down and knock on his door to ask for the pages. He would tell me that he was just about to work on them and that I should come back in two hours. When I returned two hours later, however, the pages wouldn’t be done. I wouldn’t get angry with him, but I would try to negotiate with him. He created a bottleneck because he was the only one who could do the Sanskrit editing. If anyone showed Pradyumna their frustration, he wouldn’t do the work at all. Tempers flared and things became tense. We all felt that Prabhupāda wanted his books produced *now*. When everything stopped because someone hadn’t done their service, everyone felt the pressure. Because of the nature of the work, and because the devotees were not always gentle, our working relationships sometimes became strained.

I was sent down to negotiate with Pradyumna because I never became angry with him. I still remember all the times I would come home from work after having a full day of dealing with the people in the office and then riding two buses home to Advaita standing at the door complaining that Pradyumna hadn’t done his service and that I had to go and see him immediately.
