We had a similar problem with the art department. Since I was technically the manager of the art department, it again fell to me to negotiate with the artists. The artists were wonderful workers, but they were perfectionists and that caused delays.

Uddhava was in charge of photography. Whenever the artists stalled, he would go up to their room in his green uniform and present them with an ultimatum: this painting must be done by such and such a time tomorrow. The artists always agreed, but after all, they were artists and they had a different temperament. They were never finished. Uddhava began to keep his promises. He would walk into the room, take the painting off the easel, and take it down to his office in the basement. (There is a painting in the old *Kṛṣṇa* book that is obviously incomplete.) After awhile, he began to take the paintings away in the middle of the night to avoid conflict with the artists.

Uddhava was pragmatic. It didn’t matter to him whether something was exactly finished if the book had to be printed on schedule. Prabhupāda seemed to notice that the paintings were imperfect, but he simply continued to encourage the artists.

Prabhupāda also writes, “You write that you shall send me your changes for the *Krsna* manuscript pages.” I sent Prabhupāda my “changes,” or editing, regularly. In this letter, however, he is referring to a change that came at a later stage which I needed approved or corrected. Therefore, he writes, “I shall do the needful.”

“Please also send carbon copies of the tapes you have edited in the past few months. By separate post a new tape is being sent to you.” Prabhupāda almost always sent the tapes separate from his letters. He would take an envelope, put the big circular reel into it, fold it over, and staple it. He didn’t pack the tapes securely, and he used no insulation or extra packaging.

Once Prabhupāda asked a devotee in California to send me a package of tapes. He took Prabhupāda’s package to his room and realized that it wasn’t secure. He took it back and showed it to Prabhupāda, but Prabhupāda said, “No, it is all right. Just mail it.” The devotee went out again, looked at it some more, and came back to see Prabhupāda. He said, “I can see the tape peeking through the envelope.” Prabhupāda told him not to contradict his spiritual master. Somehow or other, the tapes always arrived and Prabhupāda knew that.

It was always a joy to know that another and another tape would be arriving in the mail. Just as now I’m trying to rediscover Prabhupāda’s books by reading them again, aware that there is always more to discover if I have the right attitude, so the typing work was never finished.

It’s true that service never stops and *kṛṣṇa-bhajana* is eternal, but it never occurred to us that our services would eventually change. It didn’t even occur to me that one day Prabhupāda would no longer be sending me tapes to type. We took the eternality of our service for granted with great satisfaction and joy. Eventually he did stop sending me tapes, but he mercifully gave me another service to perform on his behalf.

Therefore, although we can look at a letter like this and feel regret that those days are gone, we can also see it in a more mystical way. Prabhupāda is still sending me tapes, still sending all his disciples whatever services they desire. We can rest assured that he knows we are here wanting to work for him and that our connection with him will continue.

The days of ISKCON Press are not a lost era for those who experienced them. I am telling a little of the saga—how the devotees were renounced, how tempers sometimes flared, how we struggled for money and help, how we subdivided rooms endlessly in our Victorian mansion temple. Now it’s gone without a trace. The mansion has been sold, the devotees have dispersed, and what do we have left?

We have Prabhupāda’s books. The fact that we can still read Prabhupāda’s books is probably the greatest tribute to what the devotees did in those days to keep ISKCON Press afloat. To produce Prabhupāda’s books was the only meaning behind all the austerity and struggle. We wanted to help Prabhupāda save crippled humanity and to send everyone back to Godhead.

Thus ISKCON Press is still going on. “By separate post a new tape is being sent to you.” Those tapes are still coming, although I’m not sure in what form. We are still waiting to hear from Prabhupāda “by separate post”—in his books.

Devotees often wonder whether it’s not conducive to their mood of surrender to become attached to a particular service. After all, the spiritual master wants unconditional surrender and may suddenly request us to do something else, as he did with Pālikā when he sent her to Boston. Changing services is often necessary to help the progress of the preaching movement. The press eventually left Boston and went to New York and then to Los Angeles. Similarly, someone may have a peaceful life in their home country but suddenly be sent to war in service to that country. We may have a particular service that we like, but we may be sent to serve in another area because of an emergency need that must be filled. Ultimately, service to Prabhupāda continues regardless of the details of how one is serving. Therefore, the most important point is to be attached to the idea of serving Prabhupāda above the particular details of one service over another.

Everything is ultimately arranged around time, place, and person. In this case, ISKCON Press no longer exists. All the devotees went on to do other services. If we are sincere, then we will still be practicing devotional service. The misfortune is not that Jayādvaita Mahārāja and I are no longer in Boston serving together in the editorial department, but if someone is no longer serving Prabhupāda. That is real misfortune. How someone can give up their service to Prabhupāda should be understood and avoided. We can’t avoid everything. We couldn’t avoid the fact that ISKCON Press moved from Boston or that the devotees went different ways. Prabhupāda called Aravinda to join him in India and Girirāja also went to India. What was really difficult to accept was when some of those devotees stopped serving Prabhupāda along the way. Therefore, even though things have changed, we are completely fortunate because we are still serving at all.

London  
5th November, 1969  
My Dear Satsvarupa,  
Please accept my blessings. I beg to acknowledge receipt of your letter dated October 30, 1969 with enclosures. I will keep the court documents in your file. Regarding BTG printing, I have already written to Brahmananda that this must be printed in our own press. So far as my books are concerned, I think there are materials for at least ten books which are ready for printing. Now all the manuscripts are with you. So now the editorial department is under you and Hayagriva, and you combinedly please get my books printed, one after another. I think the following management will be nice: I shall pay the book printing price; actual cost plus 10% maintenance charges. Then after the books are printed you will distribute them proportionately to different centers, and they will remit the price directly to me. Purushottam will keep accounts for that so that the responsibility will be lighter on your side. Besides that, if the books are distributed immediately after printing, without payment at first, the centers will be encouraged to stock them and sell them. I have consulted on this matter with Purushottam, and he says that the idea is right. Now you can give me your own opinion also. But continually all the *Bhagavatams, Krishna, Nectar of Devotion,* etc. must be published. After printing, some of them may be made softbound and some may be made hardbound, according to demand.  
I have already written to Brahmananda that we shall not accept outside work. That will complicate our situation as a tax-free organization. Brahmananda wrote me that Advaita wants to purchase a wagon, but I don’t think the press department will require a wagon just now. You have already one bus, so money should not unnecessarily be spent up. For the present the idea may be suspended, and when I come to Boston I shall see if it is actually needed.  
Please offer my blessings to the others. I hope this will meet you in good health.  
Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

In this letter Prabhupāda informs me that he has enough manuscript material for ten books and that it is all ready for printing. Prabhupāda was always ahead of his disciples. When Prabhupāda thought of writing, he visualized the entire publishing and distribution process. Fortunately, he could entangle many disciples in the loving network of publishing activity so that they were always thinking of Kṛṣṇa and serving their spiritual master’s book production. Working in Prabhupāda’s book production also always reminded us that we should give Prabhupāda’s books to the materialistic society.

Prabhupāda sometimes said that he wasn’t a great scholar or literary man, even though he had been writing since his household life. I doubt he thought of his writing as an artistic tendency or a desire for self-expression as much as he thought of it as coming from his spiritual master and the *sampradāya.* He understood that the best way to educate people in Kṛṣṇa consciousness was to present them with Kṛṣṇa conscious literature.

Therefore, it was our good fortune that we were able to serve Prabhupāda in his publishing endeavors, and we can see from his letters during this period that Prabhupāda left us with no opportunity to become sentimental about what we were doing or whatever intimacy we gained with Prabhupāda by serving him in this way. Rather, it was a demanding service. Prabhupāda regularly tested our sincerity and pushed us toward renunciation; he wanted us to do something substantial.

Prabhupāda especially expected substantial results from the Americans. Americans pride themselves on their ingenuity and leadership. Therefore, Prabhupāda expected more from us. He was disappointed that his students couldn’t rouse themselves to understand that he wanted a competent team to produce and distribute his books. He was all alone in India, without money, but he was able to produce and print three large books and a monthly magazine.

Therefore, to approach Prabhupāda in this service was particularly demanding. This letter emphasizes the fact that he wanted his book publishing to become a viable business. Normally, people who want to make money don’t publish books. Prabhupāda, of course, had a transcendental reason for writing and producing his books, but that didn’t negate the fact that he wanted it to make money for ISKCON.

In this letter, Prabhupāda proposes a new system. First he would submit a manuscript, then the press members would consult among themselves, research costs, and present Prabhupāda with a quotation. After the quotation was submitted to Prabhupāda, he would ask the press members to negotiate better prices if necessary, then accept the quotation. Prabhupāda would then agree to pay his disciples (and ISKCON Press) the required amount to produce the book. He would also pay a ten percent maintenance charge over and above production and printing costs.

Prabhupāda also proposes that the books be distributed by the press to the various ISKCON centers on consignment. When the temples sell the books, they should remit payment to Prabhupāda rather than to ISKCON Press.

Although Prabhupāda proposed this system, we never followed it. Almost all the centers’ finances were badly managed. I’m partly to blame for that as far as the press is concerned—I wasn’t a good manager—but the centers weren’t actively selling books and they were negligent about their accounting. It was quite common to sell books and use the money from book sales for temple maintenance. Then the devotees would have nothing left to send to Prabhupāda.

At one point, Brahmānanda and others thought that the press could better support itself financially if we took in outside work, so we made a green sign with yellow lettering for “ISKCON Press” and hung it from the second floor of the temple building. The temple was situated on a busy street. After awhile, people took notice and work started coming in. There were so many youth groups in those days, and most of them produced pamphlets or flyers to circulate at protests and rallies. As Prabhupāda pointed out in this letter, accepting those jobs was a threat to our tax-exempt status and we had to stop the outside work, but neither our organization nor our financial situation improved until the BBT was begun in Los Angeles in 1972. At that time, Karandhara became the manager, and by then, he was a competent businessman. The formation of the BBT was Prabhupāda’s reaction to our bad management and his insistence that his book publication and distribution be managed as a business. Prabhupāda himself was an expert businessman and he used his business skills to organize a business for Kṛṣṇa.

The BBT’s history has been discussed in detail in the *Śrīla Prabhupāda-līlāmṛta,* including the discovery of distributing books in quantity. In 1969, we had very little idea how to distribute Prabhupāda’s books. Most of the books we printed were kept in storage in New York. Some of the devotees even doubted, as Murāri had doubted, whether it was possible to mass distribute them. We concentrated only on *Back to Godhead* distribution when we went out on *harināma.* We didn’t think people were philosophically inclined enough to buy Prabhupāda’s books on the street. What, then, was the point in printing large quantities of Prabhupāda’s books just to put them into storage?

The devotees at ISKCON Press couldn’t worry about that problem; it was our job to print Prabhupāda’s books. Prabhupāda was willing not to have a practical distribution plan before he printed his books. His book printing was not an ordinary business. And of course he knew that only printed books could be distributed.

Prabhupāda never told us exactly how to distribute his books. He didn’t treat us as unimaginative, uninventive children, although in many ways that’s what we were. He wanted us to show initiative.

I certainly didn’t have any idea how to distribute quantities of his books. I was satisfied just to know that the books were being produced and printed. I thought that printing was Prabhupāda’s first concern. He had said that he wanted his movement to be based on his books. He wanted his disciples to have literature to read. I saw Prabhupāda’s books as the protection he was providing against the new philosophies that were abounding in the ’60s and ’70s—TM, various kinds of yoga and impersonalism, hedonism, and confusion.

The other devotees in the temple didn’t have plans to distribute Prabhupāda’s books either. In those days, distribution meant sending cases of books to other centers. Still, we wanted to do whatever Prabhupāda wanted. Murāri’s idea that the press shouldn’t come to Boston could have been more reasonably expressed if he had said he didn’t want to take part in it himself. Then we could have responded, “Don’t worry. You just do your service in peace and we will worry about the rest.” Devotees who took a position similar to Murāri’s couldn’t stand against the force of Prabhupāda’s order and desire. Who can determine what the whole movement should do or that we shouldn’t distribute books or that there shouldn’t be a press? Who can say that Prabhupāda shouldn’t have asked us to distribute his books? Who can dare to take that position?

Neither can any of us determine what another devotee’s taste in service should be; if we try to assert our opinions in this way, we can actually restrict the expansion of the *saṅkīrtana* movement. If the spiritual master wants his disciples to take a particular direction in their preaching, or if he wants them to accomplish a particular task, then we should serve the order and not override it with our doubts.

When the spiritual master asks us to do something that doesn’t come easily or that requires knowledge or initiative, or to do something we don’t like to do, it’s not always possible to immediately digest that order. For me, the difficulty was not book distribution, but press management. Although I was enthused to print Prabhupāda’s books, I found the management of the press boggling. Because Prabhupāda assigned that task to me, however, I was willing to be responsible and to do the needful as purely as I could. I remember walking in the temple door after work and seeing the devotees lined up with complaints: someone did this, someone did that, there were no *capātīs* for lunch, we need money for light bulbs. I could hardly cope with the pressure of working all day and at the same time, managing the temple, what to speak of managing the press too.

My inability to carry out Prabhupāda’s instruction properly worried me. I couldn’t say to Prabhupāda that I had enough to do and that someone else should manage the press. Instead, I bluffed my way through. But that only created more anxiety; I knew that eventually my inability to grasp all the details would catch up to me and I would make a mistake.

There were very few people with whom I could discuss this problem. I couldn’t really discuss it with Advaita because he already doubted my abilities. He wanted to manage the press himself. Later, Brahmānanda decided to take over. The press was a big operation. It extended beyond any temple boundaries. Brahmānanda had a taste for management. He was also as tough as the workers; they listened to him. But his coming turned out to be short-lived.

Hearing in this letter that Prabhupāda had ten books ready to print was a test of our confidence. I remember turning to Advaita and Uddhava, our printing experts, and asking what they thought. My response to Prabhupāda was based on their opinion that we could handle the work.

None of us wanted to bluff Prabhupāda by agreeing to do something we couldn’t actually do, but it is true that we were still amateurs. We hadn’t tested our own capabilities. Could we do color separations? Our presses were so small compared to the massive presses at Dai Nippon. History shows that we never did come up to industry standards. Eventually, ISKCON Press moved to New York and then to Los Angeles where it became a publishing house and the actual printing and binding were done by outsiders.

Prabhupāda’s comment at the end that Advaita did not need to buy a van showed his awareness of our inability to manage properly. Actually, in this case Advaita did need a van, and when Prabhupāda came to Boston in December, he saw the need and gave his permission for Advaita to purchase one. Although the temple already had a van, there was often a conflict of interest when the press was also dependent on it. Therefore, Advaita found a car dealer who was willing to sell us a van on a small deposit.

When we received this fatherly letter back telling us that a “wagon” didn’t seem necessary, we were willing to accept Prabhupāda’s instruction. Prabhupāda was frugal; he didn’t like to see money spent unnecessarily. In those days, Prabhupāda was still trying to manage his American disciples and their wasteful tendencies. Devotees who went to India with Prabhupāda often tell how Prabhupāda insisted they live according to the Indian standard, although they were “rich men’s sons.” Prabhupāda tried to apply this standard to the Americans too, but eventually he told them to spend the money themselves. He was almost disgusted with our inability to maintain what we had and to be frugal. It’s not that Prabhupāda wanted to impose the Indian mentality on us, but he wanted us to be sensible. And he didn’t like credit. To live free of credit is an austerity in the West.

Prabhupāda was aware that most of our money came from donations. Unfortunately, we didn’t always have the maturity to understand that we had left our rich families and become beggars, and that our begging from the public meant that we had to be accountable. When people give donations, they expect the money to be spent properly. Devotees sometimes spent irresponsibly and even moved on, leaving all the financial stress behind them. Prabhupāda saw this propensity in his Western disciples and tried to curb it.

Prabhupāda mentions that he consulted with Puruṣottama in this letter and that Puruṣottama’s idea is correct. Puruṣottama joined in New York while Prabhupāda was in San Francisco for the first time. Prabhupāda initiated him when he returned in 1967. He did quite well in Prabhupāda’s association, but when Prabhupāda started traveling, he became restless. Then he wrote a letter to Prabhupāda and proposed that he travel with Prabhupāda and serve him. Prabhupāda agreed and Puruṣottama became Prabhupāda’s first servant-secretary.

He continued in that service for awhile. Although he had no real training in secretarial duties, he got along well with the devotees and didn’t appear envious or haughty. Later, he lost faith in Prabhupāda because he couldn’t accept Prabhupāda’s assertion that the moon landing was a hoax.

Puruṣottama was young and inexperienced. It’s interesting, almost humorous, that Prabhupāda consulted him. Prabhupāda would sometimes decide something and then approach a disciple to hear his or her opinion. After, he would tell us that “I asked Govinda dāsī and she agrees.”

In this case, we can imagine the scene—Prabhupāda sitting with his secretary and telling him the details of his plan. Puruṣottama probably said, “It sounds good,” and that was enough for Prabhupāda to quote him as a consultant.

These consultations always provided sweet exchanges between Prabhupāda and his disciples. It also provided us with practical help. If something was unclear, we had someone besides Prabhupāda to whom we could go in order to get more details.

The real sweetness of these “consultations,” however, was that it proved Prabhupāda’s incredible humility and humanness. When he presented his plans as if they weren’t his ideas, or he told us that he didn’t want to impose anything on us but that he had consulted with Puruṣottama, who had agreed, we would appreciate Prabhupāda even more.

Prabhupāda also mentions the “court documents.” After the trial of the gang members who had attacked us, the judge’s statement was typed and a copy sent to us. We forwarded the judgment to Prabhupāda, and these are the court documents he is referring to.

Prabhupāda said he would keep them in our file. He kept a file on each of his temples. Knowing that always gave me solace. I felt secure that we could go forward with our preaching and that Prabhupāda knew both what we were doing and what had happened in the past as the Boston temple developed.

London  
14th November, 1969  
My Dear Satsvarupa,  
Please accept my blessings. I beg to acknowledge receipt of your letter dated November 12, 1969 and thank you very much. Regarding the delay in composing books, I have just now written to Pradyumna and Arundhuti asking them the reason why they are delaying the matter. So on receipt of their clear reply I shall take necessary action. I can appreciate also that the matter is being delayed. If they are having difficulty in composing, can you suggest any other boy or girl to take up this work in Boston? Centralized activity is desirable, but because we have not got all workers in a place, there is difficulty. Now gradually it will be centralized.  
So far as *Krsna* is concerned, I understand that you did not keep any copy of the manuscript. Generally it is the custom to make at least four copies, so how is it that you have neglected this? In the absence of a second copy it is risky to send you our copy in the mail Therefore, I am personally glancing over and putting the diacritic marks in the Sanskrit words. Besides that, one respectable friend has promised to get this printed immediately. In my previous letter I asked you to give quotation for this *Krsna* book. The size should be 7" x 10". I understand that it is the standard size of this kind of book. Please also inform me if we have got arrangement for printing color pictures also. So in consultation with Advaita please give me immediately the actual expenditure for printing 10,000 *Krsna* books on the style of TLC; four hundred pages, including fifty color pictures. It is essential for me to know this because the friend who wants to publish this book should be informed about the cost of production within a week. Also, I have not received any edited versions of the tapes which I have sent to you from Europe. So please send them to me as soon as possible, keeping carbon copies with you in Boston. If there are discrepencies in your editing techniques between the beginning and later chapters, please inform me what they are so we can make the corrections here.  
Regarding Brahmananda, I do not think he may divert his attention to Boston activities. So far I get report from Tamal Krishna, New York is not in proper condition. New York is the most important center, so things should be arranged there very first class. Brahmananda is leading very nice Samkirtan Party in New York, so that should be organized more and more. I shall write to Brahmananda soon. In the meantime, if you meet, you can also say to him. So far as press management is concerned, do you think Brahmananda’s presence is required? Or will Advaita and Uddhava be able to do things? So far as I get report from Tamal, the press arrangement is now in due order through the endeavor of Advaita and Uddhava. So Tamal Krishna will correspond with you in this connection. In the meantime you can send me the production cost of *Krsna* as described above. I hope this will meet you in good health.  
Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

Prabhupāda mentions that he has written a letter to Pradyumna and Arundhatī about their delaying the composing. Prabhupāda’s letter to them expresses his anxiety:

My dear Pradyumna and Arundhati,  
Please accept my blessings. I am very much anxious to hear from you. In my last letter I was informed by you that you were going to send me some specimens of the composed version of *Nectar of Devotion,* but I have not received anything till now. Besides that, you have not called for the remaining portion of the manuscript. This means the major portion is not yet composed. In the meantime, the press is ready. The press has already begun to print, and without having any book ready, they are printing other materials. But the press is specifically meant for printing my books. So far as I can understand, 12 or 13 books are ready for going to the press. Simply they require to be composed. So please let me know what is the exact position why the work is going slowly. Here some respectable friend has promised to publish my book, *Krsna,* in two parts. The manuscript is also ready. So after *Nectar of Devotion,* I want to take up this work immediately. Formerly the plan was that Arundhuti and Shama Dasi combinedly will compose at least 20 pages daily under the guidance of Hayagriva and Pradyumna; Pradyumna will be responsible for the diacritic marks and Hayagriva for correct English and grammatical composition. This was the arrangement. The press is ready, the manuscripts are ready, but I find from your department things are not up to standard. So please let me know what is the position, whether it is possible for you to take this responsibility. If you take the responsibility, what is the difficulty that things are going so slowly? I shall await your reply to this letter and shall thank you very much in anticipation.

I don’t remember what the problem was or how Pradyumna and Arundhatī reacted when they received this letter from Prabhupada, but perhaps we can try to enter Prabhupāda’s mood in this exchange. Prabhupāda in letters was different than Prabhupāda in his purports or lectures. It’s thrilling to study his life through his correspondence and to appreciate these other aspects of his nature—his concerns, his very human anxieties and requests. It makes life so vibrant to have a spiritual master who demands service from us like this, who tries to entangle us in his life and mission. This was especially true of Prabhupāda’s book production. Many devotees were involved, and when we didn’t work efficiently, we made mistakes. He would point them out to us. It was such a wonderful and personal dimension of him, our eternal guru.

Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura says that we should see the spiritual master not just as a sage, but as a dear friend. Not only a friend, but a master and mentor. In Prabhupāda’s case, he was the author of these books that we were producing. Working with him in his book production was like being asked to work with Śrīla Vyāsadeva. It required that we be responsible because the philosopher sage, the pure devotee guru, is a real person who is capable of feeling frustrated that his books are not coming out on time.

We might wonder at that frustration. After all, Prabhupāda was a pure devotee. How could it be our fault that his books weren’t produced in a timely way? Can’t Kṛṣṇa manifest the books despite our irresponsibility? How could Prabhupāda be dependent on us to carry out his mission? But that’s not how service is rendered and exchanged in this world. Therefore, we can relish reading of Prabhupāda’s frustration and feel his heaviness, his humanness, and use it to improve.

We can draw some lessons from this letter about the guru-disciple relationship. It is interesting to note, for example, how devotees often want to be close to their spiritual master and they try to become close by making offers of service. It is true that an offer of service is the basis for an affectionate exchange, but the exchange is not complete until the service is responsibly rendered. There is no room for sentimentality in the guru-disciple relationship. Closer intimacy with the guru means that our ability to serve sincerely and competently will be tested. Of course, our degree of competence in carrying out a particular service is not a complete test of sincerity, but it can reflect it. More importantly, however, the test forces us to face our limits as disciples. That can be frightening. It means we have to go deep into our hearts to find out how much of a Vaiṣṇava we are.

If we make a sentimental offer of help to the spiritual master, our superficiality will be exposed. We shouldn’t be unthinking or offer more than we are capable of simply because we want to create intimacy with the guru. For example, a devotee may be experiencing doubts in his Kṛṣṇa consciousness or trouble in his *āśrama.* By associating with his spiritual master, he may suddenly want to come closer to his guru. That is a positive desire, but is he ready to face the implications of greater intimacy? His work with himself may lie first in another direction. He may have to work on his philosophical grasp of *guru-tattva* or situate himself properly in his *āśrama.* He may first have to defeat his doubts. It’s not that by his attempt to come closer to the spiritual master he can sidestep the work at hand.

Although this letter is full of the heaviness of the guru’s displeasure with his disciples’ service, Prabhupāda didn’t take a stance of accusing Pradyumna and Arundhatī of neglect. Rather, he asked for an explanation. He also gave them room to admit that they had come forward with a genuine offer of service, but that it was more than they could do.

Prabhupāda allowed the devotees to find themselves, and to help them, he assigned us regular duties. He didn’t call us forward for more association with him than we could handle. Rather, he gave us regulated service, regulated *sādhana,* and healthy association, and he allowed us to develop naturally. He allowed us to find our own capacity for surrender, although just by his presence in our lives, he forced us to constantly reexamine our limits.

Examining our limits meant examining our motivations. We come from such impure backgrounds and impure culture, and those impurities color our dealings even after we come to spiritual life. If we approach the guru with material motivation, our service will be tainted with a desire to enjoy. If we are trying to enjoy rather than serve, we are in danger of becoming overfamiliar or critical. Wanting to be intimate with the guru is good for our devotional lives, but if we allow pride and faultfinding to flourish alongside that positive desire, it could become the driving force behind our service rather than *bhakti.*

We did approach Prabhupāda to receive his praise. It was childish, but he reciprocated with us because he could see that we were spiritual children. To want recognition is not wrong for a child. As we matured in spiritual life, however, we learned to settle down to steady service regardless of the attention we received from him.

That maturing and acceptance of steady service is only possible when we are introspective. We have to look at our desires to be with the guru, weed out the sentimentality, face our limits in service and surrender, and then find our place in his mission. Ultimately, it means we have to face the fact that we are fools before the guru. Being a fool isn’t synonymous with being incompetent, but it means we are not fully surrendered and that our sentimentality and lack of surrender, our lack of love for the spiritual master is exposed in his association. It means that the image we would prefer to have of ourselves—that we are intimate and loving disciples of our spiritual master—may be put into a different light in his presence.

Such exposure tests our sincerity. If we are sincere, we will see our faults and work to improve and deepen the guru-disciple relationship. It may be that we are incompetent at our service, or we may simply not be surrendered enough to do it properly. Perhaps we don’t care enough. The spiritual master may even remark on this to others, as Prabhupāda did in this letter by telling me of Pradyumna’s deficiency. It may even hurt us to hear his words. If we are honest, however, we will admit that he is right, but despite his criticism, we will continue to trust in his love and acceptance. I certainly observed Prabhupāda’s ability to always love and accept his disciples. Even though he would sometimes express dissatisfaction with a person for failing to perform a particular service, he was always willing to accept another service from that disciple. If the disciple performed the new service responsibly, he could again become pleasing to Prabhupāda.

The guru-disciple relationship has to be internalized. Even if it seems that we do not have such an intimate relationship with the spiritual master—perhaps we’re not one of his leading disciples—if we take his instructions to heart while facing our inevitable foolishness, we can prove ourselves sincere. Real intimacy has nothing to do with physical proximity. Rather, it is based on an honest and mature following of the guru’s instructions. By giving us the opportunity to perform regulated service, Prabhupāda helped us build the foundation of real intimacy with him.

A disciple becomes mature by both serving the guru and inquiring from him. If we feel we cannot perform a service he has requested of us, we may decide to step back and take up some other service. After all, the spiritual master is the most important person in our lives. We neither want to disappoint him nor hinder his mission in any way by our inability. Although we may decide we are too incompetent or unsurrendered to do a particular service, however, the guru may insist that we continue our duty.

I felt the agony of this decision when I was Prabhupāda’s servant in 1974. Prabhupāda wanted me to continue as his servant, but I wanted to leave. We can’t always understand what to do by using our material intelligence. If we are not willing to internalize our relationship with the guru and approach him with our doubts, how can we expect to bear the weight of greater intimacy? Rather, we will come to think of the guru as a harsh task master. Prabhupāda *was* a task master, but he was also our loving father and eternal shelter. Sometimes those two aspects of Prabhupāda seemed contradictory; we had to learn to work with both of them.

In this letter, Prabhupāda writes, “I shall take necessary action.” He was acknowledging my frustration in being dependent on devotees who weren’t fulfilling their responsibilities. As a manager, however, he also remarks that we should centralize ISKCON Press. Centralizing the press meant sending all the devotees who were willing to work on his books to Boston. Sending tapes and manuscripts all over America slowed production.

Another apparent contradiction was that working for the press meant serving Prabhupāda in separation. If we were producing his books, we couldn’t join his world *saṅkīrtana* party and go with him to India, but it was just as wonderful to be in Boston producing his books.

Another point Prabhupāda raises in this letter is that I somehow hadn’t made copies of the manuscript before I sent it to him. Reading this now, I am surprised to remember it. Prabhupāda made four copies of each manuscript; I usually made at least one. It was not my practice to neglect this.

Prabhupāda reprimands me for my neglect in this letter. It was something I would have had to face by looking deep within myself. We might want to respond to Prabhupāda’s reprimand by saying “Well, such and such happened and that meant that I couldn’t do my service properly,” but when we’re sitting under the spiritual master’s gaze, we realize that he will not be satisfied by our excuses. He wants us to admit our neglect and to improve. If we don’t admit the mistake, we will not be willing to change.

In this case, it was obviously a terrible omission. So much work went into the manuscripts that if one had become lost in the mail, it would have been a disaster. Although the spiritual master’s reprimand is heavy, there is real nectar there too. It’s a special mercy when the spiritual master pays attention to our inadequacies. Instead of giving us the usual pat on the back, he pinpoints our neglect or lack of surrender and insists on a response. “Why did you do this?” Having our faults exposed is good for our spiritual lives. Finally, we’ll have the opportunity to rectify ourselves, to break through our complacency, and to stop seeing ourselves as perfect disciples.

Neglect is a serious fault. If the spiritual master asks us to sweep the floor and we leave some dust behind, we are neglectful. It means we are performing our service in a superficial way.

Sometimes the spiritual master has wrong information about us. In this case, it certainly wasn’t my practice to send a manuscript without making a copy of it. I could have defended myself to Prabhupāda—“I *did* make copies!”—but even better would be to search within myself for the mistake. Even if we are not wrong in a particular circumstance, it doesn’t necessarily mean that we are right. What is the value in defending ourselves against the spiritual master’s criticism or reprimand? Whatever we did, even if we followed the proper procedures, did not produce the result the spiritual master wanted. Unless we are willing to see that, we won’t be able to keep our service.

This letter also reveals Prabhupāda’s anxiety about the *Kṛṣṇa* book. Prabhupāda encloses the specifications in this letter—the trim size, the number of color plates, the page count, the print run—and again asks me to consult with Advaita and then submit a printing quote. A day later he wrote to Advaita and questioned ISKCON Press’s ability to print in color. We had just printed our first issue of BTG. BTG was usually printed in Japan at Dai Nippon, but because Advaita had submitted a quote of 6¢ a copy, 4¢ less than Dai Nippon’s quote, Prabhupāda had accepted Advaita’s offer and switched the printing to ISKCON Press. In his letter to Advaita, however, Prabhupāda points out the discrepancies in Advaita’s quote:

There are improvements to be made because the essays are not adjusted on the right side. Besides that, it is being printed not on glazed paper. The photograph has not come out very distinctly. Also the cost is not very satisfactory. The Dai Nippon company is printing our BTG at 10¢, but the size is bigger, the papers are more, there are color pictures and glazed paper. In that comparison, 6¢ cost is not very favorable. You have also printed 20,000. Anyway, it is printed in our press. If the cost is equal or little more, we do not mind. But from business point of view you should note down the points.

The fact is, ISKCON Press did not have the capacity to print a deluxe book. We had to decline his request, and *Kṛṣṇa* book was printed by Dai Nippon. We were able to print paperbacks, and we were proud of our printed chapters of the Second Canto of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam.* Prabhupāda had already given us *The Nectar of Devotion* to print, and the *Īśopaniṣad* and a booklet called *A Kṛṣṇa Conscious Handbook* were already out.

We didn’t really feel any sadness about our inability to print *Kṛṣṇa* book. Neither did Prabhupāda make us feel bad. Soon after this exchange, Prabhupāda came to Boston. It was such a high moment in our lives to see him embrace Advaita and call the ISKCON Press his heart.

I also like Prabhupāda’s words in this letter, “one respectable friend.” It’s a charming phrase and it reminds me of the way Prabhupāda sometimes spoke in antiquated English. If someone else were to write such a phrase, people would laugh, but no one laughed at Prabhupāda’s use of English. Rather, we were charmed by it and incorporated many of his phrases and idioms into our own language. Whatever he said we said, and although we were Americans, many of us found ourselves talking like Indians. We still do that. Whether this is right or wrong can be argued, but it increased our remembrance of Prabhupāda and it created a sense of identity and belonging among the devotees.

Prabhupāda’s language also shows that he had a sense of social sensibility. He was aware of the different classes in society and to some degree, he honored the standards that distinguished one class from another. His vision was transcendental, however. He told us that we don’t even respect Lord Brahmā if he’s not a devotee, although we can respect an ant that is a devotee. Was his behavior contradictory? No, Prabhupāda was not a social rebel; he was always a gentleman. He didn’t use impoliteness or insult to shock his listeners. If someone who looked respectable came before Prabhupāda, Prabhupāda would treat that person with respect, although he might tell us in his lectures that such materialists were actually dogs.

We did wonder who Prabhupāda’s “respectable friend” was who was willing to print the *Kṛṣṇa* book. Even more than that, though, we felt the weight of Prabhupāda’s urgent request for a quote.

Later we received more details. It turned out that Śrīla Prabhupāda had requested Śyāmasundara to ask George Harrison to pay for the printing of *Kṛṣṇa* book. It was difficult for Śyāmasundara to comply with Prabhupāda’s request because he had carefully cultivated a relationship with George that wasn’t based on asking him for money. Later, when George lost heart, Prabhupāda encouraged him and George reaffirmed his willingness to give the money.

George arranged with his lawyer in New York that the devotees could come and pick up the check. The lawyer called Brahmānanda, and when Brahmānanda arrived, the lawyer first asked him to sign a receipt for the check. Just before signing, Brahmānanda noticed that the lawyer had made the check out for $1,900 instead of $19,000. The lawyer tried to pressure Brahmānanda to sign the receipt and get out, but Brahmānanda refused. He knew the lawyer was trying to cheat him.

The whole *Kṛṣṇa* book project was done under Prabhupāda’s pushing us forward. In the beginning, he wanted one painting a day. And there were other deadlines—he wanted one tape typed every day, the manuscript edited in two, the Sanskrit editing done by the next day, and everything returned to him by the fourth day. Then he would have another tape ready for us.

## 12: December 1969

London  
13th December, 1969  
My Dear Satsvarupa,  
Please accept my blessings. I beg to acknowledge receipt of your letter dated December 7, 1969 along with the article of Achyutananda. Your decision not to publish this article is correct. To the neophyte devotees we should issue instruction that there are four stages of understanding the Absolute Truth. The first stage is re-establishing our relationship with Krishna. This is the first stage. The second stage is after understanding our relationship, to perform devotional service under proper guidance. The third stage is acquisition of the desired Object. The fourth stage is relishing the nectar of perfectional love. So Radha-Krishna Leela belongs to the fourth stage of understanding, and we are publishing BTG for people in general to re-establish their forgotten relationship with Krishna. So we should always remember this and from *Srimad Bhagavatam, Bhagavad Gita,* and *Ishopanishad* they should try to write how our relationship is evoked from this stage of forgetfulness. They should write articles like this: 1) Krishna, the Omnipotent, 2) How God can be realized as All-Pervasive, 3) The Original Source of Everything, 4) Transcendental Process of Hearing, 5) How one gets out of the Clutches of Maya, 6) Prayers by Arjuna, 7) Prayers by Kunti Devi, 8) Prayers of Bhismadev. They should try to understand Krishna first in so many ways which are described in our *Bhagavatam.* They should read them carefully and pick up subject matters as above mentioned. What general people will understand about Radha-Krishna Leela? Immediately they will take it as ordinary boys and girls in spite of a thousand warnings, “This is not this, this is not this.” So you shall issue instruction that they should write articles on the subject matters as above mentioned. They should read our *Bhagavatam.* The purports are there: They should assimilate them in their own words in a literary career.  
I was very much pleased to see one of your articles which you picked up from Daksa yajna. The articles should be very scrutinizingly published. We want to make our BTG an authorized, first class magazine, and the writers and students should be equally responsible. So when we meet we shall talk more about this.  
Yes, you can expect us on the 21st December, Pan American Flight #55, arriving in Boston at 3:40 p.m. Purushottama has already written to Brahmananda about procedures for our tickets, so you may immediately consult with him in this connection. I am glad that you are corresponding with Hayagriva, and when I come there he may also come.  
Please offer my blessings to the others. I hope this will meet all of you in good health.  
Your ever well-wisher,  
A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami

In this letter, Prabhupāda establishes the editorial standard for BTG. BTG follows this standard even today and includes articles only about *sambandha* and *abhidheya,* and not *prayojana.* Prabhupāda wanted to be careful that the public would not misunderstand Kṛṣṇa consciousness.

*Sambandha* means to awaken Kṛṣṇa consciousness. Most people don’t know God. Śaṅkarācārya once analyzed the human life span in this way: when people are young, they waste their time in sports. As they grow older, they begin to waste time in romantic activities. Then they spend their lives working hard to maintain the family they created in their youth. In old age, they may begin to inquire about spiritual life, but then death comes. Because the whole world is interested only in pursuing material consciousness, BTG has to present *sambandha,* knowledge of who God is, who we are, and what our relationship with God is. Only when that knowledge is firmly fixed in the mind and heart is a person capable of becoming detached from material life and attracted to Kṛṣṇa.

*Abhidheya* means that we act upon the realization of *sambandha.* That is, when we realize that we are the servants of God, we will want to serve Him. Therefore, *abhidheya* includes topics such as descriptions of various kinds of service, worship of the guru, discussion of how to offer food to the Deity and perform other forms of Deity worship, preaching, and other such activities.

After hearing about Kṛṣṇa and beginning to serve Him, our love for Him develops. That is called *prayojana,* achieving the goal. Prabhupāda compared *prayojana* to a boy and girl whose marriage has been arranged by their parents. Although the boy and girl have no spontaneous love, by engaging in the activities of married life and by the natural course of the relationship, they learn to love each other. Eventually their love fructifies and a child is produced. In Kṛṣṇa consciousness, the “child” is *prema*. Discussing the third stage, therefore, necessarily means discussing *mādhurya-līlā.* Prabhupāda didn’t want to preach *mādhurya-rasa* publicly; Lord Caitanya did not either. Rather, Prabhupāda taught us to chant Hare Kṛṣṇa and to purify our lives. Lord Caitanya only discussed *prayojana* in private with His intimate devotees.

Prabhupāda made a clear distinction between the spiritual needs of his followers in this regard and the needs of the public. He was particularly cautious about discussing anything that might be mistaken for mundane sex life with those who were not following the processes of chanting and hearing. Although Prabhupāda didn’t discuss *prayojana* with us as his main preaching, we can see in his letters and books that he allowed us to know something of the confidential topics of Kṛṣṇa consciousness. In his introduction to *Kṛṣṇa* book, Prabhupāda quotes the last verse in the *rāsa* dance chapter: “In the *Bhāgavatam* it is stated that if one hears the pastimes of Lord Kṛṣṇa with the gopīs, from authorities with submission, then he will be promoted to the platform of transcendental loving service to the Lord, and the material disease of lust within his heart will be completely vanquished. In other words, it will counteract the material sex life.” Prabhupāda said that *Kṛṣṇa* book was good for everyone, even those inclined to material lust, because the activities of Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa are just like those of boys and girls. By hearing about them, we become purified. It’s almost an invitation to the common man. In other places, he is more restrictive.

For example, in the talks between Rāmānanda Rāya and Lord Caitanya, Rāmānanda Rāya began to sing about Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa’s conjugal pastimes. Throughout their discussion, Lord Caitanya asked Rāmānanda, “Tell Me more, tell Me more,” but when Rāmānanda started to sing this song, the Lord covered his mouth with His hand. Śrīla Prabhupāda writes in his purport that these things should not be heard and that only elevated persons can discuss such topics:

The topics that are about to be discussed between Lord Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu and Rāmānanda Rāya cannot be understood by a materialistic poet, nor by intelligence or material perception. Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura states that the spiritual mellow can be realized only when one is situated on the transcendental platform beyond the material stage of goodness. That platform is called *viśuddha-sattva (sattvaṁ viśuddhaṁ vāsudeva-śabditam*). Realization of the *viśuddha-sattva* is beyond the pale of the material world and is not perceived by bodily senses or mental speculation. Our identification with the gross body and subtle mind is different from spiritual understanding. Since the intelligence and mind are material, the loving affairs of Śrī Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa are beyond their perception. *Sarvopādhi-vinirmuktaṁ tat-paratvena nirmalam:* when we are free from all material designations and our senses are completely purified by the *bhakti* process, we can understand the sense activities of the Absolute Truth *(hṛṣīkena hṛṣīkeśa-sevanaṁ bhaktir ucyate*).  
—Cc. *Madhya* 8.193, purport

Still, Prabhupāda didn’t restrict us. Rather, he guided us how to understand these topics in his *Kṛṣṇa* book and *Caitanya-caritāmṛta* purports. He didn’t approve of these topics being taken out of the regular context of his preaching, however. For example, he didn’t like the *“gopī-bhāva*” club that was formed in Los Angeles. Prabhupāda emphasized that our service was preaching; when we preach, we don’t discuss Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa’s *līlā.* That doesn’t mean, however, that we cannot relish these topics when they appear in his books. Any devotee who has read Prabhupāda’s books knows that Lord Caitanya’s inner mission was to relish the mood of Śrīmatī Rādhārāṇī or the *rāsa* dance pastimes. We don’t avoid the conclusion of our philosophy.

Prabhupāda cautioned us that we should not take Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes as ordinary or mundane. He was aware, however, that “in spite of a thousand warnings,” people would be prone to misunderstand. When Prabhupāda came to Boston in 1968, someone showed him a sampling of books in English from the Gaudiya Math. One of them discussed Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa’s conjugal pastimes. Prabhupāda told us not to read it. He commented on the author’s statement that the subject matter is not to be confused with sex between ordinary man and woman. Although the author warned his audience, however, he went on to discuss it in his book. Prabhupāda said it was like warning a child that the razor blade he is holding is sharp. We can tell a child to be very careful with this razor blade and emphasize the danger of its misuse, but if we give the child the razor blade, we are creating a hazard for the child. A child does not have the maturity or coordination to handle the razor blade properly “in spite of a thousand warnings.”

To me, the most important sentence in this letter is this: “They should read our *Bhāgavatam.* The purports are there: They should assimilate them in their own words in a literary career.” This line is a classic to me because I am a writer.

Prabhupāda’s use of words is sometimes tantalizing both because we pay so much attention to what he says and because he uses words in such a unique way. I was particularly taken by this word “career.” A literary career in ISKCON. That’s what I wanted—to have a literary career.

In the material sense, career means life commitment or vocation. When we follow our star, it means making progress and working for success. It may sound proud for a devotee to say that he is following a particular career, but even a devotee has to perform some service in his life and make something out of it. Here Prabhupāda says that devotees who want to be writers develop unlimited ideas based on their reading of *Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam* and then express it in their own words. I took it that I would first have to read the *Bhāgavatam,* assimilate it, and then repeat not like a parrot, but however I had understood it in my own language. I loved his choice of words, and I liked his invitation to become absorbed in the *Bhāgavatam* and then express my experience. I had faith that my absorption in his books would add richness to my writing.

In this letter, Prabhupāda gives a list of the types of topics on which we should write. I took his list seriously and over the course of a year, wrote a number of articles following these topics. These topics could certainly be developed into substantial, philosophical essays: “Kṛṣṇa, the Omnipotent” and “How God Can Be Realized as All-Pervasive” stood in contrast to “Kṛṣṇa, the Plaything of the *Gopīs*.” We could call this preliminary realization, but it is important to learn to see God everywhere, and beyond that, to make the distinction between pantheism and Kṛṣṇa as the all-pervasive but eternally independent Lord of all. “Kṛṣṇa, The Original Source of Everything.” All these topics were *Vedānta,* preaching the wonderful philosophical foundation of Kṛṣṇa consciousness. We did an article on the importance of hearing about Kṛṣṇa, another on how to become free of the clutches of *māyā.*

I saw Prabhupāda’s list as an expression of his compassion. By “philosophy” he didn’t mean an intellectualization of spiritual life; he meant compassionate preaching to help people become liberated and enlightened by knowledge. “How To Get Out of the Clutches of Māyā.” “Prayers by Arjuna.” “Prayers by Kuntī-devī.” “Prayers by Bhīṣma.” He wanted us to present articles so that the readers could understand Kṛṣṇa.

Prabhupāda not only wanted us to write articles on these topics, but he wanted us to study them ourselves. He saw us busy with so many activities in his institution, but he told us it was just as important to practice *śravaṇam*. Otherwise, we could think that hearing was something that is important when you’re new and you don’t know anything about Kṛṣṇa, but later, it’s no longer required. Therefore, he wanted us to distinguish spiritual hearing from ordinary hearing. Even a dog can hear, but spiritual hearing means listening with submission and intelligence—with rapt attention—to topics about Kṛṣṇa. We also have to hear from the right source. Practicing the rules and regulations is a qualification for spiritual hearing. Following the spiritual master’s orders makes hearing a complete activity. By hearing in this way, we can attain Kṛṣṇa’s association just as Mahārāja Parīkṣit attained Kṛṣṇa’s association when he heard from Śukadeva Gosvāmī.

Often devotees wonder whether hearing really includes reading. Does reading Prabhupāda’s books have the same potency to change our hearts that Prabhupāda’s personal association had? People used to ask that question of Prabhupāda: “Kṛṣṇa spoke the *Bhagavad-gītā* to Arjuna. It was easy for Arjuna to follow Kṛṣṇa because of that, but what about us?” Prabhupāda always responded that we can still be with Kṛṣṇa by reading His book or by being with His representative. If we hear from the spiritual master, that is as good as hearing from Kṛṣṇa. If we hear from the spiritual master’s authorized representative, that can be as good as hearing from the spiritual master. And of course, Prabhupāda’s books are nondifferent from his personal association.

Prabhupāda never told us that we should spend as much time in his physical presence as possible. Rather, he wanted us to serve his mission and to hear from him through the available means—his books and lecture tapes, and through our fellow devotees. Following guru means following his instructions. If something wasn’t clear we would ask him; later we could also ask his more advanced disciples.

In this letter, Prabhupāda mentions that he expects to arrive in Boston on December 21—a week away. This time, I was free to go to the airport and greet Prabhupāda and I wasn’t going to miss this arrival!

The airport was packed with devotees when Prabhupāda arrived. The New York devotees had a banner, “New York ISKCON Welcomes Śrīla Prabhupāda,” and many devotees from other temples were also present. We had also informed reporters of his arrival, so the press was there too.

When he entered the lounge, the devotees led him to a saffron-covered sofa. I can’t remember who thought of different arrangements for his arrival, but whoever it was likely also arranged for the sofa. It was pandemonium. It almost reminded me of being in line for a rock concert or a baseball game where everyone starts pushing and the mob gets wilder. Most of the devotees had no regard for how the public would view us or what they would think of Prabhupāda. Their only interest was to see Prabhupāda. Eventually, a policeman approached Dayānanda and told him to calm the devotees down.

I shared the group excitement, but I didn’t share the hysteria. I wasn’t crying like Brahmānanda or bleeding like the devotee who had hurt his hand. I remember Jāhnavā was trembling in rapture and crying for Prabhupāda. I didn’t share those symptoms, although I was very happy. But I was unnerved by the scene because it was getting out of hand.

The energy of Prabhupāda’s arrival carried on out the doors where a rented limousine waited. There were so many devotees, but I was able to ride with Prabhupāda to the temple. He asked me about the composing work and I was able to tell him that we were now doing about three hundred pages a month. I remember being happy that I had some good news to report.

There were new devotees among us who were seeing Prabhupāda for the first time. Everyone was touched by the emotion expressed by the devotees. It was such a wonderful thing in our lives to be able to see him. Each of us wanted him to recognize us. A young girl disciple could walk up to Prabhupāda and give him a flower and be shown a moment of affection—perhaps he would smile at her or touch her head—but no one transgressed the guru-disciple etiquette. Although we loved him, we worshiped him according to the timeless tradition of guru and disciple. Our worship often took the form of crying. It was all so sincerely felt that new devotees and guests would be moved.

Nowadays when someone arrives at an ISKCON temple, there is a controlled reception with someone orchestrating it. In those days, however, the devotees would swarm around Prabhupāda. When he arrived at the temple, he took a quick tour of the building. Dozens of devotees would squeeze into whatever available space there was just to catch a glimpse of him or to hear what he was saying. The adulation was open and unrestrained. That was especially true when he toured the temple building. The halls and rooms were filled with devotees all trying to come as close to Prabhupāda as they could. No one stopped anyone else from following Prabhupāda from room to room or objected to their behavior in any way. Prabhupāda would move to the next room and the devotees would charge ahead and behind, trying to be there too, or they would try to be in the hallway as he walked by. He would stop on occasion and say, “Oh, so-and-so dāsī, how are you?” In every movement he was surrounded by enthusiastic, unrestrained young people.

He looked into the householders’ rooms on that tour and saw that they were not tidy or clean. He also saw, however, that none of them had furniture. He wasn’t pleased with the uncleanliness, but he said that the rooms were ascetic.

He also saw that husband and wife slept together in the same room. Prabhupāda didn’t say publicly or privately that this was inappropriate. He indicated to the *sannyāsīs* that *brahmacārī* life was better, but if someone married, there should be no question of divorce or having a relationship with anyone else, and of course he or she should follow the regulative principles. It was assumed, however, that the couple would spend time together, would even eat off the same plate, as he had them do at the wedding ceremony. He didn’t discourage the normal marital relationship.

Then he gave a full-length lecture. I was alert during that lecture and I still remember it. He spoke about Mahārāja Parīkṣit and his last seven days. In that context, he described how people in the material world don’t know that there is another life, that they are the soul and not the body. He said they are only interested in building up sense gratification in the form of families, homes, and furniture. Then he said, “Just like I was seeing just now the rooms of our *gṛhastha* householder boys and girls. Things scattered. But if you go to another person’s *gṛhastha,* you will find their apartment nicely decorated, chairs, cushions and sitting place, but they have no vision about the self. And here, although we see that household affairs, their resting places is not so nicely decorated, but their aim is Kṛṣṇa.”

After the lecture, Prabhupāda was handed a plate of *prasādam* while he was still sitting on the *vyāsāsana.* One devotee later said that when he went back to the house with Prabhupāda after the lecture and tour, Prabhupāda opened his hand. It had a *sandeśa* in it. It had been on his plate, so he took it with him. He ate only a few morsels of fruit on the *vyāsāsana,* and of course, each morsel was accompanied by cries of *“Jaya*, Prabhupāda!”

Then Prabhupāda toured the building for a second time. I remember how I got to the second floor before he did and then turned and saw his form—Indian, dark-hued skin—coming up our well-worn stairs. Prabhupāda was walking on the same stairs we used every day. I felt I was drinking in a vision when I saw him walk up those stairs. It was extraordinary. It felt as if I was seeing something I had waited a long time to see, and my sense of good fortune to be Prabhupāda’s disciple was renewed.

Everything Prabhupāda said on that half-hour tour was significant. I remember he went into the kitchen and said, “Oh, this is good that you keep such big stock.” Everything he said we accepted as *śāstra.* Prabhupāda also commented on the Deity worship. We still had only the small Rādhā-Kṛṣṇa Deities, and our Deity worship was done on a small scale, but Prabhupāda inspired us by telling us that when we polish the Deities’ paraphernalia, we were polishing our hearts. He even said that such polishing cleaned the spiritual master’s heart. “When you see the Deities with attention, your heart will become cleansed, and by polishing the Deity’s paraphernalia, the devotees will clean their spiritual master’s heart also.” He told us we could bathe our spiritual master as we bathed the Deities, and although the spiritual master is not dirty, the disciple can become enthused by offering that service.

Prabhupāda told Śaradīya not to fight with her husband. It wasn’t only Śaradīya who fought with her husband, however. All the householders fought. What surprised us was that Prabhupāda knew. We didn’t know *how* he knew. Śaradīya was such a young girl. I remember when Prabhupāda said this to her in the packed temple room, she blushed. Prabhupāda chuckled about it, but he was serious.

Of course, this was the famous visit where Prabhupāda toured the press and told us that the press was his heart. Back at his house, he compared the printing press to the big *mṛdaṅga.* He said that the *saṅkīrtana* party goes out and the drums can be heard for a few blocks, but the printing press was a big drum because it could be heard all over the world. The *saṅkīrtana* devotees, those who went out on the street and faced the nondevotees, were ISKCON’s frontline soldiers. Those who prepared the books were in the background, but they were the support of the *saṅkīrtana.* Prabhupāda never drew a dichotomy about the two types of service; he always presented them both as absolute.

After the second tour, Prabhupāda got into the car and was driven to the house in Needham. Again, the devotees mobbed around the car. Although he remarked to the devotees in the car, “Why are you all here?” he didn’t openly state any displeasure. He saw how much his disciples loved him and he tried to reciprocate accordingly. I was sitting in the car when he made this remark, prepared to accompany him back to the house, but I didn’t get out when he said this. Later, however, I told the devotees that we had not behaved so well and that we should be more controlled, because even though we love Prabhupāda, we should be more prepared to follow the etiquette of respect.

There’s a controversy now about how Prabhupāda is not in the center of the movement. I can at least sympathize with devotees who feel this way when I remember how at one time in ISKCON’s history, the only inspiration we received was from Prabhupāda. No one claimed to be the inspiration behind the devotees’ activities or thought that Prabhupāda was not the only link in the chain of disciplic succession, and no one felt anything but affection and worship for Prabhupāda, because he had given us Kṛṣṇa. Perhaps it is unrealistic of the devotees now not to accept the changes that have come over time, but I sympathize with them when I remember those airport arrivals and the energy and emotion we all felt.

After the lecture, Prabhupāda went to the house in Needham, which was quite far away, but was quiet and private. When we arrived at the house, Puruṣottama and Bhavānanda were already there. They had not come to the temple, but had gone on to the house to prepare it for Prabhupāda and to wait for him there. I still remember how anxious they were when we finally walked in the door. I think they had expected Prabhupāda to go to the temple, take a quick *darśana* of the Deities, talk for a few moments, and then come and rest at the house. Instead, Prabhupāda had given a full lecture and toured the house and press facilities before thinking of his own fatigue.

Prabhupāda was tired. Bhavānanda told us that when Prabhupāda took rest, he was just like a child. He pulled the blanket up and fell asleep instantly.

It was nice to see Puruṣottama again too. He was our New York boy come home. I remember Prabhupāda remarked in the car on the way back from the airport that Puruṣottama made good *purīs.* It wasn’t long after this, however, that Puruṣottama left Prabhupāda because he couldn’t agree with Prabhupāda’s statements about the moon landing.

The devotees were disappointed that Prabhupāda didn’t come to the temple except for that one initial visit. It wasn’t the distance that stopped him—the house was about a forty-five-minute drive from the temple—but the weather. It was cold and icy and everything was slippery outside.

Devotees were respectful of Prabhupāda’s privacy and didn’t go to the house in Needham unless they had a reason. Neither did they seem envious of those who did go out to see Prabhupāda. Rather, they approached the devotees who had seen him and asked to hear the details of Prabhupāda’s activities. I remember coming back and sharing Prabhupāda-kathā with the devotees who weren’t able to go. I also remember comforting the devotees by telling them that Prabhupāda knew what they were doing.

After Prabhupāda wrote this letter, the BTG staff thought we should discuss with him the standards of articles to be used, grammar, punctuation, etc., so Prabhupāda held a separate BTG staff meeting in the house where he was staying.

That was a wonderful meeting. We came to many conclusions and clear understandings, and Prabhupāda encouraged us about the importance of what we were doing. The meeting smoothed our relationship with Hayagrīva and the others because it clarified for each of us what we were supposed to be doing. We all felt very satisfied. It was so nice to be working on a project and to have Prabhupāda focus his attention on the value of it.

Prabhupāda came on December 21 and after a ten-day visit, left for Los Angeles. We felt we were sharing in an adventure with him. His adventure was to spread the Hare Kṛṣṇa movement. The mood was already so different than our days with Prabhupāda at 26 Second Avenue. We were no longer sitting with him in a room day after day, but as sons and daughters, we had gone out to try to expand his operation. Our relationship was, however, still loving, intimate, and friendly. He gave us his association either in meetings when he came to the temple or by teaching us to serve in separation, and of course in his letters. He was everyone’s affectionate well-wisher.
